# The cheeky tone. Same days, same rules, louder voice.
#
# Format, tab-separated:   BOOK CH:FIRST-LAST<TAB>summary
#
# Sister file to summaries.txt, which holds the dry tone. Both are validated
# against the same day splits by ./build-summaries.py, and the page lets a
# reader flip between them. A day present in one file and not the other renders
# whichever exists, labelled, so the two stay comparable.
#
# Where dry is deadpan and observational, this one is blunt and modern: short
# sentences, slang, the joke stated rather than implied. Same facts either way.
# Neither tone invents plot, and neither punches at the reader's expense.
GEN 1:1-31	God finds a dark soggy nothing and starts yelling things into existence. Light. Sky. Dirt. Salad. Then the sun and the moon, and "oh yeah, also the stars," tossed in like an afterthought. Fish, birds, cows, and finally people, built to look like the boss and handed the keys to the entire planet. Everybody's vegan. God looks at the whole thing, calls it very good, and takes the win.
GEN 2:1-25	God clocks out on day seven and makes napping holy. Then the do-over in close-up: guy made of mud, jump-started mouth-to-nose, dropped into a garden with one strictly no-touchy tree. God decides being alone is bad and runs a parade of every animal past the guy to see if any of them work out as a partner. None of them do, obviously. So: nap, one rib, boom, woman. Dude's first recorded words are basically a love poem. Both naked, zero awkwardness.
GEN 3:1-24	The snake slides up with sooo, did God REALLY say. The fruit looks tasty, looks pretty, and allegedly makes you smart. They eat it, notice they're naked, and staple some leaves together. God comes strolling by asking where they are. The guy blames the woman, and in the same breath blames God for making her. The woman blames the snake. The snake says nothing. Everybody gets cursed, God makes them real clothes anyway, and they get walked out past an angel with a flaming sword.
GEN 4:1-26	Two brothers bring gifts, God likes one of them. Cain gets salty. God warns him sin is parked at his door. Cain invites his brother to the field anyway and kills him. Asked where Abel is, he goes with am I my brother's babysitter. He's exiled, whines that the sentence is too harsh, and gets a protection mark. His descendants invent camping, music and metalwork, and one of them writes his two wives a song about killing a guy for scratching him. Adam and Eve have Seth.
GEN 5:1-32	The begats. Ten generations, same three facts every time: had a kid, lived an absurdly long time, died. Two names break the rhythm. Enoch walks with God for three hundred years and then simply is not there anymore, no funeral, the sentence just stops. And Methuselah clocks nine hundred sixty-nine, takes the all-time record, and does literally nothing else with it. Lamech names his son Noah hoping the kid will make life easier. Buddy.
GEN 6:1-22	Divine beings marry human women, giants show up, God caps everyone at a hundred and twenty years. Four verses. No explanation. Not now, not ever. Then: humans are awful, God regrets the entire project, and calls for a factory reset. Noah's the exception. He gets build specs, big wooden boat, tarred inside and out, three decks, one door, one window, bring the family and two of everything that breathes. Noah does not say one word in this whole chapter. He just picks up a hammer.
GEN 7:1-24	All aboard. Seven pairs of the clean animals, two of everything else, one week's notice, and the rain turns up right on schedule. The deep cracks open from below while the sky opens from above, so it's flooding from both directions at once. God shuts the door personally, which is a chilling little detail. Forty days of rain, water over the mountaintops, everything outside that boat is done. The water just sits there for a hundred and fifty days.
GEN 8:1-22	God remembers Noah, which, cool, glad that came back around, and sends a wind. Water drops, boat parks on a mountain. Noah launches bird-based weather reports: a raven that's useless, a dove that returns empty, a dove that returns with a leaf, and a dove that ghosts him entirely. Everybody disembarks. Noah's very first move on dry land is to grill a selection of the animals he just spent a year rescuing. God enjoys the smell and promises never to run the flood again.
GEN 9:1-29	New terms: have lots of babies, animals are scared of you and also they're food, just not the blood, and murder gets you killed because people look like God. Then the rainbow, which is God tying a string around his own finger. Then Noah, the most righteous man alive, plants a vineyard, gets hammered, and passes out naked in his tent. Ham sees it and tells his brothers, who moonwalk in holding a blanket up. Noah wakes up and curses Ham's kid, who wasn't in the room.
GEN 10:1-32	Seventy-odd names filed under Noah's three sons, basically a world map with everyone's family tree stapled to it. You will never hear from most of these people again. Exactly one gets a story: Nimrod, absolute unit, mighty hunter, founder of Babel and Nineveh, ends up as a saying. Everybody else is a country wearing a guy's name.
GEN 11:1-32	Everyone speaks one language, so obviously they build a giant tower to get famous and avoid being scattered. God comes down to squint at it, then scrambles everybody's language so nobody understands anybody. They get scattered. Then more begats, Shem down to Terah, with the lifespans sliding from five hundred years to two hundred. It lands on Abram and his wife Sarai, who can't have kids, which is the whole plot for the next thirteen chapters. Terah packs up for Canaan, makes it halfway, and stops permanently.
GEN 12:1-20	God tells a seventy-five-year-old to ditch his country and his dad's house, and promises him a huge nation despite his wife being unable to have children. Abram just goes. Then a famine drives him to Egypt, where he looks at his gorgeous wife and runs the numbers: "sister" keeps him alive and gets him presents. Sarai gets taken into Pharaoh's house, Abram collects livestock, and God has to plague a completely innocent Pharaoh to fix it. Pharaoh chews him out and Abram says nothing.
GEN 13:1-18	Abram comes back from Egypt loaded. He and his nephew Lot have so many animals they can't share a zip code, and the staff start fighting. Abram, being classy, tells Lot to pick any land he wants. Lot takes the lush green Jordan valley, and the narrator immediately adds "this was before God torched Sodom and Gomorrah." Lot parks next to Sodom. Abram, left with the scraps, gets told to look in every direction, because all of it is his and his kids will outnumber the dirt.
GEN 14:1-24	Four kings jump five kings, the losers run and fall into tar pits, and the winners loot Sodom and grab Lot on the way out. Abram rounds up three hundred eighteen men born in his own house, hits the raiders at night, and takes everything back. Then a priest-king named Melchizedek appears out of nowhere with bread and wine, blesses him, collects a tenth, and is never seen again. Abram refuses Sodom's loot down to the shoelaces.
GEN 15:1-21	God shows up offering himself as a shield and a big reward, and Abram says the quiet part: I have no kids and my butler inherits everything. So God walks him outside and tells him to count the stars. Abram believes, and that one sentence gets quoted for the rest of the Bible. Then he asks for receipts and gets animals cut in half, vultures to shoo off, a horror-movie darkness, four hundred years of slavery predicted, and a smoking firepot walking the aisle by itself.
GEN 16:1-16	Ten years of nothing, so Sarai says use my slave. Abram doesn't argue. Hagar gets pregnant and starts throwing shade, Sarai blames Abram for her own idea, and Abram, at his worst, says she's your problem and leaves the room. Hagar bolts into the desert, where an angel finds her, tells her to go back, and promises her descendants beyond counting plus a son who'll pick fights with everybody. Hagar then names God, the only person in the Bible who does that: the God who sees me.
GEN 17:1-27	Abraham is ninety-nine when God turns up with new names and new terms. Abram becomes Abraham, Sarai becomes Sarah, and the sign of the covenant is, ah, surgical. Every male, every age, staff included. Told he's having a baby at a hundred with a ninety-year-old, Abraham faceplants laughing, then tries to redirect the promise onto Ishmael. God says no, it's Isaac, this time next year. Ishmael gets a consolation blessing. Then Abraham circumcises every man in the household in one afternoon. Rough Tuesday.
GEN 18:1-33	Three guys turn up at Abraham's tent in blazing heat and he throws together a full banquet in ten minutes. Over lunch one of them mentions Sarah's having a baby next year. Sarah's listening at the tent flap and laughs at the logistics, then claims she didn't laugh, and gets a firm yes you did. Then they look toward Sodom, and God decides Abraham should know. Cue the politest haggle in history: fifty righteous people, then forty-five, forty, thirty, twenty, ten, and God says yes every time.
GEN 19:1-17	Two angels reach Sodom and Lot insists they stay with him, not in the square. Every man in town surrounds the house demanding to rape them. Lot steps outside and offers his two virgin daughters instead. The mob turns on him and starts breaking the door down. The angels pull him inside, blind everyone, and tell him to get his family out. His sons-in-law think he's joking. Lot dawdles until the angels drag four people out. Don't look back, they say. Next time on Genesis: somebody looks back.
GEN 19:18-38	Told to run for the mountains, Lot haggles. Can he go to that little town instead? It's little. He gets his way, which is how Zoar gets its name. Then fire and sulfur, the valley flattened, and Lot's wife turns around and becomes a salt statue. Abraham gets up early to watch the smoke. Lot then gets scared of Zoar and moves into a cave, where his two daughters get him drunk two nights running. The sons are Moab and Ben-Ammi.
GEN 20:1-18	Abraham moves to Gerar and runs the identical she's-my-sister con on a brand new king, having learned nothing from Egypt. Abimelech takes Sarah. God turns up in his dream and opens with you're a dead man. Abimelech, who hasn't touched her, argues his case and God agrees, admitting he's the one who blocked it. Then Abimelech delivers the second righteous-pagan lecture of Abraham's career, and Abraham's defense is that she's technically his half-sister. He leaves with sheep, cattle, servants and a thousand pieces of silver.
GEN 21:1-34	Isaac arrives, Abraham is a hundred, and Sarah says God handed her a laugh. Then she catches Ishmael being a smart-aleck at the weaning party and demands he and his mother be thrown out. Abraham's gutted until God tells him to listen to her. Hagar goes into the desert with one skin of water, and when it runs out she stashes the boy under a bush. God hears the kid and shows her a well. Then a hard cut to Abimelech, a treaty, and seven lambs at Beersheba.
GEN 22:1-24	God tells Abraham to take his son, his only son, and burn him as an offering. Abraham gets up early and saddles the donkey. Then the worst conversation on record: Isaac points out they have fire and wood but no lamb, and Abraham says God will sort it. He builds the altar, ties up his kid, and has the knife in the air before an angel shouts twice. There's a ram stuck in a bush. He names the place God Will Provide and goes home.
GEN 23:1-20	Sarah dies at a hundred twenty-seven, and Abraham, who owns none of the land he was promised, has to buy a grave in it. What follows is a negotiation conducted entirely in compliments: the locals insist he take their best tomb, Ephron insists on giving him the field, Abraham insists on paying, Ephron mentions that the place is worth four hundred shekels, and Abraham weighs it out on the spot. The first patch of promised land the family owns is a hole to put a body in.
GEN 24:1-32	Abraham makes his head servant swear an oath with his hand somewhere memorable: go find Isaac a wife, and do not take my son there. The servant loads ten camels and goes. At the well he invents a test, the girl who offers to water the camels too is the one, and a thirsty camel drinks like a bathtub. Rebekah shows up before he finishes praying, waters every camel, and turns out to be the right family. Next time on Genesis: the servant makes a speech.
GEN 24:33-67	Dinner's served and the servant won't eat until he's replayed the last thirty verses back at the family almost word for word. Laban and Bethuel go: God obviously did this, nothing to discuss. More gifts appear. The family asks for ten more days, the servant says no, so somebody finally asks Rebekah and she says I'll go. She rides off, spots a guy wandering a field at dusk, hops off the camel and veils up. That's Isaac. He marries her and finally stops grieving his mother.
EST 1:1-22	Ahasuerus runs 127 provinces and spends six straight months flexing his stuff, then throws the capital a week-long open bar. Vashti runs her own party for the women. Day seven, the king is wasted, and sends seven guys to fetch his wife in the crown so his buddies can gawk. She says hard pass. He melts down, his lawyers panic that every wife in the empire is about to get ideas, and out goes a permanent law: Vashti's fired, every man's in charge at home.
EST 2:1-23	King sobers up, notices he's single, and the staff pitch an empire-wide hot girl search. Every eligible young woman gets scooped up for a twelve-month beauty bootcamp, all for one night. Mordecai, an exile raising his orphaned cousin Esther, tells her to keep the Jewish thing quiet. Esther asks the eunuch in charge what to wear and just wears that. Crown, party, tax holiday. Then he overhears two doormen plotting to whack the king, reports it, they hang, and nobody says thanks.
EST 3:1-15	Haman gets promoted and everyone at the gate has to bow. Mordecai does not. The staff nag him until he admits he's Jewish, then snitch. Haman decides killing one guy is beneath him and goes for the whole ethnic group. He rolls dice for a date, then sells the king a vague story about a people with weird laws plus a ten thousand talent bribe. The signet ring changes hands with zero questions asked, and the two sit down for drinks while Susa stands there like, what.
EST 4:1-17	Mordecai rips his clothes, throws on sackcloth and ashes, and wails through downtown. Esther sends him an outfit. He declines. So the whole crisis gets handled by messenger: here's the decree, go beg the king. Esther points out that walking in uninvited is a death sentence and he hasn't called for her in a month. Mordecai's answer is brutal: you're not getting out of this either, and maybe this is literally why you're here. She calls a three-day fast: if I die, I die.
EST 5:1-14	Day three. Esther walks into the one courtyard where standing gets you killed. The king holds out the scepter and offers half the kingdom. She asks for a dinner party. With Haman. At dinner she asks for another dinner tomorrow. Haman floats home thrilled, spots Mordecai still not standing up for him, and brags about his money and his exclusive dinner invites before admitting none of it counts because of one guy at a gate. His wife says build a seventy-five foot gallows.
EST 6:1-14	King can't sleep, has the royal records read to him, and finds out he never thanked Mordecai for saving his life. Haman turns up early to ask permission to hang the guy, and instead gets asked how to honour a man the king likes. Certain it's about himself, he describes the most over-the-top parade possible, and is told to do all of it for Mordecai. He spends the day leading the horse of the man he built a gallows for, then his own wife tells him he's cooked.
EST 7:1-10	Dinner, part two. Esther finally names the plot and points at Haman. The king storms out to the garden to process. Haman throws himself onto Esther's couch to beg, the king walks back in, sees a man on his wife's furniture, and that's a wrap. A helpful servant pipes up with "you know there's a seventy-five foot gallows in his yard, right?" Haman gets to be the first to use it.
EST 8:1-17	Esther gets Haman's entire estate, Mordecai gets the signet ring, and then everyone discovers Persian law has no undo button. So instead of canceling the genocide, they write a second law letting the targets fight back. Mordecai struts out in royal blue and white, Susa throws a party, and a whole lot of locals suddenly decide this is a great week to convert.
EST 9:1-17	The big day arrives and goes extremely badly for everyone who showed up planning a massacre. Five hundred dead in Susa, Haman's ten sons included, and nobody touches the loot, which the text mentions three separate times so you definitely notice. The king asks Esther whether she needs anything else, which at this point is a genuinely dangerous question, and she asks for one more day. Seventy-five thousand later they stop and throw a feast. Next time on Esther: paperwork.
EST 9:18-32	Mordecai writes the whole thing down and invents a holiday. Purim, named after the dice Haman rolled to pick the date, celebrated with food, presents, and giving to the poor. Confirmed by letter, then confirmed again by a second letter, then entered into the official book, because nothing caps off surviving a genocide like getting the minutes right.
EST 10:1-3	The king celebrates by raising taxes. Mordecai ends up second in command and genuinely popular, and the narrator signs off by telling you to go read the official chronicles for details, which is ancient-world for "see the extended cut."
1JN 1:1-10	No greeting, no name, no address. John just starts piling up clauses about the thing they heard, saw and physically touched. Headline: God is light, zero darkness. Then a run of if-statements that all slam the same door. Say you're in fellowship while walking around in the dark? Lying. Say you have no sin? Kidding yourself. Say you've never sinned? You just called God a liar. The only door left open is admitting it, and God is faithful enough to clean it up.
1JN 2:1-29	Writing this so you won't sin, he says, but if you do, you've got a defense lawyer. Want to know whether you actually know God? Check whether you keep his commandments, old and also new. Hate your brother and you're stumbling around in the dark. There's a little poem addressed to children, then fathers, then young men. Don't get attached to the world, it's on its way out. It's the last hour: antichrists are already here, and the tell is they walked out, because they were never actually in it.
1JN 3:1-24	Look at this love, he says, we get called God's kids. Then he admits nobody has any idea what that ends up looking like. Sin is lawlessness, full stop. Telling God's children from the devil's comes down to two things: do you do what's right, and do you love your brother. Cain is the how-not-to; John asks why he did it, then answers himself. Hate your brother, he files it under murder. Then the test: you've got the goods, you see someone who needs them, and you shut the door.
1JN 4:1-21	Don't take every spirit at face value, test them, and the test is precise: does it admit Jesus came in an actual body. Then the chapter turns into the one on every wedding card. God is love, and the evidence isn't a warm feeling, it's that he sent his Son. Nobody's ever seen God, so the visible proof is people loving each other. Perfect love kicks fear out, because fear is about punishment. He finishes with a jab: claim you love God while hating your brother and you're a liar.
1JN 5:1-21	Believing and loving are bolted together: love the Father, you love his kids, no exceptions. His commands, he says, aren't heavy. Faith is what beats the world. Three witnesses line up and agree: Spirit, water, blood, and God's word outranks any human one. Near the end he finally says why he wrote it: so you'd know you have eternal life. Then a genuinely confusing bit about a sin that leads to death, and then the final line, out of nowhere: stay away from idols.
1PE 1:1-25	Peter writes to Christians scattered across five provinces and calls them foreigners right out of the gate. That's the whole mood of the letter. You've got an inheritance on hold in heaven, and meanwhile a rough time down here, compared to fire testing gold, since gold doesn't last and your faith does. The old prophets dug into this and found out it was for you, not them, and angels are leaning in trying to get a look. Remember you weren't bought with cash. Grass withers, the word doesn't.
1PE 2:1-25	Ditch the spite, the fakery and the trash talk, and want the pure milk like a baby does. Then he switches to building materials: Jesus is the stone the builders threw out that turned out to be the cornerstone, and you're all stones stacked into the same house, and you're the priests too. You're a chosen people, a holy nation, and also resident aliens, so behave well enough that the slander doesn't stick. Servants get told to take it even from cruel masters, because Christ took it.
1PE 3:1-22	Marriage section. Wives, your behavior might win over a husband who won't listen to a word of it. Skip the gold and the elaborate hair, go for the quiet spirit. Husbands get exactly one verse and it comes with a penalty: treat your wife as a co-heir or your prayers get jammed. Everyone else: don't fire insults back. Then the famous bit, always be ready to explain the hope you've got, gently. Then it gets strange: Christ preaching to spirits in prison, Noah's eight survivors, and baptism.
1PE 4:1-19	You've done plenty of the old life already, he says, then lists it: binge drinking, orgies, the lot. Now your old crowd thinks you're weird for not showing up, and they're offended about it. The end of everything is close, so keep your head clear enough to pray, love each other hard, and be hospitable without complaining, the most honest line in the chapter. Don't act shocked when the fire comes. And don't suffer as a murderer, a thief, or a nosy meddler, one of these not like the others.
1PE 5:1-14	Note to elders, from a fellow elder: shepherd because you want to, not for the money, and don't boss people around. Younger folks, defer. Everybody, put humility on like clothes, because God pushes back on the proud. Dump your worries on him, he actually cares. Stay sharp, because the devil is pacing around like a hungry lion, and Peter's comfort is that everyone else is getting chewed on too. Sign-off: Silvanus wrote it down, the church in Babylon says hi, so does Mark, and everybody kiss.
2PE 1:1-21	Peter opens with a stack: faith, then good character, then knowledge, self-control, endurance, godliness, brotherly affection, love. Skip them and you're nearsighted and you've forgotten you ever got cleaned up. He says he'll keep repeating himself on purpose, because he knows he's packing up this tent soon and wants it to stick after he's gone. Then he gets defensive: we didn't invent clever stories, we were there, we heard the voice on the holy mountain. Prophecy isn't somebody's personal hot take.
2PE 2:1-22	One full chapter of Peter roasting false teachers, and he is clearly having a great time. Exhibit A: angels thrown into Tartarus. Exhibit B: the flood, eight survivors. Exhibit C: Sodom and Gomorrah burned to ash, Lot hauled out exhausted. God knows how to rescue people and how to keep the rest locked up. Then the insults: unreasoning animals, partying at noon, following Balaam, whose own donkey had to talk sense into him. Waterless wells. Selling freedom while slaves themselves. Closes with a dog going back to its vomit.
2PE 3:1-18	The skeptics turn up asking where this big return is, since nothing ever changes. Peter says they're conveniently forgetting the flood. It isn't that God is slow, it's that he's patient: a day is like a thousand years to him, and he doesn't want anybody lost. When it lands it lands like a burglar, elements melting, everything burned up, then new heavens and a new earth. And then he drops a note about Paul: our beloved brother, but some of his letters are hard to understand and people twist them.
BEL 1:1-20	New king, Cyrus, and Daniel is his favourite. Cyrus wants to know why Daniel will not worship Bel, who obviously eats: twelve measures of flour, forty sheep, six barrels of wine, every day. Daniel laughs in his face: clay inside, brass outside, never eaten a thing. So the seventy priests bet their lives on it, the king seals the temple with his ring, and Daniel gets the floor dusted with ash first. That night the priests come in through their secret hatch. Next time on Bel: look down.
BEL 1:21-42	Ash floor, footprints. Men's, women's, children's. The priests show the king their trapdoor, he has them killed, and Daniel flattens Bel and his temple. Then the king says fine, but this dragon is definitely alive, so worship that. Daniel asks permission, no weapons, boils up pitch, fat and hair, feeds it to the dragon, and it bursts. Babylon riots and demands Daniel, so the king caves: six days, seven lions, nothing else to eat. An angel hauls Habakkuk over by the hair with stew. The accusers go in instead.
GEN 25:1-34	Abraham gets another wife, six more kids, hands the estate to Isaac and pays the rest off to move east. Then dies at 175. Isaac and Ishmael bury him together, awkwardly. Ishmael's twelve sons get a list and he dies at 137. Rebekah's twins start fighting in the womb, and God says the older will serve the younger. Esau comes out red and fuzzy, Jacob comes out holding his heel. Later Esau is starving and swaps his birthright for a bowl of lentils. Great trade, buddy.
GEN 26:1-35	Famine hits and Isaac runs the family classic in Gerar: she's my sister. Works fine until Abimelech glances out a window and catches him getting affectionate with his sister. He farms a hundredfold harvest, gets loaded, and the Philistines fill his wells with dirt. He redigs them under dad's old names, fights over two more, and finally gets one nobody argues about: Rehoboth. Then Abimelech turns up wanting a peace treaty from the guy he kicked out. Esau marries two Hittite women.
GEN 27:1-26	Blind old Isaac wants one last venison dinner before he blesses Esau. Rebekah eavesdrops and has a plan going within minutes: two goats from the flock, Esau's clothes and goat hide on Jacob. Jacob's only worry is getting busted, and his mother says fine, put the curse on me. He walks in, says I'm Esau, and blames God for the fast hunting. Isaac hears the wrong voice, feels the right arms, trusts the arms. Next time on Genesis: the real Esau walks in holding dinner.
GEN 27:27-46	Jacob leans in for the kiss, smells like a field, and gets the whole package: dew, grain, wine, everybody bows to you. Esau strolls in with actual venison thirty seconds later. Isaac shakes like a leaf and says, well, he's blessed now. Esau screams the tent down, notes his brother has now robbed him twice and is very well named, and gets a consolation prize about living by the sword. Then plans to kill Jacob after dad's funeral. Rebekah ships him off to Uncle Laban.
GEN 28:1-22	Isaac sends Jacob off to marry a cousin. Esau finally figures out that Canaanite wives are the issue and solves it by adding a third wife from Ishmael's branch. Jacob sleeps rough with a rock for a pillow and dreams a staircase to heaven with angels on it and God at the top repeating the family promise. He wakes up rattled, stands the rock up and pours oil on it, and names the place Bethel. Then makes a deal with conditions attached.
GEN 29:1-35	Jacob rolls a huge well stone off by himself to impress Rachel, then kisses her and immediately starts sobbing. Offers Laban seven years of free labour for her. Works them, says they flew by. Wedding happens, morning comes, and surprise: it's Leah. Laban shrugs, says we do oldest first around here, and charges another seven years. The guy who faked being his own brother just got swapped on. Leah, unloved, has Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah.
GEN 30:1-25	Rachel tells Jacob give me kids or I'll die, and he snaps back that he isn't God. So the baby race goes proxy: Bilhah delivers Dan and Naphtali, Zilpah delivers Gad and Asher. Then Reuben finds mandrakes and Leah sells Rachel a night with Jacob for a handful of plants. Jacob gets told the deal is done as he walks in from work. Leah lands Issachar, Zebulun and Dinah. Next time on Genesis: contract negotiations.
GEN 30:26-43	Jacob says he's leaving. Laban, who has worked out that all his luck is borrowed, begs him to name a price. Jacob asks for the speckled, spotted and black animals. Laban says sure, then pulls every one of them out of the flock and parks them three days away. So Jacob peels stripes into sticks, props them in the water troughs where the animals breed, and somehow it works. He ends up with flocks, servants, camels, donkeys, the lot.
GEN 31:1-29	Laban's sons start muttering that Jacob has taken the family fortune, and Laban's face goes cold. Jacob calls a meeting out in the field, lists the ten times his wages got changed, and mentions a dream from the God of Bethel. The wives are fully on board: dad sold us and spent the money. They bolt, and Rachel swipes his household idols. Laban chases them for seven days and gets a dream telling him not to say one word. Next time on Genesis: a very thorough tent search.
GEN 31:30-55	Turns out what Laban actually cares about is his missing idols. Jacob, who has no clue, announces that whoever took them can die. Laban tears through every tent while Rachel sits on the idols in a camel saddle and says she can't get up because she's on her period. Search over. Jacob then unloads twenty years of receipts: fourteen years for the wives, six for the flock, drought, frost, no sleep. They stack rocks, name the pile in two languages, and go home.
GEN 32:1-32	Angels show up, Jacob calls the place God's army, then gets word that Esau is coming with four hundred men. He panics and splits everything into two camps so at least one survives. Prays hard. Then sends a massive bribe ahead in waves, goats, sheep, camels, cows, donkeys, so Esau runs into the apology five times before he sees Jacob's face. That night Jacob wrestles a stranger until sunrise, gets his hip wrecked, and walks off with a limp and a new name.
GEN 33:1-20	All that prep, and Esau just sprints over, grabs him, and cries. Jacob had still lined the family up from most expendable to least, with Rachel and Joseph safely at the back, and bowed seven times on the way in. Esau says keep your gift, Jacob insists, Esau caves. Then Esau offers to travel together and Jacob says no thanks, the kids and the baby animals are slow. He then goes anywhere except Seir: Succoth, then Shechem.
GEN 34:1-31	Shechem rapes Dinah, then decides he wants to marry her and sends his father to arrange it. Jacob says nothing until his sons come in from the fields. The brothers lie: sure, we'll intermarry, but every man in your town has to be circumcised first. Hamor pitches it to the city as a solid investment. Three days later, while every man is still incapacitated, Simeon and Levi kill all of them and carry Dinah out.
GEN 35:1-29	God says go back to Bethel, so Jacob makes everyone hand over their foreign gods and earrings and buries the lot under a tree. Altar goes up, God renames him Israel a second time, apparently the first one didn't stick. Then it turns sad fast: Rebekah's old nurse Deborah dies, then Rachel dies giving birth, naming the baby Benoni before Jacob overrules her with Benjamin. Reuben sleeps with his father's concubine and the text just says Israel heard about it. Isaac dies at 180.
GEN 36:1-43	A whole chapter of Edomite paperwork. Esau's wives, Esau's sons, then the same sons again but labelled chief. Then the Horites of Seir get their own roster, featuring Anah, whose one claim to fame is finding hot springs while walking his dad's donkeys. Then eight kings of Edom in the format this one died, then that one reigned. Then eleven more chiefs. The only actual plot point is up top: Esau moved out because he and Jacob had too many animals for one country.
GEN 37:1-36	Joseph is seventeen, snitches on his brothers, is openly the favourite, and has the coat to prove it. Then he tells them about a dream where their grain bows to him, and follows it with one where the sun, moon and eleven stars bow too. They spot him crossing the field and decide to kill him. Reuben downgrades it to a pit, Judah upgrades it to or we could get paid, and Joseph goes to Egypt for twenty pieces of silver.
GEN 38:1-30	Judah moves out, gets married, has three sons. Two of them die, and instead of giving Tamar the third he sends her home to wait forever. So Tamar veils up, sits by the road, and Judah, not recognising her, hires her and hands over his signet, cord and staff as an IOU. Three months later he hears she's pregnant and immediately orders her burned. She sends his own ID back and asks him to confirm whose it is. Judah, cornered: she's more righteous than me.
GEN 39:1-23	Potiphar buys Joseph, and everything Joseph touches turns to profit, so Potiphar hands over the household and stops paying attention to anything but lunch. The text then mentions Joseph is well built and good looking. The boss's wife propositions him daily, he says no every time. She finally grabs his clothes, he bolts out the door and leaves them behind. Second time in his life a piece of clothing gets used as fake evidence. He lands in prison and is soon running the prison.
GEN 40:1-23	Pharaoh's wine guy and bread guy both annoy him and end up in Joseph's prison. Same night, both have strange dreams and nobody can read them. Joseph says interpretations are God's department, then does it anyway. Three vine branches, three days, you get your old job back. He asks the guy to mention his name to Pharaoh. The baker hears a happy ending and volunteers his own dream. Joseph tells him, also three days, but you're getting hanged. The cup bearer forgets him.
GEN 41:1-30	Two years go by. Pharaoh dreams seven fat cows get eaten by seven scrawny ones, then seven good heads of grain get swallowed by seven shrivelled ones. Every magician in Egypt draws a blank. The cup bearer suddenly remembers he owes somebody a favour. Joseph gets yanked out of the dungeon, shaved, dressed, and opens by saying this isn't my skill, it's God's. Pharaoh retells both dreams with extra detail about the ugly cows. Next time on Genesis: Joseph keeps talking.
GEN 41:31-57	Joseph wraps up the interpretation and then, completely unprompted, pitches a national strategy: appoint a wise guy, put officers everywhere, take twenty percent for seven years. Pharaoh scans the room, decides nobody is wiser than the man who just said that, and gives him the job on the spot. Signet ring, fine linen, gold chain, chariot number two, a new Egyptian name and a wife. He's thirty. Grain stacked like sand until he quits counting. Then the famine hits and Pharaoh's advice to Egypt is: go ask Joseph.
GEN 42:1-21	Famine hits Canaan and Jacob asks his sons why they're all standing around staring at each other. Ten go to Egypt, Benjamin stays home in the bubble wrap. They bow to the governor with zero idea who he is. Joseph clocks them instantly, plays it rough, and accuses them of being spies, which makes them recite the whole family tree: twelve brothers, youngest at home, one gone. He locks them up for three days. Next time on Genesis: check your bags.
GEN 42:22-38	Reuben goes straight to I told you not to hurt the kid. They still have no idea Joseph understands every word, since he's been faking it through an interpreter. He steps out, cries, comes back and ties up Simeon in front of them. Then he secretly refunds their money into their sacks, which is generous and completely terrifying when one of them finds it. Jacob does the math out loud: Joseph gone, Simeon gone, now you want Benjamin.
GEN 43:1-34	Food runs out. Jacob says go buy a bit more, pretending there's no catch. Judah spells it out: no Benjamin, no trip, and offers to carry the blame forever. Israel folds and sends balm, honey, spices, nuts and double money in case the last lot was an accounting error. In Egypt they get invited into Joseph's house and assume it's a sting about the cash. The steward hands Simeon back. Joseph spots Benjamin and goes to cry in another room.
GEN 44:1-34	Joseph plants his silver cup in Benjamin's sack, plus everyone's money again, then sends the steward to chase them down and accuse them of theft. They're so sure they're clean they offer a death penalty. The steward downgrades it: whoever has it becomes a slave. He searches oldest first, dragging it out, and the cup turns up in Benjamin's bag. Judah, the guy whose big idea was selling a brother for silver, delivers the speech of his life and offers to be the slave instead of the boy.
GEN 45:1-28	Joseph clears the room and completely loses it, crying loud enough that Pharaoh's staff hear it down the hall. I'm Joseph, is my dad alive? His brothers are too petrified to answer. He does the reframe for them: you sold me, but God sent me, and there are five more famine years coming. Pharaoh is thrilled and offers wagons. Joseph loads them up and sends them off with one rule: don't fight on the way. Jacob refuses to believe any of it until he sees the wagons.
GEN 46:1-34	Israel stops at Beersheba to sacrifice and gets a vision: don't be scared of Egypt, I'm coming with you, and Joseph will close your eyes at the end. Then a full headcount sorted by mother, thirty-three, sixteen, fourteen, seven, seventy in total. Judah gets sent ahead as the advance scout. Joseph hitches up his chariot, finds his father, and sobs on his neck for a long time. Jacob says fine, I can die now. Then Joseph preps everyone: say you do livestock, because Egyptians find shepherds revolting.
GEN 47:1-17	Joseph brings five brothers in front of Pharaoh, who asks what they do, gets the rehearsed shepherd answer, and gives them the best land plus a job running the royal herds. Then Jacob comes in, blesses Pharaoh, and when asked his age says a hundred and thirty and that his years have been few and rough. Bold thing to tell a king you just met. Joseph hoovers up every coin in Egypt and Canaan. Next time on Genesis: the livestock goes next.
GEN 47:18-31	Year two with no money left. The Egyptians come back and offer the only assets they have: themselves and their land. Joseph takes the deal, buys the entire country for Pharaoh, moves everyone into the cities, and skips only the priests because they're already on the royal payroll. Everyone gets seed and a permanent twenty percent cut to Pharaoh, and they thank him for saving their lives. Jacob does seventeen years in Egypt, hits a hundred and forty-seven, and makes Joseph swear he won't be buried there.
GEN 48:1-22	Jacob's sick, hears Joseph is coming, and props himself up in bed. He adopts Joseph's two sons on the spot, so Ephraim and Manasseh now rank like Reuben and Simeon. Then he asks who these kids are, because he's basically blind. Joseph carefully positions the older boy under the right hand. Jacob crosses his arms and blesses the younger one anyway. Joseph tries to physically move it and gets told I know, son, I know. Younger brother wins again.
GEN 49:1-33	Jacob calls all twelve sons in and delivers what he calls blessings and are mostly performance reviews. Reuben: demoted, and everyone knows why. Simeon and Levi: cursed and getting scattered, that's for the massacre. Judah: lion, scepter, everyone bows. Zebulun gets ships, Issachar gets called a strong donkey who likes lying down, Dan is a snake that bites horses' heels, Benjamin is a ravenous wolf. Joseph gets the long glowing paragraph. Then Jacob gives burial instructions, pulls his feet onto the bed, and dies.
GEN 50:1-26	Joseph has his father embalmed, forty days for that, seventy days of national mourning. The funeral convoy to Canaan has chariots, horsemen and every important person in Egypt, and the Canaanites watching name the spot after the Egyptians. Once dad is buried, the brothers panic and send Joseph a note claiming their father's dying words were an order to forgive them. Joseph cries, says he isn't in the place of God, and delivers the line: you meant it for evil, God meant it for good.
JAS 1:1-27	Verse two and he's already saying count it all joy when everything goes wrong. Need wisdom? Ask, but don't wobble, a doubter is a wave and gets nothing. Rich guy withers like a flower in a heatwave. Don't blame God when you're tempted: your own craving breeds sin, and sin grows into death. Fast to listen, slow to talk, slow to blow up. Hearing without doing is glancing in a mirror and forgetting your own face on the way out. Actual religion: widows, orphans, staying unstained.
JAS 2:1-26	Guy walks in with a gold ring and nice clothes, gets the good seat. Guy walks in filthy, gets told to stand or sit by the footstool. James asks what kind of judges that makes you. God picked the poor to be rich in faith, and the rich are the ones hauling you into court. Break one commandment, you've broken the set. Then the big one: telling a freezing, starving person stay warm and eat well while handing them nothing is faith with no works. Demons believe, and they shudder.
JAS 3:1-18	Heads up, would-be teachers: stricter grading. Then three images of something tiny steering something huge. A bit turns a horse. A little rudder turns a whole ship in a storm. One spark takes out a forest. That spark is your mouth. We've tamed every animal on the planet and nobody has ever tamed the tongue. Same mouth praises God and trashes people made in his image, like a spring pumping out fresh and salt water together. Real wisdom is gentle, peaceful, merciful. The jealous, ambitious kind he flatly calls demonic.
JAS 4:1-17	Why all the fighting? Because you want stuff. You don't have it because you never asked, and when you ask, it's to blow on yourself. Being cozy with the world makes you God's enemy, no middle option offered. Resist the devil, get close to God, and swap your laughing for mourning. Quit judging each other, there's one lawgiver and you're not him. And knock off the next-year-we're-moving-and-making-bank talk. You're fog. Knowing the right thing and skipping it counts as sin.
JAS 5:1-20	Rich people, weep and howl. Your gold's corroding, the corrosion is going to eat you, and the wages you stiffed your farm workers out of are shouting into God's ears. Then he pivots: be patient like a farmer waiting on rain, because the judge is standing at the door right now. Job held on, he says. Stop swearing oaths, just let yes be yes. Suffering? Pray. Happy? Sing. Sick? Get the elders, oil, prayer. Elijah was a regular guy, and it didn't rain for three and a half years.
JDT 1:1-16	Meet Nebuchadnezzar, king of Assyria, ruling from Nineveh, which is wrong in about three ways, and the narrator does not care. Four verses of wall measurements at Ecbatana, in cubits, thanks for that. He calls up the whole west to help him fight Arphaxad and the whole west says no and sends his messengers home embarrassed, because frankly he looked like just one guy. He wins anyway, sticks Arphaxad full of darts, and throws himself a hundred-and-twenty-day victory party. The grudge does not go anywhere.
JDT 2:1-28	Eighteenth year, first month, day twenty-two. Nebuchadnezzar holds a cabinet meeting and votes to destroy everyone who ignored his invitation. Holofernes gets 120,000 infantry, 12,000 cavalry, and orders to tell the west to have earth and water ready. Then a supply list: camels, donkeys, mules, sheep, oxen, goats, plus a heap of the king's gold. Out they go, and the text calls them locusts. Cue a burning tour of the map: Put, Lud, Midian's tents, Damascus torched right at wheat harvest. The whole coastline panics, and fairly.
JDT 3:1-10	The coast folds instantly. They send a message that is basically an inventory of everything they own followed by please do whatever you like with it: houses, fields, herds, cities, us. Holofernes says fine, garrisons the good towns, drafts their fittest men, and gets welcomed with flower garlands, dancing and tambourines. Then he chops down all their sacred groves, because his actual job is deleting every god on earth so that people worship Nebuchadnezzar instead. Then a whole month parked near Esdraelon sorting his baggage train.
JDT 4:1-15	Judea hears what he did to everyone else's temples and panics, reasonably, since they only just got back from exile and the sanctuary is barely rededicated. So: occupy the hilltops, fortify the villages, stockpile the harvest. Joakim the high priest writes to Bethulia saying hold the mountain passes, they are two men wide. Then the entire country puts on sackcloth. Men, women, kids, servants, foreigners, and yes, the livestock. Sackcloth wrapped round the altar. Ashes on every head. God hears it.
JDT 5:1-24	Holofernes asks his allies who exactly these hill people are and why they have not rolled over. Achior the Ammonite gives him the full briefing, which is basically the Old Testament in twenty verses: Chaldeans, Egypt, plagues, sea dried up, conquest, exile, back again. Bottom line, sir: unbeatable when they obey their God, easy when they do not. So check their record, and if it is clean, go around. The whole tent immediately starts muttering that he should be executed for saying so.
JDT 6:1-21	Holofernes replies with one line that is the thesis of the entire book: who is God but Nebuchadnezzar? Then some material about mountains drunk on blood. He does not execute Achior, he does something better, and dumps him at the foot of Bethulia to die alongside the people he stuck up for. Slingers drive the escort off, the locals untie him, and he tells the whole town word for word what was said in that tent. They pray, they thank him, and they feed him.
JDT 7:1-17	The army rolls up to Bethulia, now 170,000 on foot plus 12,000 cavalry, and fills the valley. The defenders take one look and say these guys are going to lick the whole earth clean. Holofernes rides out to scout the slopes and, more importantly, the water. Then his allies pitch a better plan: do not fight at all, take the spring, sit there, let thirst do the work, zero casualties. He loves it. Five thousand men go and grab the springs. Next time on Judith: nobody has anything to drink.
JDT 7:18-32	The siege turns into pure arithmetic. Thirty-four days. Cisterns dry. Water rationed by the cup. Kids losing heart, women and young men collapsing in the streets and the gateways. The entire town mobs Ozias and the elders with an argument that is genuinely hard to beat: you should have surrendered, slaves at least stay alive, and we are watching our children die. Ozias asks for five more days, says God will not ditch us completely, and promises to open the gates if nothing changes.
JDT 8:1-17	Enter Judith, sixteen ancestors deep. Widow, three years and four months. Her husband died of heatstroke on the harvest. She lives in a tent on her own roof, fasts constantly except sabbaths and feast days, is extremely wealthy and beautiful, and has not one bad word said about her. She hears about the five-day deal and tells the elders they have put God on a clock, not faith. Next time on Judith: she is not done talking.
JDT 8:18-36	She keeps going. Nobody here worships idols, unlike the ancestors who got cut down for exactly that. If Bethulia falls then so does the temple, and that is on you. This is not punishment, it is the kind of test God runs on the people he actually keeps. Ozias caves, agrees with every word, then explains the thirsty people made him swear the oath. So Judith drops it: be at the gate tonight, I am going out, do not ask why.
JDT 9:1-14	Judith prays flat on her face in ashes, timed to the exact hour the evening incense goes up in Jerusalem. She opens with her ancestor Simeon and the sword he was given for avenging a violated sister. Then: these people think horses and spears are power, and they are planning to trash your temple. Now the asks. Break them. Take the prince down. Do it by a woman's hand. Make my lying tongue the weapon.
JDT 10:1-23	The makeover gets a full inventory: sackcloth off, wash, perfume, hair braided, tiara on, the good dress from her married life, to wreck the eyes of every man who sees her. Her maid packs food she is allowed to eat. The elders can barely speak. The patrol grabs her, swallows the whole I am defecting story, and gives her a hundred-man escort while the camp comes out to gawk.
JDT 11:1-23	Holofernes opens with the classic I would never have attacked if they had not disrespected me. Then Judith goes to work. Flatter the king. Flatter the general. Confirm Achior was right, these people are only beatable when they sin. They are starving, about to eat the priests' food, and the second they do, God drops them. I will pray in the valley every night and tell you exactly when. He is sold.
JDT 12:1-20	He offers her his own food. She says no thanks and eats what she brought, setting up the routine where she walks out of camp every night to bathe and pray. Day four, he throws a private party and sends Bagoas the eunuch to fetch her, on the logic that not sleeping with her makes him look ridiculous. She says yes instantly and calls it the joy of her life. He is thrilled.
JDT 13:1-20	Room clears. Holofernes is out cold. Judith lifts his own sword off the bedrail, asks God for strength, and takes two swings. Head goes in the food bag. Then she and her maid stroll out through the camp on the prayer walk everyone has got used to. At the gate she wakes the town and states plainly: my face beat him, and he never laid a hand on me. Ozias blesses her above every woman alive.
JDT 14:1-19	Judith hands out orders like a general: nail the head to the wall, everyone armed at dawn, march down like you mean to fight, then do not fight. They will run to wake Holofernes, find nothing, and lose their minds. First though, bring in Achior. He sees a severed head held up and faints, then comes round and converts on the spot. Bagoas opens the door and finds a body missing its head.
JDT 15:1-13	The panic lands perfectly. The camp scatters, Bethulia's fighters pour out after them, and Jerusalem, the hill country, Gilead and Galilee chase them past Damascus. Looting the camp takes thirty days and everyone gets rich. Joakim travels up from Jerusalem purely to tell Judith she is the glory of Israel. They hand her Holofernes' whole tent, silver, furniture, the lot. She loads it on a mule and leads the women's dance.
JDT 16:1-25	Judith sings the victory song and she is not subtle about it. No young man took their champion down, no son of the Titans, no giant. A sandal did. Her beauty took him prisoner and the sword handled the rest. Then Jerusalem, offerings, three months of partying, and she donates the bed canopy to God. She turns down every man who asks for the rest of her life, sets her maid free, and dies at 105.
LEV 1:1-17	Chapter one and we're straight into how to burn livestock properly. Bull, sheep or goat, male, no blemishes, hand on the head, then a full butchery checklist: skin it, cut it up, rinse the guts and legs, stack the wood right. Can't afford a bull? Birds. The priest wrings the head off and chucks the crop and its contents on the ash pile to the east. Every single version ends the same way: God likes the smell.
LEV 2:1-16	Grain offering time. Fine flour, oil, frankincense, and you can do it baked, griddled or pan-fried. The priest burns one handful as the token bit and keeps the entire rest, which is a nice gig. No yeast, no honey, ever. Salt is mandatory on everything, and it is literally called the salt of the covenant. First fruits go in as roasted grain still on the ear.
LEV 3:1-17	Peace offerings, and finally the animal can be male or female. Everything else here is about fat. Fat over the guts, both kidneys, the flap on the liver, and if it's a sheep, the whole tail cut off close to the spine. The same organ list gets read out three times: cattle, sheep, goat. Ends with the standing rule, all the fat belongs to God and you never eat fat or blood.
LEV 4:1-19	Sin offerings, specifically for sins you didn't know you were committing. Priest messes up? That's a bull. Finger in the blood, flick it seven times at the curtain, smear the incense altar's horns, dump the rest at the base, then drag the whole animal out of camp to the ash pile and burn it. Entire congregation messes up without noticing? Also a bull, with the elders putting their hands on its head. Next time on Leviticus: yeah, still that bull.
LEV 4:20-35	Rank determines livestock. A chief who slips up owes a male goat. A regular person owes a female goat, or a female lamb if that's what they've got. Same routine every time: finger of blood on the altar horns, pour the rest at the base, burn the fat. And every case ends with the identical line, he'll be forgiven. Four times. That's the actual point of the chapter.
LEV 5:1-19	Ways to end up guilty by accident: you heard the call for witnesses and said nothing, you touched something dead and forgot, you blurted out an oath you can't keep. First move is to say it out loud. Then pay what you can. A lamb, or two pigeons, or if you can't swing birds, a scoop of flour with no oil and no incense on it, because it isn't a treat. Mishandle holy things and it's a ram plus the value back plus twenty percent.
LEV 6:1-30	Ripped someone off? Give it back plus twenty percent, on the day you're found guilty, then bring a ram. After that it's chores. Keep the altar fire lit, which the text says three separate times in case you weren't listening. The priest wears one outfit at the altar and changes into another to haul ashes out of camp. Priests eat everyone else's leftover grain offering, but their own gets burned to nothing. Clay pot that cooked a sin offering, smashed. Bronze one, scrubbed.
LEV 7:1-19	Guilt offering rules, plus the priests' benefits package: handle someone's burnt offering, keep the skin. Bake the grain offering, keep the bread. Then food safety. Thanksgiving sacrifice, eat it today, nothing left by morning. Vow or freewill offering gets two days max, day three it burns. Eat it on day three anyway and the entire sacrifice retroactively doesn't count. Meat that touched anything unclean goes in the fire. Next time on Leviticus: who's actually allowed to eat this.
LEV 7:20-38	Eat the sacrifice while unclean and you're cut off from your people, stated twice on the same page. No fat, no blood, ever. Fat from an animal that just dropped dead can be used for other stuff, just don't eat it. Then the priest payment scheme, which is decided by hand motion: the breast gets waved, the right thigh gets lifted, and both belong to Aaron's family permanently. Signed off as delivered at Sinai.
LEV 8:1-21	Seven chapters of instructions and now somebody actually does it. Moses gets the entire congregation to watch him dress Aaron: coat, sash, robe, ephod, breastplate with the Urim and Thummim tucked inside, turban with a gold plate on the front. Oil on the tabernacle, seven flicks on the altar, oil poured straight over Aaron's head. Bull for sin, ram burned whole, and nearly every step signs off with 'just as God told Moses.' Next time on Leviticus: there's another ram.
LEV 8:22-36	Second ram, and now the weirdest move in the book: blood on the tip of the right ear, the right thumb, the right big toe. Aaron first, then each son. They get bread and fat and a thigh loaded into their hands and waved around. Moses keeps the breast for himself, which the text mentions and then just moves on from. Then the kicker: stay at the tent door seven straight days and nights, don't go out, or you die.
LEV 9:1-24	Day eight. Aaron runs the altar himself, and he starts with a sin offering for his own sins, because you settle your own account first. Calf, ram, goat, bull, grain, all by the book. He raises his hands and blesses everybody, he and Moses go into the tent and come back out, and the glory shows up. Then fire comes straight out from God and eats the offering off the altar. Everyone yells and hits the ground. Keep that fire in mind.
LEV 10:1-20	Nadab and Abihu bring fire nobody asked for, and the fire that accepted yesterday's sacrifice comes out and kills them. Moses gives Aaron a reason. Aaron doesn't answer. Cousins carry the bodies out of camp, and Aaron and his surviving sons are told not to mourn. New rules: no drinking before the tent, and priests teach holy from common. Later Moses finds a goat burned instead of eaten and loses his temper. Aaron asks whether eating it would have been right, today of all days.
LEV 11:1-28	Two-part test for land animals: chews the cud AND has a split hoof. Both, or nothing. The fun part is the near misses. Camel, cony and hare chew but have the wrong feet. Pig has the right feet and doesn't chew, so out. Water animals need fins and scales. Then a long banned bird list that finishes by counting the bat as a bird. Bugs are out unless they hop, so locusts, katydids, crickets and grasshoppers are dinner. Touch a carcass, unclean till sundown. Next time on Leviticus: lizards.
LEV 11:29-47	Eight small unclean creatures, mostly lizards, then a genuinely detailed guide to what happens depending on where the dead one lands. Wooden bowl, soak it. Clay pot, smash it, and everything inside it. Oven, break the oven. Spring or cistern, the water's fine, don't worry. Dry seed, fine. Seed you already watered, ruined. And then it stops being a checklist and gives an actual reason: be holy, because I am holy.
LEV 12:1-8	Short chapter on purification after giving birth. Boy: seven days unclean, then thirty-three more days of purification, circumcision on day eight. Girl: everything doubles, fourteen and sixty-six, and the text does not say why. Then she brings a lamb and a bird to the tent door. Can't afford a lamb? Two birds is fine. Same move this book keeps making: the rule stays put, the price bends to what you've got.
LEV 13:1-30	The priest is now a dermatologist with a checklist. Has the hair in the patch gone white? Does it look sunk below the skin? Has it spread since last week? If it's ambiguous, quarantine seven days and look again, then another seven. Best rule in the chapter: if it covers you completely head to toe and it's all white, you're clean. One patch of raw flesh and you're not. Burns, boils and beard itch each get their own flowchart. Next time on Leviticus: it gets into the laundry.
LEV 13:31-59	More diagnostics, this time with haircuts: shave everything except the patch. Two rulings are basically reassurance. A dull white rash, harmless. Going bald, clean, and it says so twice in case you were worried. Then it gets bleak: anyone actually infected wears torn clothes, hair loose, mouth covered, shouts 'Unclean, unclean,' and lives alone outside the camp. Then the whole procedure gets applied to a shirt. Quarantine the fabric seven days, wash it, cut the spot out, burn the thing if the colour comes back.
LEV 14:1-25	The priest goes outside camp to meet the healed person, and the ritual is honestly kind of gorgeous: two birds, one killed over fresh water in a clay pot, the other dipped in its blood with cedar, scarlet thread and hyssop, then released into open country. He shaves, washes, waits a week, then shaves everything again, eyebrows included. Day eight: blood on the right ear, thumb and toe, then oil on the same three spots. Next time on Leviticus: that oil bit again, slower.
LEV 14:26-57	The cheap version of the cleansing gets written out in full again, ear, thumb, toe, oil, the lot, because being broke doesn't get you a shorter ceremony. Then: mould in your house, which God says he's the one who put there. The priest makes them empty the place before he goes in, so the furniture doesn't get condemned with the walls. Shut it seven days, pull the bad stones, scrape, replaster. Comes back? Demolish the house. Clears up? Two-bird ritual, one bird goes free over the city.
LEV 15:1-33	A whole chapter on bodily discharges, worked through with the same patience as everything else. The bed, the chair, the saddle, anyone who touches any of it, anyone he spits on. Clay pots get smashed, wooden ones get rinsed. Men and women get matching rules: seven days, then two birds. Ordinary sex just means you both bathe and you're clean at sundown. The reason turns up at the very end and it isn't squeamishness: keep this away from the tent so nobody dies by walking uncleanness into it.
LEV 16:1-17	This chapter is dated: after Aaron's two sons died. The high priest can't just walk into the Most Holy Place whenever he feels like it. He washes, wears plain linen instead of the gold kit, and brings a bull for his own sin before he handles anyone else's. Two goats get set out and lots drawn, one for God, one to be the scapegoat. He takes in coals and incense so the smoke covers the mercy seat and he doesn't die. Next time on Leviticus: the second goat.
LEV 16:18-34	Everything lands on the live goat. Aaron puts both hands on its head, says every sin Israel has out loud over it, and a man picked in advance walks it out into the wilderness and lets it go in empty country. Then everybody washes, including the guy who led the goat and the guy who burned the carcasses outside camp. The date is locked in permanently: seventh month, tenth day, zero work, applies to citizens and foreigners the same, once a year.
LEV 17:1-16	Every animal you kill has to be brought to the tent door. Kill one out in a field and it counts as shedding blood, which is a wild way to describe getting dinner. The reason given: people have been sacrificing to goat idols and this stops it. Then blood, banned four separate times, with an explanation attached, the life is in the blood and blood is what makes atonement. Hunt something, drain it and cover the blood with dirt.
LEV 18:1-30	Opens by naming the two places Israel is not to copy, the Egypt they left and the Canaan they're walking into. Then a long, methodical list of relatives who are off limits, sorted by degree of kinship: mother, stepmother, sister, granddaughter, aunts by blood and by marriage, daughter-in-law, sister-in-law. Sacrificing your kids to Molech is on the same list, so is sleeping with your neighbour's wife. The closing image is deliberately gross: the land got sick of the last lot and threw them up, and it can do that again.
LEV 19:1-37	The most quotable chapter here, and also the most random. Leave the edges of your field for the poor. Pay your workers before dark, not tomorrow. Don't curse deaf people or trip blind people, since neither can catch you doing it. Judge fairly, don't tilt for the poor or the powerful. Love your neighbour as yourself, and sixteen verses later, love the foreigner as yourself, because you were foreigners once. Filed in with all that: no mixed seed, no mixed fabric, no tattoos, and honest scales.
LEV 20:1-27	Chapter eighteen listed the offences, this one lists what happens. And it escalates. Child sacrifice to Molech: stoning, and if the town pretends not to notice, God says he'll deal with the town. Cursing your parents, adultery, several kinds of incest: death. A few cases get downgraded to being cut off, or dying childless, which in a book this obsessed with descendants is a real sentence. Ends where eighteen ended, land about to throw up, plus the line about being set apart to be holy.
LEV 21:1-24	Priest rules, stricter the higher up you go. A regular priest can bury a parent, child or sibling, nobody further out. The high priest can't go near a dead body at all, not even his own mother or father. Then a list of defects that bar Aaron's descendants from the altar: blind, lame, flat nose, bad hand or foot, hunchback, dwarf, eye problems, scabs. But notice what the chapter says carefully. He's barred from serving, not from eating. He still gets the holy bread.
LEV 22:1-33	Two halves, both about not being careless with holy things. Who eats the priests' food: not a houseguest, not a hired hand, not a daughter who married out. Yes to a slave the priest bought, yes to a daughter back home widowed with no kids. Then the animals, flawless, with a disqualifying list down to warts and running sores. Two rules near the end land differently: a newborn stays with its mother a full week, and you can't kill an animal and its young the same day.
LEV 23:1-21	The festival calendar starts with the Sabbath, which means feast number one happens every single week. Passover on the fourteenth of month one, unleavened bread from the fifteenth for a week. Then the first sheaf of harvest gets waved, and nobody eats the new grain until that's done. Count fifty days from there, seven Sabbaths, and bring two loaves, baked with yeast, notable in a book that has spent chapters keeping yeast off the altar. Next time on Leviticus: the autumn set.
LEV 23:22-44	The rule about leaving field corners for the poor gets repeated right in the middle of the calendar, as if the holidays don't count without it. Then the autumn run: trumpets on day one of month seven, Day of Atonement on the tenth with no work and no comfort, and the feast of tents on the fifteenth. That comes with instructions to grab palm branches and willows from the creek, celebrate for a week, and sleep in shelters, so your kids find out what their ancestors lived in.
LEV 24:1-23	Starts with chores: oil for a lamp that never goes out, twelve loaves on the gold table, swapped every Sabbath. Then a fight breaks out in camp and a man blasphemes the Name. They hold him in custody until they can ask God, which stands out in a book full of pre-set penalties. His mother gets named, Shelomith daughter of Dibri, tribe of Dan; his father was Egyptian. Everyone who heard it lays hands on his head, and he's stoned. The ruling widens into eye for eye.
LEV 25:1-29	Even the land gets time off: farm six years, leave the seventh alone, and whatever grows on its own is free for servants, foreigners, livestock and wild animals. Count seven cycles and year fifty is jubilee, announced by trumpet on the Day of Atonement, when everybody goes back to their own land. Land prices are calculated in harvests remaining, not acres. Someone asks the obvious question, what do we eat in year seven, and is told year six will produce triple. Next time on Leviticus: houses, and people.
LEV 25:30-55	House inside a walled city and you don't buy it back within twelve months? It's theirs permanently. Village house counts as farmland and comes back at jubilee. Levite property can be redeemed any time. Then the poor: prop your brother up, no interest on the loan, no markup on food. An Israelite who sells himself is a hired worker, not a slave, and walks free at jubilee with his kids. Foreign slaves are inheritable property, which the text just states outright. Redeeming an Israelite priced by years remaining.
LEV 26:1-25	Covenant terms in two columns. Obey: rain on schedule, harvests overlapping so you're still threshing when the grapes come in, five of you chasing a hundred, God walking around in your camp. Refuse and it ratchets, round after round, each one opening with 'if you still won't listen' and each adding seven times more: fever, enemies eating your crop, running from nobody, a sky like iron and dirt like bronze, wild animals, then sword and plague. Next time on Leviticus: it gets worse before it turns around.
LEV 26:26-46	The curses bottom out here, and the images are exact rather than showy: ten women sharing one oven, bread handed back by weight, and you eat and you're still hungry. Cities in ruins, people scattered across nations, so jumpy a leaf blowing past sends them running. Cannibalism gets named straight out. One odd thread underneath it all: the emptied land finally gets every Sabbath year it was never allowed. Then it turns. If they own up, God remembers Jacob, Isaac and Abraham, and won't write them off completely.
LEV 27:1-17	The book closes with an appendix on vows, mostly the price list. Promise a person to God and there's a fixed rate by age and sex: fifty shekels for a man in his prime, three for a baby girl, sliding scale between. Can't afford it? The priest sets a number you can afford. Animals can't be swapped, and if you try to trade up, both are forfeit. Houses get appraised, fields valued by how much barley seed they take. Next time on Leviticus: the last of the paperwork.
LEV 27:18-34	More valuations, all prorated to the jubilee, all redeemable at price plus twenty percent. A firstborn animal can't be dedicated because it's already God's, which quietly closes an obvious loophole. Then the herd tithe: count whatever walks under the shepherd's stick, every tenth one is God's, and you're specifically banned from checking whether it's a good one or quietly swapping it. The book that opened with a bull at the tent door ends with a guy counting sheep and being told not to cheat.
MAN 1:1-15	Fifteen verses of a man apologising, and he stalls beautifully before getting to the point. You made heaven and earth, fenced the sea with a word, sealed up the deep, everything alive trembles at you. Then he arrives. Repentance was not set up for Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, they did not need it. It was set up for me. My sins outnumber the sand. I am bent double under iron chains. I built idols. So he bends the knee of his heart and begs not to be written off.
MRK 1:1-45	No baby, no shepherds, no wise men. Mark just floors it. John shows up in camel hair eating bugs and honey, saying someone way bigger is coming. Sky rips open, voice confirms it. Forty days in the desert gets one line. Four guys drop their nets on the spot. Demons know exactly who he is and get told to shut up. He heals a leper, says keep it quiet, guy immediately does the opposite, and now Jesus can't walk into a town without getting mobbed.
MRK 2:1-28	Place is packed, so four guys just take the roof off and lower their buddy through. Jesus calls that faith, not property damage. He forgives the man's sins, the scribes silently lose it, so he heals him too, basically to win the argument. Then he hires a tax collector and eats dinner with people the religious crowd won't sit near. Asked why his crew doesn't fast: you don't fast at a wedding. New wine, new skins. Also, Sabbath was made for people, not people for the Sabbath.
MRK 3:1-35	Guy with a withered hand walks in and everyone watches for a Sabbath violation. He heals him, visibly angry at the silence first. The Pharisees immediately go make friends with the Herodians about killing him. Chapter three. Crowds get so bad he keeps a boat idling offshore. He picks twelve, nicknames two Sons of Thunder, and Mark flags the betrayer on the spot. Then his family turns up to take him home, and the scribes say he's demon-powered. He asks how Satan firing Satan would even work.
MRK 4:1-41	Crowd is so big he preaches from a boat. Tells the sower story: path, rocks, thorns, good dirt. Nobody asks, so he explains privately and wonders how they'll manage the other parables. Lamps go on stands, not under beds. A seed grows while the farmer sleeps. Mustard seed starts tiny, ends huge. Then a storm on the lake, and he's asleep on a cushion. They wake him with a complaint instead of a request. He tells the sea to be quiet, and it is. Now they're scared of him instead.
MRK 5:1-43	Guy lives in a graveyard, snaps chains, cuts himself. The demons say their name is Legion and ask to move into pigs. Two thousand pigs run off a cliff. The town does the math, healed man versus dead livestock, and asks him to leave. Across the lake, Jairus begs for his dying kid, and on the way a woman who blew twelve years and her money on doctors touches his coat and is fixed. Then: kid's dead. He tells her to get up, then says get her something to eat.
MRK 6:1-27	Back home everyone just sees the carpenter whose brothers and sisters they know, so he barely does anything there and is amazed at how little they believe. He ships the twelve out two by two with a stick and nothing else. Herod hears and decides, oddly, that John the Baptizer came back to life. Mark rewinds: Herodias wanted John dead, Herod kept him alive because he liked listening, then a birthday party, a dance, and a loud oath in front of guests. Next time on Mark: the platter shows up.
MRK 6:28-56	Head delivered, John's disciples bury him. The twelve come back buzzing, Jesus says let's go rest somewhere quiet, and the crowd literally runs around the lake and beats them there. Five loaves, two fish, five thousand men, twelve baskets of leftovers. Then he sends them off in the boat, prays alone, and in the small hours strolls across the water, apparently planning to walk right past. They think he's a ghost and scream. Mark's explanation for why they're so rattled: they still hadn't figured out the bread thing.
MRK 7:1-37	Pharisees come at him about unwashed hands. He blows straight past hands to Corban, the trick where your parents' support money gets dedicated to God instead. Nice tradition, he says, shame about the commandment. Food doesn't make you dirty, what comes out of you does, and then he lists it. A Greek woman argues him down over dogs and crumbs and wins the exchange outright. A deaf man gets fingers in the ears, spit, a sigh, and one word: Ephphatha. Told to keep quiet, everyone tells everyone.
MRK 8:1-38	Round two: four thousand, seven loaves, seven baskets left. Pharisees demand a sign, he sighs deeply and says no. In the boat he warns about the yeast of the Pharisees and Herod, and the disciples decide he's annoyed they forgot bread. He walks them through the numbers and asks if they seriously still don't get it. A blind guy at Bethsaida needs two goes and says people look like walking trees. Peter nails the big question, then tells Jesus off for talking about dying, and gets called Satan.
MRK 9:1-26	Six days later three of them hike up a mountain and Jesus changes in front of them. Moses and Elijah turn up. Peter, who Mark says had no idea what to say, suggests tents. A cloud shuts that down. At the bottom the other nine have failed with a boy with a mute spirit, and the scribes love it. Dad says if you can do anything. Jesus throws the if right back. Dad's comeback: I believe, help my unbelief. Next time on Mark: the boy looks dead.
MRK 9:27-50	He takes the kid's hand and stands him up, then tells the disciples privately that this kind needs prayer. He mentions his own death again; they don't get it, are too scared to ask, and spend the road arguing about who's greatest. He sits them down: want to be first, be last. Puts a kid in the middle. John proudly reports shutting down a freelance exorcist, expecting a well done, and gets told to stop. Then millstones, and cutting off hands, feet and eyes, and abruptly a bit about salt.
MRK 10:1-29	Pharisees try to trap him on divorce. He goes past Moses to the beginning: two become one, don't split it. Kids get brought over, the disciples turn them away, and Jesus is annoyed: the kingdom belongs to these. Then a rich guy kneels and says he's kept every commandment since childhood. Jesus loves him and tells him to sell it all. He walks off gutted. Camel, needle, and who can possibly make it. Peter points out that they gave up everything. Next time on Mark: what that gets you.
MRK 10:30-52	A hundred times back, he says: houses, family, land, and then, casually, with persecutions. Great sales pitch. On the road to Jerusalem he lays out what's coming: mocked, spat on, flogged, killed. James and John's takeaway: ask for the two best seats. The other ten are furious, probably because they didn't ask first. He tells them the world's rulers throw their weight around; not the job here. In Jericho, blind Bartimaeus yells, gets shushed, yells louder, ditches his cloak, and follows Jesus down the road.
MRK 11:1-33	He sends two guys to untie somebody's donkey with an excuse ready. Cloaks, branches, hosannas, huge entrance, and then he walks into the temple, looks around, notices it's late, and goes home. Next day he curses a fig tree for having no figs out of season, then storms the temple, flips the tables, and quotes house of prayer at them. Tree's dead by morning. Peter is impressed. The chief priests demand to know who gave him permission; he asks one question about John they can't answer either way.
MRK 12:1-23	Parable: a landowner rents out a vineyard, the tenants beat up every servant he sends, then kill his son, figuring the inheritance is theirs. The chief priests work out that they are the tenants, and only the crowd stops an arrest. Then Pharisees and Herodians roll up with a tax question with no safe answer; he asks whose face is on the coin. Then the Sadducees, who don't do resurrection, bring up a woman who married seven brothers. Next time on Mark: they want to know whose wife she is.
MRK 12:24-44	He tells the Sadducees they're wrong twice: nobody marries after the resurrection, and God said I am the God of Abraham, not I was. A scribe asks for the top commandment, gets love God and love your neighbor, and is told he's close. After that nobody risks another question. He poses a riddle about David calling his descendant Lord, then warns about scribes who love long robes and eat widows' houses. Then he watches a widow drop two tiny coins in the offering box and says she outgave everybody.
MRK 13:1-37	A disciple points out the impressive temple stones. Jesus says not one will be left on another. Four ask when, and get an answer that refuses to say when. Wars, earthquakes, famines: that's the warm-up. You'll get dragged into court, don't script your defense. When you see the abomination standing where it shouldn't, run, don't go back for your coat. Fake messiahs, sun goes dark, stars fall, Son of Man in the clouds. Nobody knows the day, not the angels, not even the Son. So: stay awake.
MRK 14:1-34	Two days out from Passover, the priests want him arrested quietly. At Bethany a woman pours expensive nard on his head and gets grumbled at about the cost. He tells them to leave her alone: she has prepared his body for burial. Judas goes to the priests for money. At the table he says one of the twelve will hand him over, then breaks bread and passes the cup. At Gethsemane he says his soul is crushed to the point of death. Next time on Mark: he prays alone.
MRK 14:35-72	He falls on the ground and prays for the hour to pass, then says: not what I want, what you want. Three times he comes back to find them asleep. Judas shows up with an armed crowd and marks him with a kiss. Someone takes off a servant's ear. Everyone runs, one young man leaving his clothes behind. The witnesses can't keep their stories straight, so the high priest asks outright, and he answers outright. Peter denies knowing him three times, the rooster crows, and he breaks down.
MRK 15:1-47	Pilate asks if he's the King of the Jews, gets a short answer, then nothing. Given a choice, the crowd picks Barabbas, and Pilate hands Jesus over. The soldiers put purple on him, jam on a thorn crown, salute him and beat him. Simon of Cyrene is pulled from the crowd to carry the cross. At midday the sky goes dark three hours, then he cries out asking why God has left him, and dies. The temple curtain rips top to bottom. The centurion says he really was God's Son.
MRK 16:1-20	Three women turn up at dawn with spices, worrying about who'll move the giant stone. It's already moved. A guy in white says he's risen: tell the disciples, and Peter. They run off and tell absolutely nobody because they're terrified, which is a wild place to stop a book.
