The rest lists the sightings anyway: Mary Magdalene, nobody believes her; two guys walking, nobody believes them either; then the eleven at dinner, who get chewed out for it. Then go everywhere, preach, expect signs, and he's taken up.
PS2 1:1-7	Seven verses of David telling his own story, and modesty is not the strong suit. Smallest brother, stuck on sheep duty, built himself an instrument and tuned the lute by hand. Who is going to tell God about that? God already heard. So an angel pulls him out of the sheep pen and anoints him, skipping past the taller brothers. Then Goliath in two lines flat: the guy curses him by his idols, and David takes his own sword and removes his head with it.
PSA 1:1-6	Two kinds of people, and the psalm is not subtle about which one it prefers. Guy one avoids the bad crowd entirely, walking, standing and sitting, and reads the law obsessively. He ends up a tree by a river with fruit on time and leaves that never brown. Guy two is chaff. Wind arrives, chaff leaves. That is the whole review. Only one of them is still standing at judgment.
PSA 2:1-12	World leaders get together to plot against God, and God laughs. Not thunder, not a speech. A laugh. Then he announces he has already installed his king on Zion, hands him the nations as an inheritance, and issues an iron rod for smashing pottery. Closing advice to the plotters: wise up, serve with fear, take shelter. He is not in a long-fuse mood today.
PSA 3:1-8	Enemy count keeps climbing, and everybody is saying God will not bail him out. His counterargument: he went to sleep and woke up again. That is it. That is the proof. Ten thousand people surrounding him and he is still not scared. He does ask God to hit his enemies in the jaw and break their teeth, so he is not exactly serene about it.
PSA 4:1-8	Prayer at bedtime with a side of grievance. How long are you people going to trash my name and chase nonsense? Go lie down, think hard, and be quiet about it. Everyone out there is asking who is going to show them something good. He says he is already happier than people having a record grain and wine year. Then he lies down and sleeps fine, which is the actual flex.
PSA 5:1-12	Morning prayer, request filed, and then he sits there watching for the reply. Next he lists what God cannot stomach: liars, braggarts, killers, flatterers. Their throats are open graves. He asks God to let their own plans wreck them and to let everyone hiding in God shout about it. Meanwhile he is walking into the temple regardless of how any of this goes.
PSA 6:1-10	Opening ask: please do not discipline me while you are angry. Then the argument, which is honestly a bit cheeky. Dead people cannot praise you, so keeping me alive is good for your numbers. He is soaking his bed with tears every night and his eyes are shot from grief. Then the mood snaps out of nowhere. God heard him, and everyone who has been hounding him can clear off.
PSA 7:1-17	He opens with an oath: if I did what they say, let my enemy stomp my life into the dirt. Bold move. Then he asks God to hold court and rule on his record. God shows up as a judge who is annoyed every single day and has already sharpened the sword and lit the arrows. Meanwhile the bad guy digs a pit, falls into his own pit, and gets his own violence dropped on his head.
PSA 8:1-9	Guy looks at the night sky, decides God made it with his fingers, and immediately goes existential. What even are we, that you would bother? Then the twist, we do fine. Barely below the angels, wearing a crown, put in charge of sheep, cattle, wild animals, birds and fish. It starts and finishes on the exact same line about how great God's name is. Bookended on purpose.
PSA 9:1-20	Full-hearted thank-you note listing everything God wrecked: enemies routed, nations told off, their names erased, their cities remembered by nobody at all. The throne is up permanently and court is always in session. Then he pivots to his own case and asks to be pulled back from the gates of death. The nations, he points out, fell into a pit they dug themselves. Final ask: remind them they are only men.
PSA 10:1-18	Opens with a straight-up why are you standing way over there. Then the character study. The wicked guy is rich, smug, hunts poor people from ambush like a lion, nets them, and privately assures himself God has forgotten and is not watching. The psalmist asks God to break his arm. Literally, his arm. It signs off insisting God is king and does hear the helpless, which sounds a lot like a man convincing himself.
PSA 11:1-7	Friends tell him to run for the hills like a bird, because the archers are aiming from the dark and the foundations are going. He says no. His whole case is that God is still in the temple, still on the throne, still watching people closely. And the forecast for the violent is burning coals, fire, sulfur and a scorching wind, poured out like a drink. Hard pass on fleeing.
PSA 12:1-8	Emergency: nobody honest is left. Everyone is flattering, everyone is running a second face, and their motto is basically our mouths, our rules, who is going to stop us. God actually interrupts to say he is getting up, because the needy are groaning. Then the comparison: God's words are silver refined seven times, theirs are not. Last line notes the creeps are everywhere because being awful is currently a promotion.
PSA 13:1-6	He asks how long four times in about two verses, which tells you everything. Forgotten? Face hidden? Sad every day? Enemy winning? Then he asks God to light up his eyes before he sleeps the permanent kind. And then, with zero explanation and nothing obviously fixed, he finishes on trust and singing.
PSA 14:1-7	The fool's entire theology is there is no God. So God leans out of heaven to check whether anyone down there gets it. Headcount: zero. Not one. The bad guys eat God's people like it is lunch and never call on him. Then something spooks them badly. It ends on a sigh: somebody please send rescue out of Zion already.
PSA 15:1-5	Who gets to stay on God's holy hill? Not a ritual checklist, a character one. Do not lie, including to yourself. Do not trash people. Do not hurt your friends. Keep your promise after it starts costing you, which is the hard one. No loan sharking, no taking cash to burn an innocent guy. Do that and nothing knocks you over.
PSA 16:1-11	He is out on other gods, specifically out on their blood drink offerings, and he will not even say their names. What he has instead gets described like real estate: my portion, my cup, and the property lines landed in a great neighbourhood. He gets advice from his own heart at night. Ends fully assured: not getting left in Sheol, getting the path of life, joy maxed out, pleasures forever.
PSA 17:1-15	He invites God to audit him, points out the overnight inspection already turned up nothing, and asks to be kept like the apple of an eye and tucked under some wings. The enemy is a lion crouched and waiting. Then the sharpest bit. His rivals have everything, full stomachs, rich kids, inheritance stacked, and that is all they get, this life. He wants to wake up and see God's face instead.
PSA 18:1-50	Fifty verses, no volume knob. He yells for help and God arrives as an actual thunderstorm: ground shaking, smoke out of the nostrils, fire out of the mouth, riding a cherub, throwing lightning until the seabed is showing. The rescue takes one line, pulled out of deep water. After that it is a highlight reel: clean hands, leaping walls, feet like a deer, enemies beaten into street mud. And he credits all of it to God being gentle with him.
PSA 19:1-14	Two psalms bolted together and it works. Part one: the sky preaches nonstop with no actual words, and the sun bounces out like a groom leaving the dressing room, torching everything on its lap. Part two: the law is perfect, sure, right, pure, clean and true, and worth more than gold or honeycomb. Then it goes quiet and personal. Forgive the sins I do not even know about, and keep me off the ones I do.
PSA 20:1-9	Pre-battle blessing over the king. May God pick up, may help come out of the sanctuary, may all those offerings count for something, may you get what you actually want. Banners up. Then the line everyone remembers: other people bring chariots and horses, this lot are bringing a name. And it calls the result before kickoff. They go down, we stay standing. Closing shout: save us.
PSA 21:1-13	This is the receipt for the last psalm's prayer. The king asked and got it: heart's desire, gold crown, life extended way past reasonable. Then it turns dark fast. The enemies get found, furnaced, swallowed, their descendants cut off, and a drawn bow pointed at their faces. Their plot never had a chance. The closing line is praise, which lands a bit heavy right after all that.
PSA 22:1-31	This one starts where you cannot go any lower: my God, my God, why have you abandoned me. He prays all day and night and hears nothing back, and calls himself a worm. The crowd mocks him, telling him to let God rescue him if God is fond of him. His body gives out: bones out of joint, heart melting like wax, every rib countable while they stare and cast lots for his clothes. The turn comes late and quiet: God did not hide, and heard him when he cried.
PSA 23:1-6	Yeah, that one. Shepherd, green grass, still water, soul patched up. The dark valley does not get skipped, he walks straight through it, but with a rod and a staff and no fear. Then the picture swaps. God sets him a dinner table right in front of his enemies, pours oil on his head, and keeps filling the cup past the brim. Goodness chasing him for life. Six verses, nothing wasted.
PSA 24:1-10	Verse one claims the whole planet and everyone standing on it. Then the door policy for God's hill: clean hands, pure heart, no swearing deceitfully. The rest is clearly meant to be yelled at an actual gate. Heads up, doors, the King of glory is coming through. Voice from the other side: who is that then? Answer: Yahweh, strong, mighty, battle-tested. They run the whole exchange twice, louder the second time.
PSA 25:1-22	Half the psalm is teach me your ways, the other half is please forget what I did. He asks God not to remember the sins of his youth, then asks pardon for the current one because, quote, it is great. Bold defence. In between he keeps calming down into steady lines about God guiding humble people. He is lonely, netted, hated by a lot of guys. Last verse he zooms out and asks for the whole nation.
PSA 26:1-12	He tells God to examine him, test him, run the full scan, which takes nerve. His proof is basically a list of places he refused to sit: not with liars, not with hypocrites, not with the evildoer crowd. He washes his hands and circles the altar. What he actually dreads is getting lumped in with the bribe-taking bloodthirsty lot at sorting time. Also he genuinely loves the temple, and says so.
PSA 27:1-14	First half: bring an entire army, I am not scared, God is my light. Second half: please do not hide from me, please do not leave me, even my mother and father might. Same psalm, no apology for the mood swing. And right in the middle, the one thing he actually asks for is not winning, it is living in God's house permanently. Last line is him telling himself to wait. Twice. So clearly waiting is the hard bit.
PSA 28:1-9	His real fear is not a no, it is silence. If God says nothing, he reckons, he might as well be down in the pit already. He asks not to get dragged off with the guys who say hi warmly while stacking plans behind their backs. Their sentence: paid out exactly what their hands built. Then the mood flips mid-psalm, because God heard him, and he signs off asking God to shepherd the whole nation.
PSA 29:1-11	It is a storm, tracked from the water inland, and the word voice shows up seven times. God's voice snaps the cedars of Lebanon, makes mountains hop around like calves, throws lightning, rattles a desert, strips the forests bare. Everyone in the temple just yells Glory, because what else is there. Then the last line, after all that racket: what God hands his people is strength and peace. Quietest ending going.
PSA 30:1-12	Recovery song, and he owns up to the awkward part. Back when things were good he announced nothing would ever move him. God hid his face for one second and he fell to bits. His prayer from rock bottom is straight bargaining: what do you get out of me dying, can dust sing? Verse five is the famous one, crying takes the night shift, joy clocks in at dawn. Sackcloth off, dancing on.
PSA 31:1-24	Two images tell you exactly how bad it is: forgotten like a dead man, and broken pottery. His own neighbors find him pathetic and people who spot him on the street run the other way. Verse five he hands God his spirit, a line that gets famously reused later. Then it turns. His times are in God's hand, there is goodness stacked in storage, and there is a hiding place specifically from people talking. He admits he panicked and said he was finished, and God heard him regardless.
PSA 32:1-11	Keeping quiet about it made him physically ill. Bones wasting, God's hand pressing down day and night, strength drying up like summer heat. Then he says it out loud and it is over in a single verse. Then he switches to giving advice, gently, right up to the line where he says do not be a horse or a mule, animals with no clue that need a bit and bridle to come anywhere near you. Rude. Fair. Everyone shouts at the end.
PSA 33:1-22	Starts as sheet music: new song, ten-string harp, play it well and loud. Then the point. God said it and the sky existed, breathed and got the stars. Then it starts taking things away. Nations plan, God cancels. Big army? Will not save the king. Big muscles? Will not save the strong man. Warhorse? Called a vain thing to its face. What is left is God's eye on the people hoping in him, and everybody waiting.
PSA 34:1-22	Praise for a dozen verses, then it becomes a lesson without warning. He asked, God answered, and everyone who looked to God walked off radiant. Taste and see, he says, which is a dare rather than an argument. Even young lions go hungry, but people looking for God do not. Then he sits the kids down for the syllabus: watch your mouth, quit evil, do good, chase peace and actually run after it. Softest line is the best one: God is close to broken hearts.
PSA 35:1-28	He asks God to physically grab a shield and a spear and get into the fight, and also to lean over and say I have got you. The real wound is this. When these people were sick he wore sackcloth, fasted, and mourned like it was his own mother. When he went down they turned up to enjoy it and would not stop tearing at him. They actually say Aha, twice, which is worse than a threat somehow. He wants their hidden net to snap shut on them.
PSA 36:1-12	First four verses are a character report. The guy flatters himself so hard he genuinely cannot locate his own sin anymore, and he does his scheming lying in bed. Then the psalm swings upward and goes huge. Love reaching the sky, righteousness like mountains, judgments like an ocean trench, and God looking after the animals too. There is a river of pleasure and a spring of life. Then one small ask: keep the boot of pride off me.
PSA 37:1-40	Forty verses aimed at one feeling: it is not fair that awful people are doing well. The advice repeats on purpose. Do not fret. Trust. Delight. Commit. Rest. Wait. Quit being angry. The whole argument is timing, which is why God laughs at the wicked guy, having already seen the date. Best bit is the old man's testimony: I watched a bad guy grow like a big healthy tree, went back later, and there was nothing there at all.
PSA 38:1-22	Basically a medical chart handed in as a confession. Nothing works, the wounds have gone rotten, his waist burns, his heart pounds, his eyesight is going, and he blames his own stupidity for all of it. His friends and relatives have quietly relocated to a safe distance. His plan for handling his enemies is to go completely deaf and silent. The last verses shrink into short requests: do not leave me, do not be far, hurry up.
PSA 39:1-13	He decides to keep his mouth shut around the wicked. Holds it. Holds it. The silence gets hot like a fire, and then he blurts out something way darker than whatever he was suppressing. Turns out his life is a few hand widths long, basically a breath, a walking shadow stacking money for some stranger to spend. He calls himself a foreigner passing through, like everyone before him. Last line: back off long enough for me to feel okay before I am gone.
PSA 40:1-17	First thing that happens: he gets pulled out of a mud pit, stood on solid rock, and issued a new song. Then the turn. God did not actually want sacrifices and offerings, he wanted an open ear, and the reply is just here I am. He points out he has not kept quiet about any of it. Then, straight after all that testifying, he admits his sins outnumber the hairs on his head and begs God to hurry. Confidence, then the wobble.
PSA 41:1-13	Promise up front: God sits at the bedside of anyone who looks after the poor. Then it zooms in on his own sickroom. People come to visit, lie straight to his face, then walk outside and tell everyone. The rumour mill has already decided he is not getting up again. And the worst part, the friend who used to eat at his table has turned on him. He asks to be healed specifically so he can get even, which is honest if nothing else. Two amens, and Book One closes.
PSA 42:1-11	Homesick, and it comes out as thirst. He remembers leading the crowd into the temple singing, and now he is eating his own tears while people keep asking him where his God went. He is stuck up north by the Jordan, Hermon and Mizar, nowhere near home. Deep calling to deep, waves going right over him. Twice he stops and asks his own soul what its problem is, then tells it to hope. Guy is arguing with himself.
PSA 43:1-5	Same poem, same chorus, but now the request has teeth: take my case, God, I am up against a whole nation of liars. He wants light and truth sent out as guides to walk him back to the holy hill and the altar, where the harp is waiting. Then he asks his soul what is wrong for the third time and gives it the exact same answer. Some questions need repeating.
PSA 44:1-26	Grandparents' version first: God cleared out nations and planted us here, and no, our swords had nothing to do with it. Then verse nine says but, and the rest is a formal complaint. Sold off for nothing, scattered, turned into a joke people tell, treated like sheep at the slaughterhouse. And they swear they never forgot him or broke the covenant. It finishes shaking God by the shoulders asking why he is asleep. No reply arrives.
PSA 45:1-17	Royal wedding song, and the poet opens by telling you his tongue is basically a professional's pen. Not humble. The groom gets a sword on his thigh, sharp arrows, a forever throne, and robes smelling of myrrh, aloes and cassia. The bride gets told to forget her family and her home country, delivered as friendly advice and absolutely not free. She walks in wearing gold and embroidery with her companions behind her. Their sons get to be princes.
PSA 46:1-11	Worst case scenario, front and centre. Mountains sliding into the ocean, water roaring, the earth itself changing. The promise is not that it will not happen, it is that nobody panics when it does. Meanwhile there is a river and a city just sitting there, glad. God ends wars by snapping the gear: bows broken, spears shattered, chariots burned. And be still, know that I am God, is aimed at the raging nations, not at anxious people.
PSA 47:1-9	Nine verses of pure noise. Everybody clap, everybody shout. The occasion is a coronation, God heading up to the throne with a roar and a trumpet blast. Sing praises turns up four times in about a verse and a half, so they really wanted that part. Then the last verse quietly does something big: foreign rulers get gathered in with Abraham's people. Easy to miss under all the shouting.
PSA 48:1-14	It is a walking tour with a war story in the middle. Enemy kings show up together to size Zion up, take one look, and leave immediately, panicking like a woman in labour. Then the actual instruction: go walk around the city, count the towers, check the walls, look at the palaces, and go tell your kids the numbers. The tour is not really about buildings. Last line: this God guides us the whole way.
PSA 49:1-20	Wisdom riddle, harp accompaniment, rich and poor both told to sit down and listen. Point one: money is useless where it counts. You cannot buy your brother out of dying, the price is permanently too high. Smart people and idiots die at the same rate and hand it all to someone else. The rich name their land after themselves, which is sweet and pointless. Grimmest bit: Death gets to be the shepherd. He calls them animals twice.
PSA 50:1-23	God calls a courtroom to order with heaven and earth as witnesses, and immediately clarifies the problem. Nobody stopped sacrificing. They did not. The problem is the assumption behind it. He owns every animal in the forest and the livestock on a thousand hills, and if he were hungry, he says, he would not be asking you. Then he rounds on people who quote his rules while approving of thieves and slandering their own brothers. Killer line: I kept silent, and you assumed I was just like you.
S3Y 1:1-29	Three guys are strolling around inside a furnace, singing. The story treats this as the backdrop, not the plot. Azarias prays: you are right, we are wrong, we sinned, and you handed us to the worst king going. No prince, no prophet, no altar, nothing left to sacrifice, so take a broken heart instead of ten thousand fat lambs. Outside, the staff shovel in fuel until the flames hit forty-nine cubits and roast the Chaldeans standing too close. Inside, an angel turns it into a cool damp breeze.
S3Y 1:30-68	The whole thing is a list, and it leaves nothing out. Heavens. Angels. The water above the sky. Sun, moon, stars, rain, dew, wind, fire, heat, night, day, light, dark, frost, snow, lightning, clouds, mountains, plants, seas, springs, whales, birds, cattle, humans, Israel, priests, and last of all Ananias, Azarias and Misael by name, thanking God for getting them out of the flames. Every line ends identically: praise and exalt him above all forever. A hymn made purely of repetition, performed inside a furnace. Honestly, fair enough.
SUS 1:1-33	Susanna: honourable husband, devout parents, big house with a garden that doubles as the local courthouse. Two elders get made judges and both quietly become obsessed with her. Neither admits it until they catch each other sneaking back to the same garden. So they hide, wait until she sends the maids off for oil, and corner her with an ultimatum: agree, or we swear in court we caught you with some young man. She says no and screams. They start yelling too. Next time on Susanna: whoever talks first wins.
SUS 1:34-64	The two judges testify: there was a young man, he was too strong for us, he got the doors open and ran. The court believes them purely because they are elders, and sentences her to death. Susanna prays out loud. Then Daniel stops the execution and points out nobody actually examined the witnesses. So he does it himself, one at a time: which tree? A mastick, says the first. A holm, says the second. The pair get the sentence they had cooked up for her.
TOB 1:1-22	Tobit introduces himself with a five-generation family tree and a flex: everyone else went in for the calf, he kept showing up in Jerusalem. Then he gets deported to Nineveh, refuses the local food, and lands the job of royal shopper. He stashes ten talents of silver with a guy called Gabael. Then he starts secretly burying people the king executed, gets ratted out, and loses the lot. The king gets stabbed by his own sons. Tobit's nephew pulls a string, and Tobit comes home.
TOB 2:1-14	Big Pentecost dinner, and Tobit tells his son to go fetch a poor man to share it. Tobias comes back with: there is a strangled body dumped in the market. Dinner is off. Tobit hides the corpse till sunset, buries it, and the neighbours roast him for it. Then he sleeps outside by the wall, birds drop dung straight into his eyes, and he goes blind. His wife earns a goat doing piecework. He instantly accuses her of stealing it. She fires back: so where is all that righteousness now?
TOB 3:1-17	Tobit prays to just die already. Same day, hundreds of miles away, Sarah prays the identical prayer. A demon called Asmodaeus has killed seven husbands on seven wedding nights, and now the household maids are blaming her for it. She thinks about hanging herself, decides that would destroy her father, and asks for death instead. Both prayers land at once, and God sends Raphael to fix the eyes, tie up the demon, and arrange a wedding. Nobody down here gets told any of this.
TOB 4:1-21	Tobit has just asked to die, then remembers: hang on, nobody knows about the money. So he calls in Tobias and delivers twenty verses of life advice first. Bury me. Be good to your mother. Give to the poor. Seriously, give to the poor. One more time, give to the poor. Marry a relative. Pay your workers same day. Do not get hammered. Ask wise people for advice. Then, finally, verse twenty: by the way, there are ten talents of silver parked with Gabael in Rages.
TOB 5:1-22	Tobias raises one small issue: great plan, but I have never met this Gabael. Go hire a guide, says Tobit. Tobias hires Raphael, an actual angel, and clocks none of it. Tobit interviews him about his family tree, likes the answers, agrees on a drachma a day plus a bonus if they come back fine. The dog tags on. Anna cries that money is not worth her kid. Tobit says relax, a good angel is with him.
TOB 6:1-17	Down at the Tigris a fish lunges at Tobias, the angel says grab it, and they grill it, keeping the heart, liver and gall. Tobias asks why. Raphael: burn the first two and any demon runs for it, rub the third on blind eyes and they clear. Then he casually adds that tonight they are staying with Raguel, whose daughter Sarah is legally yours to marry. Tobias: about those seven dead husbands. Raphael: bring the fish bits, you will be fine.
TOB 7:1-18	Sarah opens the door. Raguel takes one look at Tobias and says he is the image of his cousin Tobit, finds out that is exactly right, hugs him, then bursts into tears on hearing that Tobit is blind. Dinner arrives, and Tobias will not eat a bite until the wedding is legally done. Raguel says eat first, then mentions oh by the way, seven husbands, all died overnight, then marries her to him regardless, writes it up and seals it. Edna makes up the room and cries with her daughter.
TOB 8:1-21	Wedding night. Tobias sets the fish organs smoking on the incense, the demon catches one whiff and bolts to upper Egypt, where the angel ties it up. Then the newlyweds get out of bed and pray first. Meanwhile Raguel is outside digging a grave, because why would he not be, and sends a maid at dawn to check. She reports back: both asleep, both breathing. Raguel blesses God, has the hole quietly filled in, and starts a fourteen-day party.
TOB 9:1-6	Tobias is legally stuck at his own wedding for a fortnight, so he sends the angel to do the errand instead. Take a servant, take two camels, go get my money off Gabael, and bring him back for the party. Also my dad is counting days and will spiral. Raphael goes, produces the receipt, collects the bags with the seals still on, and is back by morning with Gabael in tow. The entire reason for the trip wraps up in six verses while the groom stays home.
TOB 10:1-12	In Nineveh, Tobit is counting days and inventing reasons. Maybe he is held up. Maybe Gabael died. Anna goes straight to my son is dead, stops eating, and walks out to the road every single day to watch for him. Fourteen days up, Tobias asks to go home. Raguel tries let me just send a letter, gets a no, and hands over Sarah plus half of everything he owns, servants and cattle included. Edna's goodbye is half blessing, half threat: that is my daughter, do not make her cry.
TOB 11:1-19	Raphael says let us run ahead of your wife and get the house ready, and bring that fish gall. Anna spots him and tells Tobit she can die happy now. Tobit trips at the door, and his son smears gall in his eyes. It stings, he rubs, the film peels off, and he can see. He walks out to greet his daughter-in-law, blessing God while the neighbours stare. Seven more days of wedding.
TOB 12:1-22	Time to settle up with the guide. Tobias offers half of everything they hauled home. The guide says keep it, gives a short speech about prayer, fasting and giving to the poor, and then drops it: I carried your prayers to God, I stood beside you at every burial, I am Raphael, one of the seven. Also I never ate a bite, that was a vision. Both of them hit the floor. He says write this down, and vanishes.
TOB 13:1-18	Tobit writes an entire psalm. Core idea: the God who wrecks you is the God who mends you, same hand, no exceptions. Give thanks in front of the nations, because that is why you got scattered among them. Turn back with your whole heart and he turns back to you. Then he gets onto Jerusalem and loses the plot in the best way: sapphires, emeralds, walls of solid gold, streets paved in beryl, every street shouting hallelujah.
TOB 14:1-15	Blind at fifty-eight, sighted again eight years later, and now extremely old. Tobit gathers Tobias and the six grandkids for final instructions: get out of Nineveh, Jonah called it and the place is finished, bury me and your mother properly, and remember Aman dug a pit for Achiacharus and fell into it himself. He dies mid-sentence at a hundred and fifty-eight. Tobias relocates to Ecbatana, buries the in-laws in style, inherits everything twice, and reaches a hundred and twenty-seven. Last news he hears: Nineveh is gone. He is delighted.
JOS 1:1-18	Moses just died, so God's opening move is to tell Joshua 'be strong and courageous' about four times, which is either encouraging or a hint that this job is terrifying. Joshua tells everyone to pack three days of food and reminds the Jordan's far side tribes they still owe combat duty. The people say they'll obey completely, on pain of death, which is a strong yes.
JOS 2:1-24	Two spies go check out Jericho and immediately hole up at Rahab's place, because subtlety was never the plan. Soldiers come looking, Rahab lies without blinking and stashes the guys under a pile of flax on her roof. Her price for the cover story: spare her whole family when the city falls. Deal's sealed with a scarlet cord hanging out her window, and she lowers them down the wall by rope.
JOS 3:1-17	Time to cross the Jordan, and it's flood season, so naturally this is when God picks to do the miracle. Priests carrying the ark step in, the river backs up in a heap way upstream, and all of Israel just walks across on dry ground like it's a Tuesday. The priests hold their position in the middle the whole time, which takes nerve.
JOS 4:1-24	Twelve guys, one per tribe, grab twelve rocks from the exact spot the priests were standing in the Jordan and stack them up as a permanent ask your kids story starter. Joshua drops a matching stack right in the riverbed for good measure. Forty thousand armed men march across, the Jordan goes back to normal, and everybody sets up camp at Gilgal.
JOS 5:1-15	Enemy kings are already terrified, so Joshua's first move is scheduling mass circumcision for an entire generation, with flint knives, at a place literally named Foreskin Hill. Then Passover, then the manna just stops the same day they eat local food, tidy timing. Joshua bumps into an armed stranger who turns out to be running the operation and tells him to lose the shoes.
JOS 6:1-27	Seven days of silent marching around Jericho with trumpets, then seven laps on day seven, one big shout, and the wall just falls over. Everyone and everything in the city gets wiped out except Rahab's family, who get pulled out as promised. The city burns, the metal goes into the treasury, and Joshua puts a curse on anyone who ever rebuilds the place. Specific curse. Don't rebuild it.
JOS 7:1-26	One guy, Achan, pockets loot he was specifically told not to touch, and next thing you know Israel's getting beaten by Ai and losing thirty-six men. Joshua rips his clothes and basically asks God why they bothered leaving Egypt. The lot casting narrows it down to Achan, who confesses: a nice robe, some silver, some gold, buried under his tent. Stoning and burning follow, for him and everything attached to him.
JOS 8:1-18	Ai, round two, and this time Joshua's got a plan instead of just showing up. Thirty thousand guys hide in ambush while the rest fake a retreat to drag the city's whole army out chasing them. Works like clockwork, and the cue to spring the trap is literally Joshua pointing a javelin at the town. Next time on Joshua: the trap springs.
JOS 8:19-35	Ambush team takes the empty city and burns it, and with nowhere left to run, the men of Ai get wiped out, twelve thousand people total. The king gets hanged, then dumped under a pile of stones at the gate. Then, whiplash, Joshua builds an altar and reads the entire law out loud to everyone, including the kids. Very different vibe from five minutes earlier.
JOS 9:1-27	Gibeon can't beat Israel, so they con them instead: moldy bread, patched sandals, torn wineskins, the whole we traveled from very far away act. Israel signs the treaty without checking with God first, which is the actual mistake here. Three days later, surprise, they're neighbors. Oath's an oath though, so Israel doesn't kill them, just permanently demotes them to woodcutting and water hauling duty.
JOS 10:1-23	Five kings gang up on Gibeon for making peace with Israel, so Joshua does an all night forced march to bail them out. God starts throwing hailstones that kill more guys than the actual swords do, and then the sun just stops moving because Joshua asked it to. The five kings get run down to a cave, where Israel rolls stones over the entrance and leaves them there while the mop up continues. Next time on Joshua: somebody opens the cave.
JOS 10:24-43	The five kings get dragged out of the cave, executed, and put right back in it under a stone seal. Then it's a straight run south, Makkedah, Libnah, Lachish, Eglon, Hebron, Debir, and the pattern is identical every single time: total wipeout, nobody left. The text doesn't dress this up. It just says he left no one remaining, city after city.
JOS 11:1-23	Jabin of Hazor rallies the whole north, horses, chariots, the works, and Israel still wins at the waters of Merom, then hamstrings the horses and torches the chariots per instructions. Only Hazor gets burned to the ground, since it was the ringleader city; the others are left standing. After all that, the land finally gets a break from war.
JOS 12:1-24	This chapter is just the scoreboard. Sihon and Og, Moses' kills from before, then thirty-one more kings Joshua took down on this side of the river, listed one by one like a very grim box score. Thirty-one kings, final tally, chapter over.
JOS 13:1-33	Joshua's old now, and God's first move is basically a to do list of everything still not conquered. Then it's paperwork: recapping exactly what Moses handed Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh east of the river, border by border. The Levites get zero real estate here too, on purpose, they live off the offerings instead.
JOS 14:1-15	Land dividing kicks off, and eighty-five-year-old Caleb muscles to the front of the line. He was forty when he scouted this exact land forty-five years ago and refused to be scared off then, so now he wants the hill country, giant warriors included, thanks. Joshua just hands it over. Honestly, who's going to say no to this guy.
JOS 15:1-14	This chapter is Judah's border, described point by point in a way that only means something with a map in front of you. One actual moment of drama: Caleb personally clears out three sons of Anak from Hebron, because apparently eighty-five is just a number to this guy. Next time on Joshua: Caleb raffles off his daughter.
JOS 15:15-63	Caleb offers his daughter Achsah's hand to whoever conquers Kiriath Sepher, and Othniel wins her. But Achsah's the real MVP here: she shows up and asks her dad for water springs to go with her dry land, and gets both the upper and lower ones. Then it's just town names for Judah, dozens of them, wrapping up with the awkward admission that Jerusalem never actually got taken.
JOS 16:1-10	More border tracing, this time for Ephraim, one of Joseph's two tribes. The one interesting line: they never actually kick the Canaanites out of Gezer, just put them to work instead. This is going to keep happening.
JOS 17:1-18	Manasseh's land gets divided up, and Zelophehad's five daughters, all named, actually get their promised inheritance since dad had no sons. More Canaanites left in place as forced labor instead of driven out, noticing a pattern. Then Joseph's tribe whines about only getting one lot, and Joshua basically tells them to stop complaining and go clear their own forest, iron chariots be damned.
JOS 18:1-28	Tabernacle's set up at Shiloh, and Joshua calls out seven tribes for basically procrastinating on claiming their land. He sends out surveyors, splits what's left into seven chunks, and draws lots. Benjamin goes first: a border description plus twenty-six cities, including Jerusalem, which at this point is still very much not Israel's.
JOS 19:1-51	Last six lots get handed out: Simeon squeezed inside Judah's leftover space, then Zebulun, Issachar, Asher, Naphtali, town lists all around. Dan can't actually hold their assigned territory, so they just go conquer a different city, Leshem, and rename it Dan. Even Joshua finally gets his own plot, the town he specifically asked for, and that's the whole map wrapped up.
JOS 20:1-9	Six cities of refuge get set up, three per side of the Jordan, for anyone who kills someone by accident and needs to outrun an angry relative long enough to get an actual trial. He's stuck there until the sitting high priest dies, then he's free to go home. Ancient world's version of witness protection.
JOS 21:1-20	Levites don't get their own turf, so instead they get forty-eight cities scattered through everyone else's territory. Aaron's family gets thirteen of those from Judah, Simeon and Benjamin, including Hebron, though the actual farmland around Hebron already belongs to Caleb, not the priests. Bureaucracy has layers. Next time on Joshua: the rest of the list, and one genuinely good sentence at the end.
JOS 21:21-45	Rest of the Levite cities get assigned, forty-eight total once you add it all up, three doing double duty as refuge cities. Then the chapter zooms out for the big statement: God delivered on literally everything he promised. Not one word of it fell through.
JOS 22:1-18	Joshua sends Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh home with a blessing and their cut of the loot, job well done. Then they build a huge altar by the Jordan on their way out, and the rest of Israel hears about it and immediately assumes civil war is starting. Phinehas gets sent to ask what exactly is going on before anybody actually fights. Next time on Joshua: turns out it was never an altar.
JOS 22:19-34	Turns out the altar was never meant for actual sacrifices, it's a monument, so nobody's kids get told a generation from now that they don't belong. Phinehas buys the explanation, everyone stands down, and they name the thing Witness so the point isn't lost on anybody later.
JOS 23:1-16	Old Joshua calls the leaders together and it's less a pep talk than a warning label: don't intermarry with the nations still around, don't even say their gods' names, or every blessing God just delivered flips straight into a curse. He's watched the good half of the promise come true completely, and he's very clear the bad half works the same way.
JOS 24:1-16	Shechem, and Joshua runs through the entire history, Abraham to right now, in God's own voice, then puts it to the people straight: pick a god already. His own answer is the one that ends up on plaques forever, as for me and my house, we serve Yahweh. The crowd says the same. Next time on Joshua: Joshua tells them they can't actually do it.
JOS 24:17-33	People say yes again, and Joshua, weirdly, argues back: you can't just casually serve this God, he's serious about it and won't shrug off betrayal. They insist anyway, so the covenant gets sealed and a stone set up as a witness. Then Joshua dies at a hundred and ten. Joseph's bones, hauled out of Egypt this whole time, finally get buried too. Loose ends, tied.
RUT 1:1-22	Famine, family moves to Moab, sons marry local girls, and then in short order the dad and both sons die, leaving three widows with nothing. Naomi decides to go home and tells her daughters-in-law to bail while they can. Orpah does. Ruth refuses, gives the speech that's still read at weddings today, your people will be my people, and comes along anyway. They land in Bethlehem right at harvest time, and Naomi tells everyone to call her Bitter instead of her own name.
RUT 2:1-23	Ruth goes out gleaning just to keep them fed, and by total coincidence ends up in a field owned by Boaz, who happens to be related to her dead father-in-law. He clocks her, tells his workers to look out for her and leave extra grain on purpose, and she comes home with way more barley than gleaning should produce. Naomi hears the name and immediately lights up: that guy's family, and not distantly.
RUT 3:1-18	Naomi's plan for Ruth is bold: sneak to the threshing floor at night, wait for Boaz to fall asleep, then uncover his feet and lie down, which is basically a marriage proposal in this culture. Boaz wakes up startled but says yes in spirit, then has to admit there's a closer relative who gets first dibs. He sends her home before sunrise with six measures of barley, which is basically a status update for Naomi.
RUT 4:1-22	Boaz heads straight to the city gate and offers the closer relative first crack at redeeming the land. Guy's in, until he finds out Ruth's part of the package and it'll mess with his own inheritance, so he bows out, sealed with the old shoe swap tradition. Boaz gets the land and the wife. Their kid Obed grows up to be David's grandfather, and the whole book ends with a genealogy flexing that connection.
NAM 1:1-15	Nahum kicks off with God as a jealous avenger and spends a whole chapter on what happens to mountains and oceans when he turns up in a mood. Target is Nineveh. The good news is strictly for everyone Nineveh has been stepping on.
1KI 1:1-27	David is old and can't get warm. Abishag the Shunammite is brought in to keep him warm, nothing more. Adonijah, next up by age, runs the exact Absalom playbook: chariots, horsemen, fifty runners out front. His dad never once asked him why. He throws himself a coronation barbecue, and the guest list gives it away: everyone except Nathan, Benaiah and Solomon. Nathan's counter is choreography, and the pitch is blunt: you and your son are about to be executed. Next time on 1 Kings: David wakes up.
1KI 1:28-53	Asked straight, David answers in one sentence, then runs a coronation like a man who has organised a coup before: my mule, Gihon spring, Zadok and Nathan, one trumpet, go. The mule is the point: the king lending out his own status in public. Adonijah's party is still finishing dessert when the noise reaches them, and Jonathan delivers the news slowly, right down to David bowing on his bed. The guests just leave. Adonijah clings to the altar and gets a conditional promise and a curt go home.
1KI 2:1-23	David's deathbed speech comes in two halves that don't match. Half one: keep the law of Moses, be a man. Half two: a hit list. Joab, who murdered Abner and Amasa, must not die peacefully. Shimei, whom David swore not to kill, is not to be considered innocent, and hey, you're a wise man, you'll figure something out. Then Adonijah, pardoned once already, asks Bathsheba to get him Abishag: a throne claim dressed as a wedding request. Next time on 1 Kings: he works through the list.
1KI 2:24-46	Solomon clears the list in one chapter, brutally efficient. Adonijah dies that same day. Abiathar gets spared for carrying the ark but exiled to Anathoth, closing the account against Eli's house. Joab tries the altar trick that saved Adonijah, and this time it isn't a loophole: he says he'll die right here, and Benaiah obliges. Shimei gets house arrest with one rule, don't cross the Kidron, and keeps it three years until two runaway slaves make him forget. Last line: the kingdom was established in Solomon's hand.
1KI 3:1-28	Solomon marries Pharaoh's daughter, because nothing says stability like an in-law in Egypt. Everyone still sacrifices on hilltops, no temple yet, and Solomon burns a thousand offerings at Gibeon. Then God turns up in a dream: ask for anything. Solomon calls himself a little kid who doesn't know how to go out or come in, and asks for a discerning heart. God, pleased he skipped long life and dead enemies, hands him the money anyway. Then the sword and the baby. A trap, and it works instantly.
1KI 4:1-34	The admin chapter, and the interesting bit is the plumbing. Full cabinet listed, then twelve district officers each feeding the royal court for one month a year. Daily: thirty cors of flour, ten stall-fed cattle, a hundred sheep, plus deer, gazelles and fattened poultry. Two of those twelve are married to Solomon's daughters, one way to keep your supply chain loyal. Then the wisdom stats: three thousand proverbs, a thousand and five songs, cedars through hyssop through fish. Somebody is paying for all of this.
1KI 5:1-18	A trade deal conducted entirely in compliments. Solomon writes to Hiram of Tyre: dad was too busy fighting to build, and honestly nobody cuts timber like you Sidonians. Flattery, and also true. Hiram is thrilled and offers to float the cedar down the coast as rafts. The price is groceries: twenty thousand cors of wheat and twenty of oil, every year. Then the labour numbers: thirty thousand conscripts, seventy thousand haulers, eighty thousand cutting stone. The guy running the forced labour gets named. Remember him.
1KI 6:1-18	Four hundred and eighty years after Egypt, month of Ziv, the temple starts going up. Sixty cubits by twenty by thirty, lattice windows, three floors of side rooms stepping outward on ledges so no beam pierces the wall. And here's the detail the book wants you to keep: every stone was finished at the quarry, so no hammer, axe or iron tool was ever heard on the site. Then cedar over every surface, no stone visible. Next time on 1 Kings: the gold.
1KI 6:19-38	This is the gold chapter and the word turns up in basically every verse. Gold walls, gold altar, gold floor, gold chains across the sanctuary entrance, gold on the carvings and the doors. Inside, two olive-wood cherubim ten cubits tall with wings spread so wide that one wingtip touches each wall and the inner two meet in the middle, so the ark sits under a canopy of wings. Then the flat closing accounts: foundation in Ziv, finished in Bul, seven years total.
1KI 7:1-26	Do the maths the book won't: seven years on God's house, thirteen on Solomon's. His complex has a hall with so many cedar columns they called it the House of the Forest of Lebanon, plus a courtroom and a house for his Egyptian wife. Then Hiram of Tyre shows up, a bronze genius, and the tone shifts to pure craft. Two bronze pillars eighteen cubits tall, with names: Jachin and Boaz. And a giant bronze sea on twelve oxen. Next time on 1 Kings: ten basins on wheels.
1KI 7:27-51	Ten bronze stands, each on four wheels built like chariot wheels, all cast to one identical pattern. Somebody engineered rolling wash stations for a building that was never going anywhere, and the text describes their axles with real affection. Then the full inventory, all cast in clay pits down in the Jordan valley and hauled uphill. Then the line that says everything: Solomon didn't bother weighing the bronze, there was too much of it. Then the gold list, down to the door hinges.
1KI 8:1-24	The ark finally comes up to the temple: elders, tribal heads, a festival, and so many sacrifices nobody counted them. The priests set it under the cherubim's wings, and the writer can't resist noting the carrying poles stick out far enough to see from the holy place, and they're still there. Also dropped in with zero comment: the ark contains nothing but the two stone tablets. Then the cloud rolls in and the priests literally cannot work. Next time on 1 Kings: the actual prayer.
1KI 8:25-44	Solomon opens his prayer with a concession no builder wants to make: heaven itself can't contain you, so obviously this building can't either. And then it becomes a list of disaster scenarios, which is what makes it great. Perjury at the altar. Defeat. Drought. Famine. Plague. Blight, mildew, locusts. Siege. And every man knowing the plague of his own heart. Every case ends the same way: hear from heaven, and forgive. Even the foreigner gets an answer. Next time on 1 Kings: he plans for exile.
1KI 8:45-66	Solomon finishes his prayer by planning for the worst possible outcome, which is exactly what eventually happens: deportation. His starting point is that nobody doesn't sin, so it's not if but when, and his ask is that repentance work from anywhere, prayed toward a place instead of in it. Then he gets off his knees, blesses the crowd, and says not one word of God's promise has failed yet. Then the absurd numbers: twenty-two thousand cattle, a hundred and twenty thousand sheep.
1KI 9:1-28	God shows up a second time and repeats the promise with the fine print read aloud: turn to other gods and I'll rip Israel out of the land, throw this house away, and make you a punchline, with tourists hissing at the ruins asking what happened. That paragraph is the rest of Kings, delivered in advance. Hiram gets twenty Galilean towns for twenty years of timber and gold, inspects them, hates them. A Red Sea fleet brings back four hundred and twenty talents of gold.
1KI 10:1-29	The queen of Sheba turns up to test Solomon with hard questions and a caravan of spices, gold and jewels. He answers everything, and then the tour finishes her: the house, the food, the seating plan, the stairs to the temple. There was no spirit left in her, which is the polite version. Then the inventory piles up. Gold shields, an ivory throne with fourteen lions, gold cups because silver stopped being worth counting, ships bringing apes and peacocks. Annual intake: six hundred and sixty-six talents.
1KI 11:1-23	Seven hundred wives, three hundred concubines, from every nation God said not to marry into. And the charge isn't the arithmetic, it's the construction: Solomon builds hilltop shrines for Chemosh and Molech within sight of the temple he built for Yahweh. The verdict comes with an aggravating factor stapled on: God had appeared to him twice. Sentence: the kingdom torn away, deferred to his son's reign both concessions made for David's sake rather than his. Next time on 1 Kings: a ruined coat.
1KI 11:24-43	Two enemies and one employee. Jeroboam is the dangerous one, and the reason is right there in his job title: Solomon promoted him for being hardworking and put him over the forced labour gangs of the northern tribes. So the guy who knows what the building programme cost now has a following. Then Ahijah catches him alone in a field, rips his brand new coat into twelve pieces, and hands him ten. Forty years reigned, and the obituary is one line.
1KI 12:1-17	The kingdom gets lost in one meeting. The north's request is completely reasonable: your dad worked us into the ground, ease off and we're yours. Rehoboam consults the men who actually ran the country, who give him the best political advice in the Bible: serve them today and they'll serve you forever. Israel answers with the old rebel slogan and nobody stops them. Next time on 1 Kings: he sends the forced-labour boss out to calm them.
1KI 12:18-33	Rehoboam responds to a national strike by sending the minister for forced labour, who is immediately stoned to death, after which the king discovers exactly how fast his chariot goes. Jeroboam's problem is real, because a pilgrimage route to your rival's capital is a security hole. His fix becomes the sin the book measures every northern king against: two gold calves, a priesthood of whoever's available, and a knockoff festival a month late.
1KI 13:1-17	A man of God walks up from Judah and, while Jeroboam is mid-sacrifice, starts shouting at the altar itself. He names the king who'll eventually desecrate it, Josiah, three hundred years early. Jeroboam points at him and yells seize him, and his arm locks solid in the pointing position. The refusal is specific: no bread, no water, go home a different way. Next time on 1 Kings: the lie.
1KI 13:18-34	The old prophet's pitch: I'm a prophet too, and an angel told me to bring you home for a meal. And the man of God takes a second-hand message over the one he got directly. Then, at the table, God's word comes to the liar, who has to tell his guest he won't be buried with his fathers. On the road a lion kills him and stands guarding the body, not eating him, not touching the donkey. Jeroboam, given all of this, changes nothing.
1KI 14:1-14	Jeroboam's son gets sick, so he sends his wife in disguise to the very prophet who made him king, which tells you he already knows the answer and hopes a fake name improves it. Then the template every northern dynasty gets: I lifted you up, you were no David, and now your whole house gets swept out like dung. The one mercy: the boy dies before she gets home. Next time on 1 Kings: the doorway.
1KI 14:15-31	The sentence expands from one family to a whole country: Israel shaken like a reed. Then the detail that lands, the boy dying the exact moment his mother steps onto her own threshold. Then south, where Rehoboam is no better, and Shishak of Egypt rolls up in year five and just takes the treasury, including Solomon's gold shields. Rehoboam has bronze copies made for the door guards to carry on ceremonial duty. Same ritual, cheaper metal, everyone pretending not to notice.
1KI 15:1-19	Two Judean kings, a paragraph each. Abijam: bad and the narrator uses the moment to sum up David as flawless except in the matter of Uriah the Hittite. Then Asa: good, forty-one years. Cult prostitutes out, family idols destroyed, and he removes his own grandmother Maacah as queen mother for making an Asherah image. One blemish: he leaves the hill shrines standing. Then he empties the temple treasury to bribe Syria. Next time on 1 Kings: Damascus cashes the cheque.
1KI 15:20-34	Damascus takes the money and rips through the north, so Baasha drops the blockade and goes home. Asa then does the thing that makes him competent rather than just pious: conscripts all Judah, nobody exempt, to haul Ramah away stone by stone and rebuild it as two fortresses of his own. Meanwhile the north starts its cycle: Nadab lasts two years before an officer named Baasha murders him mid-siege, then exterminates Jeroboam's entire family. Then Baasha does exactly what Jeroboam did. Twenty-four years of it.
1KI 16:1-17	The prophet Jehu gives Baasha the exact same speech Ahijah gave Jeroboam, dogs and birds included, and the repetition is the point: the north keeps electing the same king under new names. Then it speeds up. Elah gets two years and is murdered by his own chariot commander while drunk, and Zimri's first move is exterminating Baasha's entire family, precisely what Baasha did to Jeroboam's. Then Zimri reigns for seven days. Seven. Next time on 1 Kings: the week runs out.
1KI 16:18-34	Zimri's exit strategy is to lock himself in the palace and set it on fire, and even he gets the standard verdict for walking in the way of Jeroboam, over a one-week reign. the single most durable thing anybody manages here: buys a hill for two talents of silver and builds Samaria on it. His verdict of worse than everyone before him holds for exactly one paragraph, because Ahab shows up, marries Jezebel of Sidon and builds Baal a temple in the capital.
1KI 17:1-24	Elijah appears out of nowhere tells Ahab there'll be no rain except on his word, and disappears. Ravens bring him bread and meat at the brook Cherith, which is not a detail anyone would make up. Next stop: a widow in Zarephath, in Sidon, Jezebel's home turf, down to a handful of flour and preparing a last meal for her and her son. Elijah asks to be served first. She does it, and the flour holds. Then her son dies anyway, and she rounds on Elijah, and honestly, fair.
1KI 18:1-24	Best character in this chapter is Obadiah, who runs Ahab's household while secretly keeping a hundred prophets alive in two caves on bread and water. His reaction to bumping into Elijah is pure professional panic: the Spirit will whisk you off, Ahab won't find you, and I'm the one who gets killed. Then Elijah sets terms stacked against himself: four hundred and fifty to one, their pick of bull, nobody lights a fire. Next time on 1 Kings: they hop all day.
1KI 18:25-46	Baal's team gets the whole day and first pick, and gets nothing. Then Elijah starts heckling: shout louder, maybe he's thinking, maybe he stepped out, maybe he's asleep. The self-cutting that follows isn't funny and the text doesn't play it that way. Then Elijah rebuilds God's altar, digs a trench, and has twelve jars of water dumped over it. Fire takes everything, water included. Then the Kishon, a cloud the size of a hand, and a prophet outrunning a chariot.
1SA 1:1-28	Elkanah has two wives. Peninnah has kids and never lets Hannah forget it, and the annual trip to Shiloh is Hannah's worst day of the year. Elkanah's pep talk is aren't I better than ten sons, which, no. Hannah prays silently and Eli, the actual priest, assumes she's hammered. She has Samuel, then weans him and hands him over for good.
1SA 2:1-20	Hannah's song is a power ballad. Bows snap, the starving get full, the childless woman has seven, and God hauls people off the trash heap to sit with princes. Cut to Eli's sons running a protection racket at the altar, stabbing the pot before God gets his cut. Next time on 1 Samuel: somebody tells Eli the truth.
1SA 2:21-36	Eli hears his sons are sleeping with the women who serve at the Tent door and his entire discipline strategy is guys, why though. Then a man of God lays it out: you put your sons ahead of God, so the forever promise is cancelled. Nobody in your line grows old, and here's the proof, your two sons die on the same day.
1SA 3:1-21	Nobody's hearing from God much and Eli can barely see. One night God calls Samuel's name and Samuel runs to Eli three times going you called? So God's first message to Samuel is: everything I said about Eli's house is happening, because he knew and did nothing. Eli makes him repeat it and takes it.
1SA 4:1-22	Israel loses four thousand men, so the elders go get the ark, like it's a weapon you can wheel in. Round two is a massacre: thirty thousand dead, ark captured, both of Eli's sons dead on the same day, exactly as promised. Eli, ninety-eight and blind, sits through the body count and only drops dead when he hears the ark is gone.
1SA 5:1-12	The Philistines haul the ark to Ashdod and park it next to Dagon as a flex. Next morning Dagon is face down in front of it. They prop him back up. Next morning: face down again, head off, both hands off. Then the tumors. Ashdod ships it to Gath, Gath ships it to Ekron, and Ekron starts screaming before it arrives.
1SA 6:1-21	Seven months of plague later, the Philistines ask their priests how to return the thing. Answer: not empty. Five gold tumors and five gold mice, and don't pull a Pharaoh. The test is genuinely clever: two cows that have never been yoked, calves locked up at home, and see where they go. They walk dead straight to Beth Shemesh, mooing the whole way.
1SA 7:1-17	The ark sits in Abinadab's house for twenty years while Israel mopes. Samuel finally lays down terms: dump the foreign gods first. They do it, gather at Mizpah, fast, and admit they blew it. The Philistines see a big crowd, assume it's an army, and attack. Mid-lamb, God thunders and they fall apart. Samuel names a stone Ebenezer: God helped us this far.
1SA 8:1-22	Old Samuel puts his sons in charge at Beersheba and they immediately start taking bribes, which is Eli's story again. So the elders ask for a king. God says let them, they're rejecting me, not you, but read them the invoice. So: your sons drafted, your daughters in the palace kitchens, your best fields, ten percent of everything, and when you complain, silence.
1SA 9:1-13	Meet Saul: son of Kish, a head taller than everyone in Israel, and currently unable to find his dad's donkeys. Four regions, nothing, and he wants to quit before his father starts worrying about him instead of the animals. The servant is the one who knows there's a seer in town. Next time on 1 Samuel: the seer already knows he's coming.
1SA 9:14-27	Samuel got a heads up from God a full day early, so this ambush is premeditated. Saul walks up to the most famous prophet in the nation and asks for directions to the seer's house. Samuel goes: that's me, you're eating with me, your donkeys turned up, and everything worth having in Israel is basically yours. Saul says hold on, I'm from the smallest tribe.
1SA 10:1-27	Samuel pours oil on Saul's head and hands him three receipts: two men at Rachel's tomb with donkey news, three men at the oak of Tabor with two loaves, and a band of prophets where the Spirit will make him a different man. All three land, and is Saul among the prophets too? becomes a saying. At Mizpah they find him hiding in the luggage.
1SA 11:1-15	Nahash rocks up at Jabesh Gilead and offers a peace deal: sure, if every man there loses his right eye, specifically to humiliate Israel. He even gives them a week to find help. Saul hears about it while coming in behind his oxen, because apparently the new king still has a day job. He chops the oxen up and mails the pieces across Israel.
1SA 12:1-25	Samuel's farewell address starts with him auditing himself out loud: whose ox did I take, who paid me off? Nobody can name one. Then the history recap, same loop every time: forget God, get flattened, cry out, get rescued, and never once did that need a king. Then he calls down thunder and rain in wheat harvest, and the crowd instantly begs him to pray.
1SA 13:1-23	Jonathan kicks the war off by smashing the Philistine garrison at Geba. The Philistines answer with thirty thousand chariots, and Israel's grand strategy is hiding in caves, bushes, rocks, tombs and holes. Saul waits the seven days Samuel told him to, watches his army evaporate, and on the last day does the sacrifice himself. Samuel walks in as he's finishing. Verdict: your dynasty is over.
1SA 14:1-26	Jonathan tells nobody and goes with his armour bearer to raid the Philistine outpost. His rule: if they say wait there, we wait; if they say come on up, they're ours. The Philistines yell come up, purely to be rude. Two men climb up and drop twenty of them. Then Saul bans his starving army from eating till sundown. Next time on 1 Samuel: honey.
1SA 14:27-52	Jonathan never heard the no-eating order, dips his stick in a honeycomb and perks right up. Told of it, he says flatly: my father has ruined this day, and if the men had eaten we'd have won bigger. He's right. Saul asks God about a night raid, gets silence, and goes witch-hunting for the sin. Lots say Jonathan. The army saves him.
1SA 15:1-35	Orders: wipe out Amalek entirely, nothing spared. Saul keeps King Agag alive and all the best livestock, destroys only the junk, then reports total success to a prophet who can literally hear sheep. Samuel: so what's that bleating? Saul: the people did it, and it was for sacrifices, to your God. Samuel drops the hammer: obeying beats sacrificing, and God has fired you.
1SA 16:1-23	God tells Samuel to quit sulking and go anoint a new king at Jesse's house. Samuel's reaction: Saul will kill me. Then he sees the tall impressive eldest and thinks that's the guy, and God says you're doing the Saul thing again, I look at the heart. Seven brothers, seven nos. The one nobody invited is out with the sheep.
1SA 17:1-31	Two armies parked on facing hills for forty days, because one gigantic man in an obscene amount of bronze keeps offering to settle the war one-on-one and nobody takes it. Then a teenager turns up with grain and cheeses for his brothers, hears the challenge, and asks what the reward is. Then asks again. Next time on 1 Samuel: five smooth stones.
1SA 17:32-58	David's pitch to the king: relax, I'll do it. Saul says you're a kid. Saul loads him into the royal armour and David takes it all off because he's never trained in it, genuinely the smartest move in the chapter. Staff, sling, five smooth stones. Goliath asks if he's a dog, being come at with sticks. One stone, straight in the forehead, done, then Goliath's own sword takes his head off.
1SA 18:1-30	Jonathan, the actual crown prince, immediately hands David his robe, sword, bow and belt, which is basically resigning in his favour. Then the women make up a chant: Saul killed thousands, David tens of thousands. Saul does the maths out loud: what's left for him except my job? Two spears thrown at a man playing music for him. A front-line promotion meant to get him killed. Merab promised and quietly married off to someone else. Then a bride price of a hundred Philistine foreskins. David brings two hundred.
1SA 19:1-24	Saul tells Jonathan and the staff to kill David. Saul swears an oath and means it for about one battle, then throws a spear at David mid-harp. David runs. Michal lowers him out the window, stuffs the bed with a household idol and goats' hair, and later tells Saul that David threatened to kill her, a lie doing serious work. Saul sends three arrest teams and every one starts prophesying instead. Then Saul goes himself, strips off and lies there a day and a night.
1SA 20:1-20	David turns up asking Jonathan straight out: what did I do? Jonathan insists his dad tells him everything, the kind of thing you believe until you don't. David says there's one step between him and death. So they rig a test: David skips the new moon dinner, Jonathan covers with a family sacrifice in Bethlehem, and they read Saul's reaction. Jonathan's own ask is quietly heartbreaking: look after my family later, when all your enemies are gone. Next time on 1 Samuel: three arrows.
1SA 20:21-42	The code first: arrows on this side means safe, arrows beyond means run. Night one, David's chair is empty and Saul assumes a purity thing. Night two he asks where the son of Jesse is, gets the Bethlehem cover story, and loses it: insults Jonathan's mother, says he'll never inherit while David breathes, orders David executed. Jonathan asks what David actually did, and his father throws a spear at him. Question answered. Then the goodbye in the field, both of them crying, David worst of all.
1SA 21:1-15	David turns up at Nob alone and the priest is instantly nervous. David spins him a line about secret royal business he can't discuss. What he really wants is food, and the only bread is the holy showbread, which he talks his way into. Then he asks for a weapon, and the only one going is Goliath's own sword. He takes it, obviously. Also present that day, casually mentioned: Doeg the Edomite. Remember him. Then David flees to Gath, gets recognised from the hit song, and survives by acting insane.
1SA 22:1-23	David sets up in the cave of Adullam and his first army is everyone in the country who's broke, in debt or fed up. Four hundred of them. Meanwhile Saul sits under a tree with his spear whining that nobody feels sorry for him, and Doeg takes the opening and rats out the priest at Nob. Ahimelech points out he had no idea. Doesn't matter. The guards flatly refuse to touch priests, so Doeg does it: eighty-five priests, then the whole town, women, children, animals. One son escapes.
1SA 23:1-29	Philistines are raiding Keilah, so David checks with God, gets a yes, then checks again for nervous men, and God patiently repeats himself. He saves the town. Then Saul hears David is inside a walled city and is thrilled, because gates and bars work both ways. So David asks two practical questions: will Saul come, and will the people I just rescued hand me over? Yes, and yes. Brutal. He leaves with his six hundred. Then the Ziphites volunteer as informants, and a Philistine raid pulls Saul away.
1SA 24:1-22	Saul takes three thousand men to hunt David at En Gedi, then wanders into a cave alone to use the bathroom. David is at the back of that exact cave. His guys call it God's plan, so David sneaks up, cuts a piece off Saul's robe, and then feels bad about the robe. Saul walks out, and David yells after him, waves the fabric, and asks why the king of Israel is chasing a dead dog. Saul cries, admits David is the better man, and begs for his family.
1SA 25:1-23	Samuel dies in a single verse. Then, sheep. Nabal is loaded and has a personality like a slammed door. His wife Abigail is sharp. David sends ten guys with a polite note that boils down to: nice shearing season, your shepherds came to no harm, got anything for us? Nabal's answer is basically "David who?" David puts on his sword. Four hundred men, on the road, immediately. A servant tips off Abigail, who loads donkeys and rides out without telling Nabal. Next time on 1 Samuel: the speech.
1SA 25:24-44	Abigail hits the ground and delivers the best save in the book. She takes blame she doesn't owe, notes that her husband's name means fool, then reframes it: God is keeping you from bloodguilt, and one day when you're king you'll be glad there's no massacre on your record. Just remember me. David admits he was hours from killing every male there, takes the food and sends her home. Nabal is blind drunk, so she waits till morning. She tells him, he goes rigid, and ten days later he's dead.
1SA 26:1-25	The Ziphites snitch again and Saul brings three thousand men. This time David goes to him: he and Abishai stroll into the camp at night and find Saul asleep, spear stuck in the dirt by his head. Abishai offers one stroke, no follow-up. David says no, not touching the anointed king, and takes the spear and water jug as receipts. Then he stands on a hill and humiliates Abner for failing to guard his king. Saul says he's sinned and played the fool. They never meet again.
1SA 27:1-12	David figures Saul will get him eventually, so he defects: into Philistine territory with six hundred men, in with Achish of Gath. Saul immediately stops looking. David asks for his own town rather than a room in the capital, because he doesn't want his movements watched, and gets Ziklag. Then sixteen months of two sets of books: he raids desert peoples, leaves zero survivors, and tells Achish he's hitting southern Judah, his own people. Achish concludes he's got a servant for life. Wrong.
1SA 28:1-25	Achish tells David he's marching against Israel. Awkward. Saul banned every medium, and now he's staring at the Philistine army in a panic. God won't answer: no dreams, no Urim, no prophets. His staff find one at Endor. He goes in disguise and swears he won't prosecute her under his own law. She calls up Samuel, clocks her client, screams. Samuel has nothing new: the kingdom went to David, and tomorrow you and your sons join me. Then the woman he outlawed cooks him dinner.
1SA 29:1-11	David is marching at the back of the Philistine column, the most awkward position going. The commanders take one look: why are the Hebrews here? He could flip mid-battle, and the fastest way to make up with Saul is our heads. Also, the song. Achish, who genuinely likes David, gives him a glowing reference and sends him home. David objects, asking why he can't fight the enemies of my lord the king, doing enormous work. Then he rides away from the battle that kills Saul.
1SA 30:1-15	David gets back to Ziklag: burned flat, every wife and kid gone, taken in an Amalekite raid. Nobody killed, at least. The men cry until they can't, then discuss stoning David. He asks God through the ephod: chase them? Go, you'll get everything back. Two hundred are too wrecked to cross the brook Besor. Then they find an Egyptian slave his master dumped when he got sick. Bread, water, figs, and he'll walk them to the camp. Next time on 1 Samuel: the party.
1SA 30:16-31	The Egyptian delivers: the Amalekites are sprawled out eating, drinking and dancing over their loot. Worst possible setup. David hits them dusk to next evening; only four hundred on camels get out. He gets it all back, nothing missing, plus the livestock. Then his tough guys say the brook two hundred get their families and nothing else. David shuts it down: the guy guarding the bags gets the same cut as the guy fighting. He makes it law, then mails plunder to elders all over Judah.
1SA 31:1-13	Israel breaks on Gilboa and the Philistines kill Jonathan, Abinadab and Malchishua. Saul, wounded, asks his armour bearer to finish him before the Philistines can. The man is too frightened, so Saul falls on his own sword, and the bearer follows. Saul, three sons, his bearer, one day. The Philistines take his head and armour and nail his body to a wall. Then the men of Jabesh Gilead, the town he saved in his first week as king, march all night to take his body down.
2SA 1:1-27	A man shows up at Ziklag in torn clothes with dirt on his head and reports the battle: Israel routed, Saul and Jonathan dead. Then he embellishes. He personally found Saul leaning on his spear, Saul asked him to finish it, so he did, and look, here is the crown. He has read the room catastrophically wrong. David mourns and fasts till evening, then asks why an Amalekite was not afraid to kill God's anointed, and has him killed. Then the lament, and it is genuinely beautiful.
2SA 2:1-32	David asks God where to go, gets Hebron, and Judah crowns him king. First move as king: a thank-you note to Jabesh Gilead for burying Saul, which is class and also politics. Meanwhile Abner sets up Saul's surviving son as rival king. Then the civil war opens at the pool of Gibeon with the grimmest thing ever called a game: twelve men each, let the young men play, and all twenty-four kill each other at once.
2SA 3:1-21	The civil war grinds on, David getting stronger, Saul's house fading. Six sons born in Hebron, including Amnon, Absalom and Adonijah, all names to remember. Then the whole north collapses over an argument about a woman. Ishbosheth accuses Abner of sleeping with Saul's concubine, and Abner erupts. Am I a dog? Fine, I will hand the whole kingdom to David myself. David's one condition is getting Michal back. Next time on 2 Samuel: Joab finds out.
2SA 3:22-39	Joab gets back to find his brother's killer wined, dined and let go. He tells David he's been played, which is cover for a decision he's already made. Then, without telling David, he brings Abner back from the well of Sirah, pulls him aside in the gateway, and stabs him. David goes into full damage control: curses Joab's family, walks behind the coffin, cries at the grave, refuses to eat until sundown. It works. Then the honest line: the sons of Zeruiah are stronger than me.
2SA 4:1-12	Abner's dead and Ishbosheth loses his nerve. Two of his own captains walk in at noon, kill him in his bed, take his head, and ride overnight to Hebron to present it like a gift basket. They have not been following the news. David reminds them he executed the last man who claimed to have killed a king. What follows is grisly. He buries the head in Abner's grave. Also mentioned casually: Jonathan had a son, Mephibosheth, crippled at five when his nurse dropped him. Keep that one.
2SA 5:1-25	The tribes come to Hebron: we're your own flesh, and you led even under Saul. They anoint him king. Thirty years old, forty year reign. He goes after Jerusalem and the Jebusites taunt that the blind and lame could hold him off. Ages badly. He takes Zion and builds. Hiram of Tyre sends cedar and craftsmen, reading the room. More wives, more kids, one called Solomon. Then the Philistines come twice, and both times he asks first: go, then don't, loop behind, wait for marching in the treetops.
2SA 6:1-23	Thirty thousand men, a new cart, a parade. Then the oxen stumble, Uzzah grabs the ark to steady it, and drops dead. David isn't humbled, he's angry, names the place Perez Uzzah, and parks the ark at Obed-Edom's three months. He retries on hearing Obed-Edom is getting blessed into the ground. Take two: a sacrifice every six paces, David dancing in a linen ephod. Michal tears into him for exposing himself to the help, he says he'll get less dignified still, and she never has a child.
2SA 7:1-29	David has peace and a cedar house. God is still in a tent, and it bugs him. He floats it to Nathan, who says go for it. That night God corrects Nathan: don't answer prophecy off the cuff. I never asked for cedar, I've been fine in a tent since Egypt. Then the flip: you build me a house? I'm building you one. Your son builds the temple, your throne lasts forever, and if he sins I'll discipline him without yanking my loyalty as with Saul.
2SA 8:1-18	The conquest ledger chapter, and some entries are brutal. Philistines subdued. Moab beaten, the captives laid on the ground and measured with a rope, two lengths executed for every one spared, in the flattest voice imaginable. Hadadezer of Zobah loses seventeen hundred horsemen and twenty thousand infantry, chariot horses hamstrung but for a hundred. Damascus sends help and loses twenty-two thousand men. Toi of Hamath sends congratulations in silver and gold, dedicated to God. Eighteen thousand more in the Valley of Salt. Then the cabinet list.
2SA 9:1-13	David asks if anyone from Saul's family is alive, so he can be good to them for Jonathan's sake, which is a dangerous question to be the answer to, because new kings usually ask for the opposite reason. Ziba coughs it up: Jonathan has a son, lame in both feet, hiding in a place literally called Lo Debar. Mephibosheth arrives face down, calling himself a dead dog. Instead he gets Saul's land back, a permanent seat at the king's table, and Ziba's household assigned to farm it. Remember him too.
2SA 10:1-19	The Ammonite king dies and David sends envoys to say sorry for your loss. Hanun's advisers convince him the sympathy card is a spy mission, so he shaves off half of each envoy's beard and cuts their clothes off at the backside. David tells them to sit tight in Jericho until the beards grow back. Then Ammon hires thirty-three thousand mercenaries. Joab finds enemies in front and behind, splits the army, takes the hard side, and they agree to bail each other out.
2SA 11:1-27	The chapter opens with the line about the season when kings go out to war, and then: but David stayed in Jerusalem. Everything else comes from that. He sees Bathsheba from the roof, sends for her, and she gets pregnant. Then the cover-up, which is the ugly part. He recalls Uriah and tells him three times to go home to his wife. Uriah refuses every time, because the army is sleeping in a field. David sends him back carrying his own death warrant.
2SA 12:1-16	Nathan does not accuse, he runs a court case. Rich man with huge flocks, poor man with one pet lamb that ate off his plate and slept in his arms, and the rich man takes the lamb to feed a visitor. David, career judge, explodes: that man deserves to die. Nathan: you are the man. Sentence, the sword never leaves your house, and what you did behind closed doors gets done to you in daylight. Next time on 2 Samuel: day seven.
