2SA 12:17-31	A week on the ground, no food, nobody able to move him. Then the child dies and his own servants are too scared to say it. He clocks them whispering, asks straight out, then washes, changes, goes and worships, and asks for dinner. When they ask what that was about, he gives the clearest answer anyone gives in this book: while there was a chance I begged, now there is not, I will go to him instead. Then Solomon is born, and God loves him.
2SA 13:1-18	The sword-in-your-house promise starts collecting straight away, inside the family. Amnon fixates on his half-sister Tamar and makes himself sick over it. His cousin Jonadab hands him the plan: fake an illness, ask your father to send Tamar to cook for you. David does it without asking a single question. Amnon clears the house and grabs her. She says it plainly: this is not done in Israel. He does not listen, and he rapes her. Next time on 2 Samuel: her brother.
2SA 13:19-39	Tamar tears her robe, puts ashes on her head and goes down the street crying, the only voice anybody raises about this. Absalom tells her to keep it quiet, and she lives on in his house, desolate. David hears and gets very angry, and that is the whole of what David does, and that failure drives everything after. Absalom says nothing to Amnon for two solid years. Then a sheep shearing, all the princes invited, and Amnon killed drunk.
2SA 14:1-17	Joab can see David is aching for Absalom, so he hires a professional. A wise woman from Tekoa turns up in mourning with a case that sounds routine: two sons, a fight in a field, one dead, and now the family wants the survivor executed, wiping out her last heir. David rules for her twice and swears a royal oath. Then she springs the trap: why one rule for my son and another for your banished one? Next time on 2 Samuel: David spots the ghostwriter.
2SA 14:18-33	David works out in one question that Joab put her up to it, and she admits it while somehow still flattering him. Absalom gets brought home from Geshur and then frozen out: back in Jerusalem, banned from the king's presence, for another two years. Here is where the narrator drops in that Absalom was the best looking man in Israel and cut his hair once a year because it weighed about five pounds. When Joab ignores two summonses, Absalom sets his barley field on fire.
2SA 15:1-19	Absalom runs the slickest campaign in the Bible. Chariot, horses, fifty men running ahead, and every morning he plants himself at the city gate and intercepts anyone coming to see the king. Your case is completely valid, shame nobody has been appointed to hear it, if only I were judge. Two years of that and he owns the country. The coup launches under cover of a religious vow at Hebron. David evacuates instantly, to spare Jerusalem a bloodbath. Next time on 2 Samuel: Ittai says no.
2SA 15:20-37	Ittai the Gittite, a foreigner who basically just got here, gives the single most loyal speech in the book: wherever you are, alive or dead, that is where I will be. The whole country cries as they cross the Kidron. Then David starts making sharp calls. Send the ark back, because he will not use it as a lucky charm, and leave the priests in the city as a spy network. He hears Ahithophel has defected and prays one line: make his advice stupid. Then Hushai turns up.
2SA 16:1-23	Two men meet David on the road out. Ziba shows up with donkeys, bread and wine and a story that Mephibosheth stayed behind hoping to get the crown back, and David hands him the whole estate on his word alone. That is going to look bad later. Then Shimei, one of Saul's relatives, walks alongside throwing rocks and calling David a murderer. Abishai offers to remove his head. David says no: my own son wants me dead.
2SA 17:1-29	Ahithophel's plan is right and would have ended it: twelve thousand men, tonight, hit David while he is wrecked, kill only him, bring everyone home. Absalom loves it, then asks Hushai for a second opinion, which is the mistake. Hushai talks up David as a war veteran hiding in a pit and suggests gathering all Israel with Absalom leading, which sounds amazing and burns the one thing David needs: time. Absalom picks the worse plan. Ahithophel goes home, tidies his affairs, and hangs himself.
2SA 18:1-17	David splits the army three ways under Joab, Abishai and Ittai and wants to march himself, and the troops refuse. He gives one order out loud where everyone can hear it, and it is about his son: go gentle with Absalom. Then the forest of Ephraim, twenty thousand dead, and the line that the forest killed more men that day than the sword did. Absalom gets his head jammed in an oak. Next time on 2 Samuel: someone has to tell his father.
2SA 18:18-33	The chapter opens by mentioning the monument Absalom built himself because he had no son to carry his name, placed right after they buried him under a heap of rocks. Then the scene about not wanting to be the messenger. Ahimaaz volunteers and Joab says no, not you, not today. He sends a Cushite. Ahimaaz insists anyway, gets there first, and then says he saw a lot of commotion and could not say. And then it is one line, over and over: my son Absalom, my son.
2SA 19:1-22	The army wins the war and then has to creep into the city like they lost, because the king is upstairs sobbing over the man who tried to kill him. Joab goes in and lets him have it: you have humiliated every man who saved your family, and if you do not get out there tonight you will wake up alone. Harsh, and completely right. David sits in the gate. Next time on 2 Samuel: two more men on the road.
2SA 19:23-43	Mephibosheth turns up not having washed, shaved or changed his clothes since the day David left, which is an alibi you can smell, and says Ziba lied about him. David, who handed Ziba the whole estate on Ziba's word alone, splits the land instead of admitting he got played, and Mephibosheth says let him have the lot, you are home. Then Barzillai, eighty, turns down a place at court: I cannot taste my food or hear the singers.
2SA 20:1-26	The reunited kingdom lasts roughly one chapter. Sheba son of Bichri blows a trumpet, announces the north has no stake in David, and walks off with all Israel while Judah stays. Back home, David puts the ten concubines Absalom violated under guard, fed and housed and shut away for life, and the text says nothing more about it. Amasa, freshly promoted over Joab, misses his very first deadline. So Joab handles it personally: a friendly greeting, a hand on the beard, a sword.
2SA 21:1-22	Three years of famine, and the answer comes back that it is Saul's old massacre of the Gibeonites, people Israel had sworn to protect. The Gibeonites do not want money. They want seven of Saul's descendants. David hands them over, keeping Mephibosheth back because of his oath to Jonathan. And then the part that matters: Rizpah, mother of two of them, sits on the rock for months keeping birds and animals off the bodies, alone, until the rains come.
2SA 22:1-51	David's big thank-you song, and the special effects budget is enormous. He calls for help and God does not just reply, he arrives: earth shaking, smoke out of his nostrils, darkness under his feet, riding a cherub, firing arrows and lightning, and blowing so hard the bottom of the sea is exposed. All that to pull one man out of deep water. Then a middle stretch about his own clean hands, which reads a bit rich after the last ten chapters.
2SA 23:1-39	David's last words, and they are about governance: rule justly and you are like sunrise after rain, while the wicked are thorns nobody can pick up barehanded. Then the veterans list, which is where this book keeps its heart. One man killed eight hundred in one go. Eleazar fought until his hand cramped shut around the sword. Three punched through the Philistine line to get their homesick king a drink, and he poured it on the ground. The roll ends with Uriah the Hittite. Deliberately.
2SA 24:1-25	David decides to run a census, which sounds like paperwork and gets treated like a disaster. Even Joab, not a squeamish man, asks him why he wants this. Nine months and twenty days later: one million three hundred thousand swordsmen, and David's conscience kicks in immediately. Gad shows up with a menu: famine, flight, or plague. David picks God's hand, on the grounds that God's mercy is bigger. Seventy thousand die anyway. He then refuses a free threshing floor: an offering that costs nothing is not one.
ACT 1:1-26	Recap, dedication, and a question that shows they've absorbed nothing: so are you restoring the kingdom now? Hard no on dates, big yes on geography. Then he goes up, and two guys in white have to say that gawking at the sky is not the job. Judas's replacement gets picked by drawing lots.
ACT 2:1-24	Wind, fire, languages. Luke wants the audience noticed: devout Jews from everywhere, each hearing his own dialect. Two reactions get recorded: bafflement, and "they're drunk." Peter handles the drunk one first. It's nine in the morning. Then it's all Joel until the last two lines: the man you killed, God raised. Next time on Acts: an argument about a tomb.
ACT 2:25-47	The heart of the sermon is close reading. David wrote about not being left in the grave. David is still in his tomb, just up the road. So he wasn't talking about himself. Then the blunt part: the guy you crucified is Lord and Christ. Repent, get baptized. Three thousand in one day. Then meals, prayer, and selling their stuff for each other.
ACT 3:1-26	A man who gets carried to the same gate every day asks for cash, is told there isn't any, and gets something else entirely. Luke keeps the detail: the ankle bones, the standing up, a grown man leaping into the temple. The crowd stares at Peter and Peter kills that instantly. His speech is brutal about the trial, then calls it ignorance.
ACT 4:1-37	Arrested for preaching resurrection, the one doctrine the Sadducees can't tolerate. A night in the cells, and the believer count climbs to five thousand anyway. Asked who authorised them, Peter names the name and tacks on "whom you crucified." The council's problem isn't theology, it's logistics: the healed man is standing right there. So they warn instead of sentencing.
ACT 5:1-21	Two people sell property, quietly keep part of the money, and hand over the rest as if it were all of it. Nobody made them sell it, so the lie was pointless as well as dishonest. Ananias drops dead. Sapphira walks in three hours later, gets one chance to answer differently, and doesn't take it. Next time on Acts: an empty cell.
ACT 5:22-42	The best scene here is pure paperwork. The officers report the prison locked, guards at their posts, nobody inside. Then someone walks in to say the prisoners are back in the temple teaching. They get collected politely, because the crowd is a stoning risk. Gamaliel says wait and see. The council takes his advice and flogs them anyway.
ACT 6:1-15	The first internal fight in the church is about food, and it runs along a language line: the Greek-speaking widows keep getting missed. The twelve delegate. Seven get appointed, all with Greek names, so the complainants got the department. Stephen, hired to sort out meals, immediately starts out-arguing several synagogues at once. The response is coached witnesses.
ACT 7:1-31	Asked a yes or no question, Stephen launches into the longest speech in Acts, and for ages it's just history. Abraham, given a promise and not one square foot of land. Joseph, sold by his brothers, runs Egypt. Then the bit he keeps circling: Moses kills an Egyptian and his own people ask who put him in charge. Next time on Acts: the burning bush.
ACT 7:32-60	The history now has a point, and it's the same sentence twice: the man God sent got refused. Moses, refused, then made ruler and rescuer. The people, handed living words, built a calf. Then a quotation arguing God was never housed in a building, which is the exact charge he was arrested for. They cover their ears and pick up stones.
ACT 8:1-40	The persecution scatters the church, and Luke frames that as the actual mechanism for spreading it: everyone who runs preaches on the way. Samaria goes brilliantly. The awkward part is Simon, a professional miracle man who believes, gets baptized, then tries to buy the franchise. Then a senior Ethiopian official reading Isaiah aloud and admitting he has no idea who it's about.
ECC 1:1-18	Everything is vapour, and here is the proof. Sun comes up, rushes back to where it started, does it again. Wind loops around and returns. Rivers dump into a sea that never fills. Nothing is new and nobody is going to remember any of it. Then his resume: wisest king Jerusalem ever had, studied folly too, conclusion is that knowing more just hurts more.
ECC 2:1-26	Fully funded research project, sample size one. Laughter accomplishes nothing. Wine, no. Then construction on an imperial scale: houses, vineyards, gardens, reservoirs, herds, silver, gold. He denies himself nothing and becomes the biggest deal in Jerusalem's history, then files the lot under wind. What breaks him is inheritance. Somebody else gets all of it, and that somebody might be an idiot.
ECC 3:1-22	The famous poem, fourteen matched pairs, quoted at every funeral and much stranger in context. His commentary is the good part: God stuck eternity in the human heart, so now you want the whole blueprint while built to see one page. Enjoy it, eat, drink. Then he checks the courts and finds corruption sitting where justice should be.
ECC 4:1-16	He looks at oppression, and the phrase he cannot shake is "they had no comforter," said twice in one verse. Conclusion: dead people have it better, never-born people better still. Then he clocks that achievement runs on envy of your neighbour. And then the warmest passage in the book. Two beats one, somebody to pick you up, a three-strand cord that will not snap.
ECC 5:1-20	Rules for religion, and they boil down to talk less. Go to listen. Keep it short, on the strictly geographical grounds that God is in heaven and you are not. On corruption: every official has a bigger official watching him. Then the money. Whoever loves silver never has enough, and the working guy sleeps great while the rich guy stares at the ceiling.
ECC 6:1-12	The nightmare here is not being poor, it is owning something you cannot use. Riches, honour, every desire met, and then no capacity to enjoy any of it while a stranger eats it. The Preacher calls that an evil disease. Then he escalates. A hundred kids, long life, zero satisfaction, no grave, and a stillborn child comes out ahead because at least it got rest.
ECC 7:1-29	A chapter of deliberately unwelcome preferences. Good reputation beats perfume, the day you die beats the day you were born, and a funeral beats a party, because the funeral is where the useful info is. Nostalgia is banned outright. Then the weirdest advice in the book, delivered totally straight: do not be excessively righteous, and also do not be excessively wicked.
ECC 8:1-17	Court survival tips from a guy who clearly worked near power. Keep the command, do not storm out of the room, and nobody gets to ask the king what he thinks he is doing. Under that sits the real subject: consequences do not show up on time, so people just go ahead. He does not resolve it. Eat, drink, be glad.
ECC 9:1-18	The great levelling chapter. Same ending for everybody, good, clean, unclean. A living dog beats a dead lion, because at least the living know what is coming. And from that he draws a list of pleasures instead of a warning: bread, wine, white clothes, oil on your head, the wife you love. Sheol has no projects.
ECC 10:1-20	Observations, mostly about incompetence in high places. Dead flies wrecking the perfumer's oil is the template: a little folly outweighs a lot of wisdom. The fool announces himself just by walking down the street. Then workplace injuries, pits, walls, quarried rock, and a snake that bites before the charmer has started. Do not curse the king, even in your head. A bird might be listening.
ECC 11:1-10	The Preacher's guide to acting when you do not know anything. Throw your bread on the water. Split it across seven ventures, make it eight. Anybody standing around watching the wind will not sow. Nobody knows how bones grow inside a womb, so sow morning and evening. Enjoy your youth, kid, then he mentions the judgment on his way out.
ECC 12:1-14	The book ends on the best description of getting old anybody has written, and it works by never naming what it is describing. Keepers of the house tremble, strong men bow, grinders quit, window-lookers go dark, a bird chirps and you are awake, a grasshopper is too heavy. Then the breakages: silver cord cut, golden bowl smashed, pitcher broken at the spring.
ISA 1:1-31	God calls heaven and earth as witnesses, then says the ox has better recall than Israel. Next: stop bringing sacrifices, I hate them, your hands are covered in blood. Wash up, do justice, look after widows, and scarlet sins come out snow white. Jerusalem gets called a prostitute watering down the wine, and God says fine, into the furnace.
ISA 2:1-22	Vision one: God's mountain on top of every other hill, nations lining up for lessons, swords hammered into plows, nobody training for war again. Vision two, same chapter, opposite vibe: the country is stuffed with silver, imported horses and gods people made with their fingers. Judgment day comes for everything tall, and everyone hides in caves and chucks their gold idols to the bats.
ISA 3:1-26	God pulls every prop out from under Jerusalem: judges, prophets, elders, even the fortune tellers. Things get so bad that owning a coat makes you a candidate, and the nominee says no thanks. Elders go on trial for grinding the faces of the poor. Zion's fashionable women lose the whole wardrobe, everything swapped for its opposite: rope belt, shaved head, stink for perfume.
ISA 4:1-6	Opens grim: so few men left that seven women share one, offering to buy their own food and clothes just to borrow his name. Then it flips. Whoever's left in Zion gets called holy, the filth washed off by a spirit of justice and fire, and God installs weather over Mount Zion, smoke by day, flames by night, sunshade and rain shelter in one.
ISA 5:1-30	Isaiah starts a nice love song about his friend's vineyard and it is a setup. Premium vines, watchtower, winepress, garbage grapes. Down comes the fence, and the clouds get told to stop raining. He wanted justice, he got bloodshed. Six woes: land grabbers, breakfast drinkers, people who call evil good, know-it-alls, judges who'll acquit for cash. Then he whistles and a tireless army shows up.
ISA 6:1-13	Uzziah dies and Isaiah sees the actual throne, robe big enough to fill the temple, seraphim yelling holy holy holy loud enough to rattle the doorframes. Isaiah: I'm done for, my lips are filthy. Fix: a burning coal pressed to his mouth. Then he volunteers before hearing the gig: preach to people guaranteed not to get it. How long? Until the place is empty.
ISA 7:1-25	Syria and Israel come for Jerusalem and Ahaz starts shaking like a tree. Isaiah shows up with his kid and says relax, those two kings are burnt-out torch stubs. Pick any sign you want. Ahaz goes all humble, so God picks one: a young woman has a son named Immanuel, and before the kid knows right from wrong those two kingdoms are toast.
ISA 8:1-22	Isaiah writes Maher Shalal Hash Baz on a giant tablet with witnesses watching, then names his newborn the same thing. Before the kid can say mama, Assyria loots Damascus and Samaria. You turned down the gentle stream of Shiloah, so here's the whole River, neck deep. Also stop yelling conspiracy with everyone else. The mediums chirping and muttering are not a source.
ISA 9:1-21	Lights on for Zebulun and Naphtali: darkness gone, yoke snapped, bloody army boots on the fire. A kid is born and racks up titles, Wonderful, Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace. Then straight back to bad news up north, where Samaria loses bricks and brags it'll rebuild in cut stone. Syria and Philistia bite Israel from both sides. Hand's still out.
ISA 10:1-34	First a woe for lawmakers who write the fine print specifically to rob widows. Then the twist: Assyria is God's stick and doesn't know it, with a king bragging he scooped up the earth like eggs from an unguarded nest. God's answer: does the axe brag about the guy swinging it? Assyria's forest burns to a remnant a toddler could count.
ISA 11:1-16	A shoot pops out of Jesse's dead stump, loaded with wisdom, counsel, might and knowledge, and it doesn't judge on looks or hearsay. It defends the poor and drops the wicked with a breath. Then the animal kingdom loses its mind: wolf naps with lamb, lion eats straw, and a toddler is in charge, hanging out at a cobra hole.
ISA 12:1-6	Six whole verses of good mood, which around here is basically a vacation. You were mad at me, now you're not, thanks, and God is his strength and his soundtrack, so fear is off the table. Draw water from the wells of salvation. Then it gets loud: tell every nation what he did, sing, yell. The Holy One of Israel lives here now.
ISA 13:1-22	Babylon's number comes up. God calls up an army and the day of Yahweh shows up with the sun, moon and stars switched off. People will get rarer than Ophir gold. The sack is told straight: infants killed in front of their parents, homes looted, wives raped. The Medes come and cannot be bought. The glory of kingdoms ends up jackal country.
ISA 14:1-32	God brings Jacob home and the prisoners end up running the place. Israel sings a very satisfied funeral song over Babylon's king. Even the trees are relieved nobody's showing up with an axe. Sheol wakes and the dead kings ask, oh, you're weak now too? Maggots for a mattress. He said he'd park his throne above the stars. He ends up unburied, stepped on.
ISA 15:1-9	Moab gets wiped out in one night, Ar and Kir gone before sunrise. What follows is a map of grief: every head shaved, every beard gone, sackcloth on the roofs, the noise carrying from Heshbon to Jahaz. The prophet says his own heart is breaking for Moab. The water at Dimon runs red, and a lion waits for anyone who made it out.
ISA 16:1-14	Step one, send tribute lambs to Zion. Step two, Judah, be shade at noon, hide the refugees, don't rat out the fugitive. Step three, the catch: Moab is proud and the bragging is worth nothing. So Moab cries for Moab, and the thing mourned is the raisin cakes of Kir Hareseth. Then a sad bit about the vineyards, nobody stomping grapes. Three years, contract terms.
ISA 17:1-14	Damascus: no longer a city, now a pile. Ephraim's fortress goes too, and Israel gets thinned down to two or three olives stuck at the top of the tree. Then people finally look at their Maker instead of stuff their own fingers built. They imported fancy seedlings, forced them to bloom overnight, and the harvest walked off. Nations roar like surf, and blow away.
ISA 18:1-7	Shout out to the land of rustling wings past the rivers of Ethiopia, doing diplomacy in papyrus boats, sending runners to a people the text twice calls tall and smooth and terrifying since forever. Watch for the flag, listen for the trumpet. And God's move is: nothing, for now. Then the pruning hooks come out right when the flower turns into a grape.
ISA 19:1-25	God rolls into Egypt on a fast cloud and the idols start shaking. Egypt turns on itself, then goes looking for answers from wizards. The Nile dries up and the whole economy goes with it, fishermen to weavers. Pharaoh's genius advisers get called flat-out stupid, staggering in their own vomit. Then out of nowhere: an altar to Yahweh in Egypt and a highway to Assyria.
ISA 20:1-6	Assyria takes Ashdod, and God's assignment for Isaiah is: lose the sackcloth, lose the sandals. So he walks naked and barefoot for three years. The point: that's exactly how Assyria will march Egyptian and Ethiopian prisoners out, bare backsides on display. Message for everyone in Judah betting on Egypt. The coast people ask the obvious follow-up: if that's our backup plan, we're finished, right?
ISA 21:1-17	A vision blows in like a desert storm and wrecks the prophet: labor pains, can't hear, can't see. Somebody's setting the dinner table, then: princes, up, grease the shields. A watchman waits until he spots horsemen two by two: fallen, fallen is Babylon. Then Dumah, where a guy keeps asking how the night's going and gets told morning's coming, and so is night.
ISA 22:1-25	Whole city up on the roofs partying, its leaders caught running away. Then the crisis response, genuinely excellent engineering: count the breaches, knock down houses for stone, build a reservoir. Not one glance at the God who set this up. Told to mourn, they throw a barbecue: eat, drink, tomorrow we die. Shebna gets busted carving himself a fancy rock tomb.
ISA 23:1-18	Tyre goes down and the ships of Tarshish have nowhere to dock. Sidon gets shamed, and the sea pipes up to say it never had kids. Tyre was the mall, and God says that's the point: I planned this to wreck the pride of everything glorious. Seventy years forgotten, then back to business, a prostitute walking the streets with a harp.
ISA 24:1-23	God flips the whole earth over, and it hits everybody the same: priest, peasant, boss, servant, lender, borrower. The land got trashed because people broke the everlasting covenant, so the curse eats it. Wine mourns, harps stop, booze tastes bitter. Run from the noise and you fall in the pit, climb out and you hit the snare. The earth staggers like a drunk.
ISA 25:1-12	A thank-you song for turning a fortress into rubble that never gets rebuilt. God's been a fort for poor people, and he shuts the bullies up like a cloud killing the glare. Then the party: every nation on this mountain, death swallowed, every tear wiped away. Then Moab gets stomped into a manure pit, flailing like it's trying to swim.
ISA 26:1-21	Judah gets a victory song: strong city, walls made of salvation. Keep your head steady and you get total peace, because God is bedrock. The proud city is dust now, and the ones stomping on it are the poor. The wicked get shown kindness and learn nothing. Then Israel admits: we were in labor and gave birth to wind. Answer: the dead will live.
ISA 27:1-13	God brings out the big sword for leviathan, plus the sea dragon. Then the chapter 5 vineyard gets a do-over with God as the gardener: watering constantly, guarding it around the clock, saying look, I'm not even angry. Forgiveness comes with homework: smash your own altar stones into chalk dust. Then the big trumpet, and exiles come home one at a time.
ISA 28:1-29	The drunks of Ephraim wear a crown of wilting flowers. Priests and prophets are hammered on the job and every table has vomit on it. They'd mocked Isaiah's preaching as baby talk, blah blah, line on line, so God says fine, that's exactly how you'll get it. Jerusalem's leaders brag they cut a deal with Death. God's counteroffer is a cornerstone.
ISA 29:1-24	Ariel gets surrounded until it's whispering out of the dirt like a ghost. Attackers of Zion are like a starving guy who dreams a huge meal and wakes up hungry. The sealed book bit is great: hand it to the educated guy, he says can't, it's sealed. Hand it to the uneducated guy, he says can't read.
ISA 30:1-18	Woe to kids who make big plans and never ask. They trot down to Egypt through lion and snake country. God's nickname for Egypt: Rahab Who Sits Still. These people tell their prophets to prophesy something nice. Their guilt is a bulge in a tall wall that shatters into pieces too small to scoop coals. They picked fast horses, so now the chasers are faster.
ISA 30:19-33	Zion stops crying, and God answers the second the shout goes up. The teachers stop hiding and there's a voice behind you at every turn going: this way, walk here. The idols get their gold plating peeled off and chucked out like trash. Then God shows up in smoke, and every whack of the rod on Assyria lands on the beat, tambourines and harps.
ISA 31:1-9	Woe to everyone running to Egypt, counting chariots and never once looking at the Holy One of Israel. Egyptians are men, not God. Their horses are meat. God flexes one hand and the bodyguard lands on the guy he was guarding. Two pictures for God over Zion: a lion that won't leave its kill, and a bird over the nest.
ISA 32:1-20	Picture a king who actually rules right and officials who actually do justice. The guy is basically a windbreak, cold water in a desert, shade from a big rock. Stutterers speak clean, and fools stop getting called noble. Then Isaiah rounds on the comfortable ladies of Zion: no harvest, so strip down and cry. Thorns eat the party houses.
ISA 33:1-24	Hey destroyer who's never been destroyed, hey backstabber nobody's stabbed yet: your turn's coming, it's just scheduling. Right now the roads are empty, the treaty's shredded, and the peace negotiators are openly crying. Who survives endless burning? The guy who won't take a bribe. Zion's loot gets carried off by people with limps.
ISA 34:1-17	Everybody get in here and listen. God is done with the nations: corpses stacked and stinking, mountains melting with blood, the sky rolled up like a scroll. The sword, described as drunk, comes down on Edom, staged as a sacrifice. Then the new tenants move in: owl, raven, jackals, ostriches. Every one is listed in God's book, with its mate.
ISA 35:1-10	Desert wakes up happy and blooms like a rose, handed Lebanon's glory as a housewarming gift. Shore up the weak hands, brace the wobbly knees, tell the scared people God's coming with payback. Blind eyes open, deaf ears pop, the lame guy jumps like a deer. The rescued come home to Zion singing.
ISA 37:1-21	Hezekiah rips his clothes and heads for the temple with the grimmest possible metaphor: the baby's coming and there's no strength left to push. Isaiah's answer is short: don't be scared of a speech, he'll go home and die there. Hezekiah's move is completely literal: he spreads the letter open in front of God. Next time on Isaiah: the reply comes in bodies.
ISA 38:1-22	Isaiah walks in with: get your affairs in order, you're dying. Hezekiah rolls over to face the wall, reminds God of his excellent track record, and sobs. It works. Fifteen bonus years, plus the sundial shadow rewinds ten steps. Then his diary of the illness: life snipped off the loom, voice down to bird noises. Also the actual treatment was a fig cake.
ISA 39:1-8	Babylon sends Hezekiah a get well card and a gift, and he's so pleased he gives them the full VIP tour. Treasury, armory, everything. Isaiah shows up asking questions he already knows the answers to. All of it, says Hezekiah. Great, says Isaiah, all of it's going to Babylon, plus your sons, who'll be eunuchs there. Hezekiah: at least not while I'm alive.
ISA 41:1-29	Court's in session, islands, sit down. Who raised up the conqueror from the east? Spoiler: me. The nations react to panic by building an idol together: carpenter hyping up the goldsmith, then nailing the god down so it won't tip over. Israel gets called worm Jacob, then upgraded to a threshing sledge. Then God dares the idols to predict anything. Silence.
ISA 42:1-25	Here's my servant, defined by what he doesn't do: no yelling in the streets, doesn't snap an already-cracked reed, doesn't blow out a wick that's barely lit. Just quietly gets justice done. Then God, who admits he's been keeping quiet for ages, compares himself to a woman in labor, gasping. Then the gut punch: who's blinder than my own servant?
ISA 44:1-28	Don't be scared, Jacob. Water on dry ground, Spirit on your kids. Then the best takedown in the book, a factory tour. The blacksmith bangs away at his god and gets lightheaded. The wood guy burns half a tree for heat, roasts dinner, goes ahh, toasty, then carves the leftover chunk into a god and begs it to save him.
ISA 45:1-25	God calls Cyrus his anointed and smashes bronze doors open ahead of him. Twice it says Cyrus has no idea who's doing this. Then a line nobody quotes at weddings: I make the light and I make the dark, I make peace and I make disaster. Ends huge: everybody on earth, look at me and get saved.
ISA 46:1-13	Bel and Nebo have a moving-day problem: they're statues, so they need donkeys, and they get hauled into captivity like furniture. Compare: God's been carrying Israel since the womb and will keep going until their hair goes gray. The bought god sits exactly where it was set down, unable to move an inch. And there's a bird of prey coming from the east, already booked.
ISA 47:1-15	Babylon gets addressed like a spoiled princess and told to get in the dirt, no throne, grab the millstones, hike up your skirt and wade the river. Empire to day laborer. The charge sheet: I handed you my people and you showed zero mercy, even yoking the old ones. Her stargazers are invited to save her. They're kindling.
ISA 48:1-22	Here's why I told you everything in advance: you'd have credited your idol. Stiff-necked, iron sinew, brass forehead, crooked since birth. I'm holding back my anger for my own reputation, not because you earned it. If you'd just listened, your peace would've run like a river. No sugar at the end: no peace for the wicked.
ISA 49:1-26	The servant opens with two weapon lines, mouth like a sharp sword, himself a polished arrow in the quiver, then immediately gripes: I worked for nothing. God's response is to make the job bigger, so now he's a light to every nation. Zion says God forgot her, and God says: can a nursing mother forget her baby? Also, I carved your name into my palms.
ISA 50:1-11	God starts with paperwork: show me the divorce papers, name the creditor I supposedly sold you to. There aren't any. You sold yourselves. Then the servant speaks, quiet and hard: I gave my back to the guys hitting me and my cheeks to the guys ripping out my beard, and I didn't dodge the spit. Face set like flint.
ISA 51:1-23	Look at the rock you got cut from. Look at Abraham and Sarah, one guy and one woman, and now here you all are. Then the prophet yells at God's arm: wake up, you chopped Rahab into pieces. Then the cup gets taken off Jerusalem and handed to the people who told her to lie down so they could walk on her back.
ISA 52:1-15	Wake up, get dressed, shake off the dirt, and untie the rope on your own neck. You were sold for zero and you're getting bought back for zero. God's beef is reputational: my name gets trashed all day. This exodus isn't a panic sprint like the last one: nobody rushes, God walks in front and covers the back.
ISA 53:1-12	Who believed any of this? He came up like a shoot in dry dirt, the kind of person people glance away from. And here's the turn: the sickness he was carrying was ours, and we assumed God had it in for him, and we were wrong. He never argued. Silent like a lamb going to slaughter. Buried with criminals and a rich man.
ISA 54:1-17	Hey, you who never had kids: start singing, because you're going to have more of them than the married woman. Now go get a bigger tent, more rope, deeper stakes. God's version of an apology: I walked out for a second, and now I'm bringing you back with huge mercy. Closer: I invented the blacksmith, so no weapon he makes works.
ISA 55:1-13	Everybody thirsty, come get water. No money? Great, come buy wine and milk for free. And the killer question: why are you paying good cash for stuff that isn't even bread? Heads up though: my thoughts aren't your thoughts, and the gap is sky to dirt. My word works like rain, it doesn't bounce back empty.
ISA 56:1-12	Do justice, keep the Sabbath, salvation's almost here. Then two people who assumed they were out get pulled in personally. The foreigner who figures he'll be filtered out: no. The eunuch calling himself a dead tree: also no, and you get a name better than sons and daughters, which is exactly what you were mourning.
ISA 57:1-21	It opens on something bleak: good people die and nobody even notices, and the twist is that they're being spared what's coming. Then the charges, the ugliest in the book: worship under every green tree, children killed in the ravines, a shrine token hidden behind the door. The saddest detail: they exhausted themselves doing this and never once said, this isn't working.
ISA 58:1-14	The people file a complaint: we fasted, why didn't you notice? God's answer: because on your fast day you were doing business and cracking the whip on your workers. Here's the fast I actually wanted: cut the ropes, free the oppressed, hand out your bread, take the homeless poor into your house. Also, lose the pointing finger.
ISA 60:1-22	Get up, turn the lights on, your glory's here while everybody else is in thick darkness. Then the deliveries start: camel trains from Midian and Sheba hauling gold and frankincense, Kedar's flocks, ships from Tarshish, Lebanon's best timber. The gates never close because the shipments never stop. Upgrade list: gold for brass, silver for iron, and peace gets hired as governor.
ISA 61:1-11	The Spirit's on me and here's the job: good news for the humble, bandages for broken hearts, doors open for prisoners, the year of God's favor, plus a day of payback. And the best part is a trade-in program: a flower crown for your ashes, joy oil instead of mourning, a praise outfit instead of a heavy heart. Instead of shame, double.
ISA 62:1-12	I'm not shutting up about Zion, says the prophet, and neither should God get any peace until Jerusalem is famous for the right reasons. She gets a brand new name straight from God's mouth. Nobody calls her Forsaken or Desolate anymore. Now it's Hephzibah and Beulah, delighted-in and married. Sworn on his own right arm: the pickers drink the wine.
ISA 63:1-19	Somebody comes marching in from Edom in red clothes and the question is the obvious one: why are you covered in that? Answer: winepress. Alone. Nobody helped. Then the tone flips into a family history: he carried them the whole way, and then they rebelled and he turned into their enemy. It ends as an actual complaint: where's your zeal?
ISA 64:1-12	Just rip the sky open and come down. Shake the mountains. Set the brush on fire, let the nations shake when they see you. Then the confession, and it's brutal: we're all unclean, our best behavior is a filthy rag, we're fading like leaves. Nobody's even praying anymore. Are you really going to stay quiet through all this?
ISA 65:1-25	I got found by people who weren't even looking, says God, and stood here all day with my hands out for people who ignored me. Charge sheet: sacrificing in gardens, incense on bricks, sleeping in graveyards, eating pork, and telling everyone else to back off because you're holier. But like a guy who won't toss a grape cluster, I'm not wiping you all out.
ISA 66:1-24	Heaven's my throne and earth's my footstool, so what kind of house were you planning to build me exactly? The person I actually look at is poor, crushed and shaking at my word. And sacrifices from smug people rate the same as murder and pig's blood. Then the softest line in the entire book: I'll comfort you like a mother comforts her kid.
ISA 36:1-22	Sennacherib takes every fortified town in Judah, then sends his mouthpiece to finish the job with a speech where the whole wall can hear. Egypt's a cracked stick that goes right through your palm, and the God you're counting on is the one whose altars your own king tore down. Next time on Isaiah: the king takes it upstairs.
ISA 37:22-38	God's answer to the letter is a poem. Zion's a girl shaking her head at him. His own bragging gets read back: chopped Lebanon's cedars, gonna dry up Egypt with my feet. Reply: those cities you flattened were grass on a roof. Then 185,000 Assyrians dead by sunrise, and two of Sennacherib's sons kill him mid-prayer in his god's temple.
ISA 40:1-31	The whole book changes gear and the first word is comfort, twice. Tell Jerusalem it's over, the debt's paid double. Grade a highway through the desert. People are grass, they wilt, God's word doesn't. Then God shows up as a conquering warrior who also carries lambs in his coat. Even young athletes collapse, but people who wait get eagle wings.
ISA 43:1-28	Don't be scared, says the one who made you. Note the fine print: not no water and no fire, just that neither gets to finish you. Egypt, Ethiopia and Seba go on the tab as your ransom. The witnesses called at the trial are blind people with eyes. Then the complaint: I asked almost nothing and you brought me your sins.
ISA 59:1-21	First, rule out the excuses: God's arm isn't short and his ear works fine. It's the sins. Then the images: they hatch snake eggs, so eating one kills you and stepping on one hatches a viper, and they weave webs that will never be a shirt. The confession is honest: we feel along the wall like blind men, growl like bears, moan like doves.
JDG 1:1-18	Joshua's dead, so Israel asks God who bats first. Judah. Ten thousand die at Bezek, and Adoni-Bezek loses his thumbs and big toes, which he admits he did to seventy kings first. Jerusalem burns. Othniel takes Debir and wins Achsah, who immediately asks her dad for the water rights. Next time on Judges: all the places nobody can take.
JDG 1:19-36	Judah owns the hills, but the valley is off limits because those guys have iron chariots. Caleb clears Hebron and the three sons of Anak. Benjamin just lets the Jebusites stay. Joseph's crew gets into Bethel because a local rats out the entrance. Manasseh, Ephraim, Zebulun, Asher, Naphtali and Dan all flunk, and settle for forced labour.
JDG 2:1-23	God's angel shows up at Bochim: you cut deals with the locals instead of wrecking their altars, so now they stay as a permanent trap. Everyone cries. Joshua dies at a hundred and ten, and the next generation has no idea who God is. Then the loop: worship Baal, get raided, get a judge, get rescued, judge dies, do it worse.
JDG 3:1-31	The leftover nations are a pop quiz. Israel marries in, worships along, and gets sold to Cushan Rishathaim for eight years until Othniel bails them out. Then Eglon of Moab. Ehud, a lefty from Benjamin, straps a sword to his right thigh where nobody frisks, asks for a private word, and buries it in the very fat king. Shamgar: six hundred Philistines, one cattle prod.
JDG 4:1-24	Twenty years under Jabin of Hazor, whose general Sisera rolls nine hundred iron chariots. Deborah holds court under a palm tree and tells Barak to take ten thousand men up Mount Tabor. Barak says only if you come, so she says fine, a woman gets the credit. His army breaks, he runs, Jael gives him milk. Then, asleep, a tent peg through the skull.
JDG 5:1-31	Victory single, Deborah and Barak on vocals. God marching from Seir with the ground shaking, then the roll call. Ephraim, Benjamin, Machir, Zebulun, Issachar and Naphtali showed. Reuben stayed with the sheep, Dan hung around the boats, Asher chilled at the beach, and Meroz gets cursed for no-showing. Jael is most blessed of women, and Sisera's mum waits for a chariot that isn't coming.
JDG 6:1-21	Midian swarms in for seven years, eats the harvest, and Israel ends up living in caves. God sends a prophet whose whole message is: I got you out of Egypt and you didn't listen. Then an angel finds Gideon threshing wheat inside a winepress so nobody sees him and opens with "mighty man of valour." Next time on Judges: fire out of a rock.
JDG 6:22-40	Fire eats the offering, the angel vanishes, and Gideon panics until he's told he'll live. That night he and ten servants pull down dad's Baal altar, after dark, because he's scared. The town wants him dead; his father Joash says a real god can defend his own altar, and nicknames him Jerub-Baal. Then Gideon tests God twice with a fleece: wet, then dry.
JDG 7:1-12	Gideon musters an army and God's opening note is: too many, you'll take the credit. Anyone scared can leave. Twenty-two thousand leave. Still too many, so there's a drinking audition and the three hundred who lap like dogs make the cut. That night God says go scope out the camp, and adds "take Purah if you're scared." Next time on Judges: a dream about bread.
JDG 7:13-25	Gideon sneaks up on the camp and hears a guy describe a dream where a barley loaf flattens a tent, and his buddy goes "yeah, that's Gideon." So now he's sentient bread. Three hundred men, three squads, trumpets and torches in clay jars. Midnight: horns, smashing pottery, screaming. Midian panics, stabs itself and runs. Ephraim locks the crossings and hand delivers two heads.
JDG 8:1-17	Ephraim is furious, not about the war, about not getting invited. Gideon fixes it with shameless flattery. Then, running on empty, he asks Succoth for bread and they say call us when you've actually won. He jumps the last fifteen thousand at Karkor, bags Zebah and Zalmunna, then comes back through Succoth with thorns. Next time on Judges: why this was personal all along.
JDG 8:18-35	One question about Tabor and it's clear: those were his brothers. He tells his kid Jether to execute them, the kid can't, and the kings ask for someone who knows how. Israel offers him a dynasty, he gives a speech about God being the real king, then collects everyone's gold and builds an ephod Israel worships immediately. Plus a son by a Shechem concubine: Abimelech.
JDG 9:1-28	Abimelech sells Shechem on one boss beating seventy brothers, funds the takeover with temple cash, and kills all seventy on one rock. Jotham hides, lives, and climbs Gerizim to yell a fable: olive, fig and vine all say no thanks, we have jobs, so the crown goes to a thornbush that threatens to burn everything down. Next time on Judges: the fire, and a millstone.
JDG 9:29-57	Zebul runs a double game: cheering Gaal publicly, tipping off Abimelech at night. Gaal spots troops and Zebul tells him it's shadows. He loses the fight and is thrown out. Abimelech wipes out the city and salts the ground. A thousand burn in the tower. A woman at Thebez drops a millstone on his head, and his dying request is a favour to his reputation.
JDG 10:1-18	Two judges go by with basically no drama, which for this book is a miracle. Jair's highlight reel: thirty sons, thirty donkeys, thirty towns. Then Israel goes shopping for gods: Syria, Sidon, Moab, Ammon, Philistia. Eighteen years of getting stomped follow. They cry out and God claps back: go ask your new gods. They dump the idols and mean it, and God can't stay mad.
JDG 11:1-20	Jephthah: son of a prostitute, kicked out by his half-brothers, now running a crew of outlaws in Tob. Ammon attacks and the elders remember he exists. He gets the promise in front of God before he moves. He writes to Ammon's king, who says Israel stole his land, and fires back the whole exodus itinerary. Next time on Judges: the worst promise in the Bible.
JDG 11:21-40	Jephthah's last argument: you've had three hundred years to complain. Ammon ignores him. Then the Spirit comes on him and he vows to burn whatever comes out of his door to meet him. The one who runs out is his daughter, his only child. He says he cannot take it back. She asks for two months in the hills, comes back, and he does it.
JDG 12:1-15	Ephraim shows up after the fighting to complain about not being invited, and threatens to burn Jephthah's house down. Jephthah isn't the flattering type, so he fights them. Gilead grabs the river crossings and runs a pronunciation checkpoint: say Shibboleth. Anyone who says Sibboleth dies. Forty-two thousand men killed by one consonant. Then three judges get a paragraph and it's all numbers: sons, daughters, donkeys.
JDG 13:1-25	Forty years under Philistine rule. The rescue plan: an angel drops in on Manoah's wife in Zorah, who can't have kids. You're having a son, Nazirite from birth, no haircuts, and you're off wine and unclean food. Manoah prays for a second appointment, gets one, and hears the exact answer his wife already relayed. Then the angel rides the altar flame. Baby: Samson.
JDG 14:1-20	Samson spots a Philistine girl in Timnah. A lion jumps him and he rips it apart barehanded, then eats honey from the carcass, which for a Nazirite is a lot of corpse contact. He bets thirty guys thirty outfits on a riddle only he can solve. They lean on his wife, she cracks him, and he pays with thirty dead men in Ashkelon.
JDG 15:1-20	Samson strolls back at harvest with a goat like nothing happened. His wife has been given to his best man; here is her little sister instead. So: three hundred foxes, torches between the tails, harvest gone. The Philistines burn his wife and her father, and the comedy stops. Judah sends three thousand men to arrest its own champion.
JDG 16:1-16	Samson visits a prostitute in Gaza and the locals stake out the gate to kill him at dawn. He leaves at midnight with the gate. Doors, posts, bar, uphill toward Hebron. Then Delilah, and eleven hundred pieces per lord for the secret. Cords: snap. Ropes: snap. Hair woven into the loom: he rips out the pin. Next time on Judges: he tells her.
JDG 16:17-31	He caves: Nazirite from birth, never cut, shave it and he is just a guy. She has his locks shaved while he sleeps on her lap. He wakes ready to shrug it off and does not realise God has left. Blinded, chained, put on the prison mill. Then one line: his hair starts growing back. At Dagon's party he pushes the two centre pillars over.
JDG 17:1-13	Micah confesses to his mum that he took her eleven hundred pieces of silver. She says bless you, son, then dedicates the money to God by having idols made out of it. Only two hundred reach the silversmith. He ordains one of his sons, then hires a passing Levite at ten silver a year. His takeaway: God has to bless me now.
JDG 18:1-15	Dan still has not claimed the land it was given, so it goes shopping for someone else's. Five scouts stop at Micah's place, clock the Levite by his voice, and ask him to bless the trip. He says go in peace and asks nothing. Laish turns out to be calm, unfortified and allied with nobody. Next time on Judges: the priest gets headhunted.
JDG 18:16-31	Six hundred armed men at the gate, five guys inside loading up the idols. The priest asks what they are doing and gets offered a promotion: why be one man's chaplain when you can have a whole tribe? Micah says they took my gods and my priest and I have nothing left, and is told to shut up or his family dies.
JDG 19:1-16	No king in Israel, and here comes the worst story in the book. A Levite goes to Bethlehem for the concubine who walked out on him. Her father will not let him leave: one more night, one more meal, five days of it. He finally goes, too late, and skips Jebus because it is a foreign city. Next time on Judges: the night in Gibeah.
JDG 19:17-30	The old man takes them in and feeds them. Then men of the town surround the house and demand the guest. The host offers his own daughter and the concubine instead. The Levite ends it by putting her out the door, and they rape her all night. She reaches the doorstep and collapses. In the morning he tells her to get up. No answer.
JDG 20:1-25	The Levite tells his version, in which the mob was after him and the part where he handed her over does not come up. Benjamin refuses to surrender the guilty men, and a murder case becomes a civil war. God is asked only who goes first. Day one: twenty-two thousand dead. Day two: eighteen thousand. Next time on Judges: day three.
JDG 20:26-48	This time they fast, sacrifice, and ask the real question, and God says go, tomorrow it is over. The plan is the old Ai trick: fake the retreat, then torch the empty town. Benjamin is still bragging that it is going like the last two days when they look back and see their city burning. Twenty-five thousand dead, six hundred escape to Rimmon.
JDG 21:1-25	Israel nearly wipes out a tribe, then sits at Bethel crying that a tribe is nearly wiped out. They had sworn not to give Benjamin their daughters, so they hunt for a workaround. Jabesh Gilead skipped the assembly, so twelve thousand men kill everyone there and keep four hundred virgins. Two hundred short. So: grab the girls dancing at the Shiloh festival.
JER 1:1-19	God hires Jeremiah with a line that leaves no room to negotiate: I knew you before you were even formed, you're a prophet to the nations. Jeremiah's counter: I'm a kid, I can't talk. Overruled. God touches his mouth and hands him a job description that's four demolition verbs and only two building ones. Then a pot boiling over from the north.
JER 2:1-19	God starts out sounding like someone flipping through the wedding album: remember the honeymoon, you followed me through the desert. Then the questions get pointed. What exactly did your parents find wrong with me? Two crimes: you walked away from a spring of running water and chiselled your own cisterns, and they leak. And now your plan is to go drink out of the Nile.
JER 2:20-37	The images come fast: a premium vine gone feral, a stain that laughs at lye and soap, a camel bolting, a wild donkey in heat that nobody could stop. They call a log dad and a rock mom, and they've got exactly as many gods as they have towns. Then the one that stings: a bride doesn't forget her jewelry, and you forgot me.
JER 3:1-25	The chapter quotes the law saying a remarried divorcee can't come back, then says: anyway, come back. She's been waiting by the road with a face that refuses to blush. Then the two sisters. Israel got served papers, Judah watched and did the same, then came back faking it. Verdict: the open backslider looks better than the faker. The offer is weirdly specific, shepherds who actually know things and a Jerusalem so full of God nobody misses the ark. Ends in the people's voice, and it's grim.
JER 4:1-31	Starts with terms and conditions, ends with an air raid siren. Circumcise your hearts, and then: blow the trumpet, get inside the walls, there's already a lion on the road. Jeremiah says the boldest thing yet, straight to God: you let these people be told they'd have peace with a sword at their throat. Then the vision, Genesis running backwards: earth empty, sky dark, mountains rocking, no people, not even birds. Last shot, the city in scarlet and eyeliner, dressing for lovers who came to kill her.
JER 5:1-31	God's challenge: find me one person in Jerusalem doing justice, and I'll pardon the whole city. Jeremiah figures the poor just don't know the law, so he goes to the important people; they've all broken the yoke. Now a lion, a wolf and a leopard are waiting outside. Their take: nothing bad's coming, the prophets are hot air. God: fine, my words are fire and you're firewood. Closer: the prophets lie, the priests do what they want, and my people love it like this.
JER 6:1-30	Get out of Jerusalem, blow the trumpet, something bad is watching from the north. We hear the attackers arguing: hit at noon, or wait for dark. The city pumps out wickedness the way a well pumps water. Everybody's greedy, slapping a bandage on the wound going peace, peace, when there is no peace, and they can't even blush. Told to ask for the old road, they said flat out: not walking it. Ends in the smelter: bellows blasting, lead burned off, silver rejected.
JER 7:1-19	Jeremiah is told to stand in the temple gate and preach. His target is a slogan, repeated three times so you hear it as one: Yahweh's temple, Yahweh's temple, Yahweh's temple. You cannot steal, murder, sleep around, lie under oath and burn incense to Baal, then walk in here announcing you're fine. That makes it a robbers' hideout. Then: go look at Shiloh. And the harshest line yet, at Jeremiah: stop praying for these people. Next time on Jeremiah: a valley gets a new name.
JER 7:20-34	Fine, pile up your burnt offerings, eat the meat. What I actually asked for coming out of Egypt was not sacrifices, it was listen to me. I've sent prophets every day since, and every generation stiffened its neck a little more. Jeremiah gets told upfront that nobody will listen to him either. Then the valley: they built Topheth to burn their own sons and daughters, and God says it never even entered his mind. Renamed the Valley of Slaughter, graves until there's no room, every wedding in Judah silent.
JER 8:1-22	Graves get opened: the bones of kings, priests and prophets under the sun, moon and stars they worshipped, left like manure. God asks why a guy who falls down doesn't get up. The bird lesson: the stork knows when to migrate, so do the dove, swallow and crane. My people don't know my law. The scribes' pens are lying, and it's peace, peace, when there's no peace, again. Ends inside Jeremiah: harvest over, summer gone, not saved, and is there no balm in Gilead?
JER 9:1-26	Jeremiah wishes his head was a reservoir so he could cry nonstop, then wishes he could move to a desert motel. Trust nobody, your brother included; every tongue is a bow strung for lies. God says he's crying too, over burned pastures where you can't hear a single cow. Professional mourners get hired to teach their daughters to wail: death is climbing in the windows. Parked in the middle of it: stop bragging about being smart, strong or rich, and brag about knowing me instead.
JER 10:1-25	Their gods are a joke: somebody chops a tree, a carpenter shapes it, they plate it in silver and nail it down so it doesn't fall over. It can't talk. And the real burn: it can't even do anything bad to you. Yahweh pulls wind out of a storehouse, and any god that didn't make heaven and earth is going away. Then the mood crashes: a guy whose tent is flattened and his kids gone, asking for correction, gently, not in anger.
JER 11:1-23	God has Jeremiah read out the contract terms: cursed is anyone who ignores the covenant I made when I hauled your ancestors out of Egypt's furnace. Then the stats: as many gods as towns, as many Baal altars as Jerusalem has streets. And for the second time, stop praying for these people. Israel was a beautiful olive tree and God is personally setting it on fire. Then, personal: Jeremiah finds a plot against him, out of Anathoth, his hometown. Their offer, quit prophesying or we kill you.
JER 12:1-17	Jeremiah starts with the polite bit, you're righteous, then asks anyway: why do the crooks do so well? You planted them, and you're right there on their lips and nowhere near their hearts. God's answer is a raise: if jogging with foot soldiers wore you out, how will you race horses? If flat ground scares you, wait till the Jordan thickets. Also, your brothers are in on it. Then God grieves too: I handed over the one I loved, and my vineyard got trampled because nobody cares.
JER 13:1-27	God's first assignment: buy a linen belt, wear it, never wash it. Then bury it in a rock crack by the Euphrates. Dig it up later: rotted. That's the sermon: Judah's pride. Next, a setup: announce that every bottle should be filled with wine, the crowd goes yeah, obviously, and the bottles are people, the filling is drunkenness, and God's going to smash them together. Then: can an Ethiopian change his skin, or a leopard his spots? No. Neither can people this practiced at being awful.
JER 14:1-22	Drought chapter, all in the details: kids back from the cistern with empty jars, farmers with heads covered, a doe walking off from her own newborn, wild donkeys panting on bare rock. The prayer: why are you acting like a tourist who booked one night here? God's answer: prohibition number three, stop praying for them. Jeremiah pushes back: your other prophets are promising guaranteed peace. God: I never sent them, and they'll starve with their audience. Closes where these always close: nobody's idol ever made it rain.
JER 15:1-21	God names Moses and Samuel, the two best closers going, and says it wouldn't matter if either showed up. Four kinds of destroyer get assigned, including dogs. Then: I am tired of being compassionate. The mother of seven watches her sun go down at noon. Then Jeremiah's complaint: I never loaned anybody money and everybody curses me. He ate God's words, loved them, skipped every party. God's answer offers zero sympathy and a job: sort the good from the garbage, be my mouth, be a bronze wall.
JER 16:1-21	The instructions here are the weirdest thing God asks of any prophet, and all about not showing up. Don't get married. Don't have kids. Don't go to funerals. Don't go to weddings. Jeremiah's entire life becomes the sermon, effective and brutal. People ask what exactly they did and get told they're worse than their parents. Then the flip: nobody will swear by the God who brought us out of Egypt, they'll swear by the God who brought us back from the north. Fishermen first, then hunters.
JER 17:1-27	Judah's sin is carved with an iron pen and a diamond tip. Permanent marker, ancient edition. Then the famous contrast: trust people and you're a desert bush that doesn't notice when good stuff shows up; trust God and you're a tree on a riverbank, green through a drought. Then: the heart is deceitful above all things, who can even know it. Money got wrong is a partridge sitting on eggs she never laid. And the chapter ends on, of all things, Sabbath delivery rules.
JER 18:1-23	Jeremiah is sent to the pottery shop. A pot goes wrong and the potter calmly reshapes it. That's the sermon: you're clay in my hands, but nothing's locked in yet. God spells it out both ways, so the threats are conditional too. Judah's reply is the flattest no in the book: forget it, we're doing our own thing. Then they cook up a plot against Jeremiah, weapon of choice specified as the tongue. His prayer back is long, detailed and asks for exactly zero forgiveness.
JER 19:1-15	New prop: a clay bottle. New venue: the valley where they burned their own children, which God says never even crossed his mind. He brings elders and priests as witnesses. The siege he describes includes people eating their own children, stated flat. Then he smashes the bottle, and the detail that lands: a shattered pot cannot be put back together, unlike the one on the potter's wheel a chapter ago. Then he walks to the temple court and says it all again to a bigger crowd.
JER 20:1-18	The temple's chief officer beats Jeremiah and locks him in the stocks overnight. Jeremiah's first move on release is to rename the guy Terror On Every Side, both a great insult and an actual prophecy. Then comes the rawest passage in the book. He tells God: you talked me into this, you're stronger than me, and now I'm the town joke. When he decides to quit, the word turns into a fire in his bones. One verse of praise, then he curses the day he was born.
JER 21:1-14	Zedekiah sends a delegation to ask Jeremiah for a miracle, specifically one of the classic wondrous works. Bad news: God is on the other side of the wall. He's turning their own weapons back inward and fighting them personally. Then the offer, framed as a choice and honestly not much of one: stay in the city and die of sword, hunger or disease, or walk out and surrender to the Chaldeans and live. Least patriotic advice any prophet ever gave, said out loud in public.
JER 22:1-30	Jeremiah gets sent to the palace door with the terms: do justice, protect the immigrant, orphan and widow, and your kings keep riding through these gates. Otherwise God swears on himself the place is rubble. One king gets roasted for his renovation: wide rooms, cedar ceiling, painted red, all built on wages he never paid, while his dad ate, drank and did justice for the poor. Jehoiakim gets a donkey's funeral. And Coniah, the signet ring on God's own right hand, gets yanked off and filed as childless.
JER 23:1-18	The shepherd thing becomes a performance audit: you scattered my sheep and never checked on them. So God takes the job himself and promises David a righteous Branch, Yahweh Our Righteousness. The oath gets updated: not the God who brought us out of Egypt but the God who brought us back from the north. Then the prophets: bones rattling, staggering like a drunk. Samaria's prophets at least worked for Baal openly; Jerusalem's cheer evildoers into staying put. Closing question: who here has stood in the council and heard anything?
JER 23:19-40	God's problem with the fake prophets isn't that they're wrong, it's that they never showed up: I didn't send them and they ran anyway. Am I only a God who's nearby? Can anybody hide from me? Then the dreams: I had a dream! I had a dream! Fine, tell your dream, but what's straw got to do with wheat? My word is fire and a hammer that shatters rock. And the phrase the message from Yahweh gets banned outright, because everybody's opinion has become a divine message.
JER 24:1-10	Two baskets of figs in front of the temple, one gorgeous first-of-the-season, one so rotten you couldn't eat them. Then the twist, which flips what everyone in Jerusalem assumed. The good figs are the exiles already dragged off to Babylon: God's watching them for good, bringing them back, planting them, giving them a heart to know him. The rotten ones are Zedekiah, his officials and everyone still living in Jerusalem and Egypt. Surviving was not proof you were the favorite, which is exactly why this one stung.
JER 25:1-18	Jeremiah does the math on his own career: twenty three years of preaching, zero listeners, and the same for every prophet before him. Then Nebuchadnezzar gets the title that must have hit like a slap: my servant. The losses aren't military, they're domestic: no wedding music, no millstones, no lamp at night. Seventy years, and Babylon gets an expiration date too. Then the weirdest prop yet: a cup of wrath walked to every nation until they all stagger. Next time on Jeremiah: the rest of the guest list drinks.
JER 25:19-38	The cup goes on tour, and the guest list is the point: Pharaoh, Ashkelon, Gaza, Ekron, Edom, Moab, Ammon, Tyre, Sidon, the Arabian kings, Elam, the Medes, every king in the north, and eventually every kingdom on earth, with Sheshach last. Instructions: drink, get drunk, throw up, fall down, don't get back up. Refusing isn't an option, and the reasoning is chilling: judgment already started at the city with my name on it, so nobody else gets a pass. Then God roars like a guy stomping grapes.
JER 26:1-24	The instructions come with a warning: leave nothing out. He doesn't, and priests, prophets and the crowd grab him, all agreeing he should die. So: a trial, the best documented in the book. His defense is four lines: God sent me, I'm in your hands, and if you kill me that blood is on you. The officials acquit him, the elders citing case law: Hezekiah didn't kill Micah for saying the same thing. Then Uriah, the case that went the other way: chased into Egypt, extradited, executed.
JER 27:1-22	New costume: a yoke, worn on his actual neck, with copies mailed to five neighboring kings whose envoys happen to be in Jerusalem. The message is politically insane and theologically simple: the earth belongs to whoever made it, and right now he's handed it to Nebuchadnezzar, so stick your neck in the yoke and keep your land. Then a challenge to the temple optimists predicting the stolen gear back any day: if you're real prophets, pray the stuff still here stays here. It won't.
JER 28:1-17	A rival prophet stands up in the temple with a way better message: yoke's broken, everything's back in two years. Jeremiah does not take the bait. Amen, he says, I really hope you're right. The test is brutally simple: prophets have always predicted disaster, so anybody predicting peace only gets credit afterward. Hananiah's comeback is physical, he rips the wooden yoke off Jeremiah's neck, and Jeremiah just walks off. The real answer comes later: you broke bars of wood, now they're iron. Also, you're dying this year.
JER 29:1-17	Jeremiah mails a letter to the exiles in Babylon, and the contents are the opposite of what anyone there wanted. Build houses. Plant gardens. Get married. Have kids. And then the hardest line: pray for the city that kidnapped you, because your peace is tied to its peace. Only after all that domestic advice do you get the famous verse about thoughts of peace and a future, and it's a promise about seventy years out, not next spring. Next time on Jeremiah: two prophets get named out loud.
JER 29:18-32	The mail turns personal and starts naming names. Ahab son of Kolaiah and Zedekiah son of Maaseiah get handed to Nebuchadnezzar and executed in front of the exiles, and their names become a curse formula people use. Then Shemaiah the Nehelamite, who wrote to the temple's enforcement guy complaining that nobody had thrown Jeremiah in the stocks yet, quoting Jeremiah's letter as evidence. Zephaniah the priest reads the complaint out loud to Jeremiah, which is how Jeremiah answers it in writing, with a prophecy about Shemaiah's family line.
JER 30:1-24	Write it down in a book, a hint this stuff is meant to outlast the crisis. Terror first, and the image picked for grown men is labor pains, hands clutching their sides. Then a weirdly honest assessment: the wound is incurable, there's no medicine, and the guy who did it did it on purpose. Then with zero transition the same speaker says: I'm going to heal you. The reason? A nickname that got back to him. That's Zion, the one nobody bothers about.
JER 31:1-20	Loyalty said out loud to people living in rubble: I have loved you with an everlasting love. The homecoming parade includes the blind, the lame, and women in active labor, nobody's idea of a triumphal procession. The guy who scattered Israel is the guy gathering him. Then Rachel, crying in Ramah for kids who are gone and refusing to be comforted, and God doesn't argue, he says the work gets paid back. Ephraim's apology is quoted instead of described, which makes it sound like a person talking.
JER 31:21-40	Practical first: put up road signs, you're coming back this way. Then a line nobody has cracked, a woman will encompass a man. Jeremiah mentions his sleep had been sweet, the one nice thing he says about his own night. Then the sour grapes proverb gets retired: everybody answers for themselves. Then the new covenant, and the new part is purely location: the law moved off tablets and into people, so nobody teaches anybody. Even the valley full of corpses and ash gets rezoned holy.
JER 32:1-23	Jeremiah is locked up mid-siege for saying the city will fall, and in there God tells him to buy property. His cousin Hanamel shows up with the offer on cue, which is how he knows it's real. The paperwork is pure accountant energy: seventeen shekels weighed, deed signed, sealed, two copies, witnesses, handed to Baruch for a clay jar. Why? One sentence: houses and fields and vineyards will be bought in this land again. Next time on Jeremiah: he asks what the field was even for.
JER 32:24-44	Jeremiah wraps up his prayer by pointing out the window: the siege ramps are built, the city's gone, and you had me buy a field. God's answer quotes Jeremiah's own opening line back at him: is there anything too hard for me? Then the charge sheet, ending with children passed through fire to Molech, which he says never crossed his mind. Then the reversal, in the same paperwork language: fields bought, deeds signed, sealed, witnessed, all over Benjamin and Judah.
JER 33:1-26	Still under guard, the word starts with an invitation, not a verdict: call me and I'll show you huge things you don't know. Same breath: the houses knocked down for siege defenses fill with corpses, and he's bringing health, healing, peace and truth. The promise is a sound: wedding music heard again where there is now neither a person nor an animal. And the guarantee is the sunrise. Break day and night if you can, and then, sure, the covenant with David can break too. Not before.
JER 34:1-22	Zedekiah's verdict: your city burns, you go to Babylon, but you'll die peacefully with a proper funeral. Then the chapter's real subject. Zedekiah declares freedom for every Hebrew slave, everybody complies, then drags them all back into service. The worst thing in the book. God's answer is a pun with teeth: you wouldn't proclaim liberty, so here's a liberty for you, to the sword, the plague and the famine. The men who sealed that deal walking between the halves of a butchered calf get handed over.
JER 35:1-19	God's instruction: throw a wine party for the one family in Israel famous for never drinking, in a temple side room. The Rechabites refuse: our ancestor Jonadab said no wine, no houses, no crops, tents only, and we've kept it for generations. God, by contrast, has spoken in person and sent every prophet he's got, and gotten nowhere. The Rechabites get their own promise, the only good news anybody gets for a while.
JER 36:1-15	This whole chapter is about a document, lovingly recorded. Jeremiah dictates, Baruch writes it out. Banned from the temple, Jeremiah has him read it by proxy, on the fast day. And it works. Baruch reads it in a scribe's room, one guy walks straight to the palace and reports. The officials, being officials, don't act on it. They send for the document and have it read again from the top. Next time on Jeremiah: the king has a fire going.
JER 36:16-32	The officials react correctly, with fear, and ask the right question: how was this written? Baruch: he talked, I wrote it in ink. They tell him to go hide with Jeremiah. The king, sitting by a brazier, listens to a few columns, slices the scroll up with a penknife and feeds it into the fire. Nobody in the room tears their clothes. Three men try to stop him and get ignored. God's response: write it again, longer, the worst possible news for a guy with a penknife.
JER 37:1-21	Egypt marches out, the Chaldeans pull back, and for one glorious minute it looks salvageable. Jeremiah's word: Pharaoh's going home, and even a handful of wounded soldiers would come back and burn this city. He checks on his property during the break, and gets arrested at the gate as a defector. Beaten, thrown in a dungeon, then hauled out for a secret audience where he gives the same answer that got him arrested, plus a question: where are those prophets who said Babylon wasn't coming?
JER 38:1-14	The charge against Jeremiah is now purely military: he's killing morale. The officials want him dead, and Zedekiah's answer is one of the saddest lines any king gets: he's in your hands, I can't do anything. They lower Jeremiah down a cistern on ropes into mud, and he sinks. Ebedmelech, an Ethiopian eunuch, tells the king flatly the man will starve down there. He grabs old rags to pad Jeremiah's armpits so the ropes don't cut him. Next time on Jeremiah: the king wants a private word.
JER 38:15-28	Jeremiah's opening position: if I tell you, you'll kill me, and you won't take the advice anyway. Under oath, he lays out the deal: walk out and surrender, and you live and the city doesn't burn. Zedekiah's real fear turns out to be the guys who defected earlier laughing at him. Jeremiah shows him the other option: the palace women led out chanting a taunt, feet stuck in the mud Jeremiah just got hauled out of. The two agree on a cover story, and it holds.
JER 39:1-18	Everything Jeremiah warned about for forty chapters happens in eighteen verses, narrated flat. The wall breaks, the Babylonian officers sit in the middle gate like they own it. Zedekiah bolts at night and gets caught on the Jericho plain. Nebuchadnezzar kills his sons in front of him, then blinds him, so the last thing he sees is chosen for him. City burns. Two small mercies: the poorest get fields and vineyards, and Jeremiah gets taken care of. Ebedmelech, the guy with the rags, is told he lives.
JER 40:1-16	Jeremiah gets released at Ramah by a Babylonian officer with a short, accurate sermon on why Jerusalem fell. The offer: come to Babylon and I'll look after you, or go anywhere you want. He picks staying with Gedaliah at Mizpah. For a few verses things are fine: refugees return, and there's a huge harvest of wine and fruit, the only prosperity in the book. Then Johanan shows up with intel on an assassination plot. Gedaliah's answer is the last mistake he ever makes: nah, you're lying about Ishmael.
JER 41:1-18	Ishmael sits down and eats a meal with Gedaliah, then kills him at the table, along with everyone with him and the Chaldean garrison. Eighty pilgrims show up in mourning, carrying offerings for a temple that isn't there anymore. Ishmael walks out to meet them crying, invites them in, kills them, and fills Asa's old cistern with the bodies. Ten survive by telling him where the food's buried. He hauls off the survivors toward Ammon, gets intercepted at Gibeon, and the captives walk over to the other side.
JER 42:1-22	The delegation's oath is emphatic: whatever the answer is, good or bad, we'll do it. Jeremiah takes ten days, which suggests he genuinely didn't already know. The answer: stay, and the reason is unusual for this book, God says he grieves over the damage he's caused. The alternative gets a neat inversion: the sword you're running from will find you in Egypt. Jeremiah adds his own verdict: you'd already made up your minds before you asked, and you conned yourselves.
JER 43:1-13	They waited ten days for the answer and respond by accusing the prophet of taking dictation from his own secretary. Then they do the exact thing they were told not to, and drag Jeremiah and Baruch along into Egypt against his own preaching. He gets another prop job: bury stones at Pharaoh's door, and announce a Babylonian throne is going to sit there. The image for the conquest is weirdly casual: Nebuchadnezzar puts Egypt on like a shepherd shrugging into his coat, then strolls out.
JER 44:1-16	The argument is made from evidence: you saw what happened to Jerusalem, it's empty, and here you are doing the identical thing in a brand new country. The offender list is unusually complete, including the kings' wives and your wives and you. The threat is that the return home you keep dreaming about isn't happening. Then the crowd actually answers back, which almost never happens in this book: we will not listen. Next time on Jeremiah: they explain the reasoning.
JER 44:17-30	Their reasoning turns out to be economic: when we burned incense to the queen of the sky we had plenty to eat, and it fell apart after we quit. The women add a sharp note: our husbands helped make the cakes. Jeremiah accepts the timeline and flips the causation. Then the cutting line: fine, go ahead, keep your vows, perform them. What follows is basically erasure: no Judean in Egypt will ever use God's name in an oath again. Proof offered: Pharaoh Hophra goes the way Zedekiah went.
JER 45:1-5	Five verses, addressed to the guy holding the pen. Baruch's complaint gets quoted instead of described: sorrow on top of pain, exhausted from groaning, no rest anywhere. God doesn't deny a word of it. What he offers is scale: I'm tearing down what I built across this entire land, so this is a bad moment to chase big things for yourself. Then one promise, small and enormous: wherever you go, you get out alive.
JER 46:1-28	Nation oracles kick off with a battle already lost, Carchemish, told in loving detail: harness up, polish the spears, strap on the armor. Mid-stanza: wait, why is everyone running away? Egypt floods like the Nile but the day belongs to Yahweh. Best jab in the chapter: go get some balm from Gilead, none of your medicines work. Then the real invasion, Egypt a heifer, her mercenaries pampered stall calves who bolt. Jacob gets the last word: don't be afraid.
JER 47:1-7	Short oracle, and the detail that sticks is the dads: at the sound of chariots they don't even turn for their own kids, hands gone limp with fear. That beats the flood metaphor around it. Gaza gets its head shaved, Ashkelon erased. Then the chapter does something rare: it talks to the sword directly, tells it to rest. The sword's answer: it has orders and doesn't get to stop.
JER 48:1-26	Moab's oracle is basically a map with a town destroyed per clause. There's a genuine joke too: Madmen, an actual place, gets told it will be brought to silence.
