The best image is a wine cellar: Moab's sat on his own sediment so long he tastes exactly the same, so the punishment is decanting, smashing the jars. Chemosh gets marched off into captivity with his own priesthood. Tucked in the middle: a chilling aside about doing God's work half-heartedly.
JER 48:27-47	Opens with the grudge stated flat out: you shook your head every time you mentioned Israel. Then out of nowhere the register flips and God says he'll wail for Moab himself, crying harder than Jazer ever cried over the vine of Sibmah. The losses named are exact: nobody shouting at the winepress. Then the terror, pit and snare combo returns, and after forty seven verses of demolition there's one line saying he'll bring Moab back.
JER 49:1-19	Ammon's up first, a property dispute: does Israel have no heirs, that somebody else is squatting in Gad? Their god Malcam gets marched into captivity with his whole priesthood. Then Edom, and the argument is thoroughness: grape pickers leave gleanings, burglars stop once they've got enough, but Esau's stripped naked, hiding spots exposed. Tucked in the middle: leave your orphans, I'll keep them alive. Then the pride gets located exactly: you built your nest as high as an eagle, and it isn't going to help.
JER 49:20-39	Four quick oracles, each with a single great image. Edom's collapse is loud enough to reach the Red Sea. Damascus gets asked, almost tenderly, how the city of praise hasn't already been abandoned, then her palaces go up in flames. Kedar and Hazor get indicted for being comfortable: living at ease with no gates, no bars, more headstone than charge. Elam gets four winds and a promise no country will be without an Elamite refugee. Two of the four end with God bringing them back.
JER 50:1-23	Marching orders read like a press release: raise the flag, Babylon has fallen, Bel and Merodach humiliated. Israel and Judah come home crying, asking the way to Zion. God calls his people lost sheep and blames the shepherds who walked them until they forgot home. The predators get quoted offering a theological excuse for eating them, the nastiest line here. Then the accounting: Assyria ate Israel, Babylon broke his bones, so Babylon gets what Assyria got. Best promise: they'll look for Israel's guilt and it won't be there.
JER 50:24-46	Opens with the trap already sprung: gotcha, Babylon, and you didn't even notice. Then God opens his armory, and the attack orders are practical: come from the farthest border, crack the storehouses, kill the bulls, leave nothing. The middle just runs the word sword down a list: princes, wise men, soldiers, chariots, treasure. Then drought on the water, because this country's lost its mind over idols. New tenants: wolves and ostriches. The closing note is a sound, the earth shaking and a cry going up across every nation.
JHN 1:1-28	No baby, no shepherds, no genealogy. John starts before Genesis: the Word was with God, was God, made everything. First person up is John, not the light, just the guy pointing at it. Then the Word became flesh and moved in. The interrogation he answers by denial. Not the Christ. Not Elijah. Just a voice. Next time on John: the Lamb of God.
JHN 1:29-51	John says "look, the Lamb of God" twice, and the second time two of his own disciples just walk off after Jesus. Recruiting goes person to person. Andrew grabs Simon, who gets renamed on the spot. Philip fetches Nathanael, whose reaction is the most honest line here: can anything good come out of Nazareth? Jesus mentions a fig tree, and that's that.
JHN 2:1-25	First miracle in John is a wedding running out of booze. His mother reports it rather than asking, he says it isn't his hour, and she tells the staff to do whatever he says anyway. Six giant washing jars come out as good wine, and the caterer congratulates the groom, who has no clue. Then Jerusalem: whip, livestock, tables flipped.
JHN 3:1-36	Nicodemus shows up at night, which John mentions on purpose. Born again lands entirely literally: am I supposed to climb back into my mother? Jesus notes that the teacher of Israel could do better. Then he quietly vanishes from his own scene and the speech keeps going: the wind, the snake on the pole, the most famous verse in the book.
JHN 4:1-27	The longest conversation Jesus has with anybody is with a Samaritan woman at a well at noon, and it kicks off because he's thirsty with no bucket. She clocks the etiquette problem instantly. He offers water that ends thirst forever, she hears a plumbing upgrade. Then he tells her what he tells nobody else this early: I'm him. Next time on John: the town.
JHN 4:28-54	She drops the water jar, the detail John keeps, and sells the whole town on a guy who told her everything she'd done. The town's closing line to her is generous and a little brutal: we believe now, and not because of you. Then Galilee, where a desperate official gets one sentence: go, your son lives. He checks the exact hour afterwards.
JHN 5:1-22	A pool with five porches, sick people waiting for the water to move, and one man ill thirty-eight years with nobody to carry him in. Jesus asks if he wants to get well, which in that place is a fair question. He walks, and the first thing anyone says is that carrying a mat on the Sabbath is illegal. Next time on John: the evidence.
JHN 5:23-47	This turns into a legal argument. He grants that testifying about yourself doesn't count, then starts calling witnesses. John, a lamp they enjoyed while it was new. The works. The Father. Then he turns it around: you comb the scriptures hunting for life and won't come to the guy they point at. And he won't be the one accusing them. Moses will.
JHN 6:1-34	The feeding opens with a pop quiz: where do we buy bread? Philip answers with math and fails. Andrew turns up a kid with five loaves and two fish and doesn't pretend it helps. Five thousand fed, and a detail only John keeps: waste nothing, twelve baskets. The crowd tries to crown him by force, so he vanishes. Next time on John: bread of life.
JHN 6:35-71	The bread thing gets deliberately harder. First: I'm the bread of life, fine, metaphor. Then: I came down from heaven, and they counter by naming his mum and dad. Then: the bread is my flesh. Asked how anyone eats that, he says it again and adds blood. Result: the only mass walkout in the gospels. Peter's answer is closer to where else would we go.
JHN 7:1-26	His brothers, who don't believe in him, give him media advice: nobody hides, go make a scene. He says no, then goes anyway, quietly. The city's full of whispering, half good man, half con artist, none of it out loud. Halfway through the feast he starts teaching, and the first complaint is that he never went to school. Next time on John: the last day.
JHN 7:27-53	The argument about where he's from is a circle: we know where he's from, and the Christ's origin is meant to be a mystery, so knowing disqualifies him. Officers sent to arrest him come back with nobody, and their excuse is the best line here: no man ever spoke like this. Nicodemus asks a procedural question and gets asked if he's a Galilean too.
JHN 8:1-29	The trap is nicely built: a woman caught in the act, a law that says stone her, and a question with no safe answer. He crouches down and writes in the dirt. Twice. Then one sentence: whoever's got no sin goes first. The crowd thins out from the oldest down. What he says to her contains zero lecture. Next time on John: Abraham comes up.
JHN 8:30-59	Starts with people believing him, ends with them reaching for rocks. The trigger is freedom: they hear an insult, because Abraham's kids were never slaves, and he means slaves to sin. They claim Abraham, he grants the bloodline and denies the resemblance. They claim God, he names the devil. Then the line that ends everything: before Abraham existed, I AM. Out come the stones.
JHN 9:1-41	The disciples open with the question, whose fault is it, and get told that's the wrong question. Then mud made with spit, a pool called Sent, and a man who comes back seeing. Then a four-round courtroom farce: neighbours who can't agree it's him, parents too scared to say anything, and the man himself: I was blind, now I see. He gets thrown out.
JHN 10:1-42	The sheepfold speech lands so badly he has to start over, and take two is weirder, because now he's the door as well as the shepherd. The comparison is the hired hand, whose response to a wolf is completely rational and useless. Then, at a winter festival, they demand a straight answer and get one: I and the Father are one. Out come the stones.
JHN 11:1-31	Word comes that his friend is dying, and Jesus waits two days. The disciples don't want to go back to Judea because last time involved stoning, and Thomas settles it with the bleakest line in the gospel: let's go die with him. Jesus says Lazarus is asleep, they hear he'll be fine, and he has to spell it out. Next time on John: the tomb.
JHN 11:32-57	Mary says exactly what Martha said, and this time Jesus doesn't answer with theology. In the shortest verse in the book, he cries. At the cave, Martha makes the practical objection about four days and the smell. He shouts one name. What walks out is still tied up. Then a committee meeting, and a high priest noting that one death is cheaper than a nation.
JHN 12:1-25	Dinner at Bethany, with a guy at the table who was dead last week. Mary pours a pound of nard on his feet. Judas objects for the poor, and John torpedoes him: he ran the cash box and skimmed it. Next day: palm branches, a donkey, and disciples with no clue what it meant. Next time on John: a voice from the sky.
JHN 12:26-50	He says his soul is troubled, then rules out asking to be let off. A voice from the sky reaches the crowd as thunder, or maybe an angel. He tells them to use the daylight, then hides. John explains the unbelief from Isaiah, then adds the sting: plenty of the rulers believed and said nothing, because they liked being liked.
JHN 13:1-38	No bread and cup in John. Instead the Lord and Teacher strips down, ties on a towel, and washes twelve pairs of feet, including the pair belonging to the man who has already agreed to sell him. Peter refuses out of respect, gets overruled, then swings the other way. Then the dipped bread, "do it quickly," and Judas walks out into the night.
JHN 14:1-31	The comfort speech keeps getting derailed by the disciples, which is what makes it readable. Thomas admits they have no clue where he's going. Philip asks to be shown the Father, after years standing next to him, and gets a reply that's patient and just a little bit "seriously, Philip?" Then it stops mid-thought with "get up, we're leaving."
JHN 15:1-27	The vine picture isn't cosy. The Father runs the operation, dead branches get pulled, live ones get cut back, and the point is yield. Then the promotion: you're not servants any more, because servants don't get told what's going on, and you've been told everything. Then it goes cold. The world's hatred isn't a risk, it's a schedule, and it's inherited.
JHN 16:1-33	The heads-up is specific and grim: thrown out of synagogues, killed by people who think they're doing God a favour. Then the argument that his leaving is good news, because the Counselor can't come otherwise. He admits there's more he can't tell them yet. The riddle about "a little while" they debate among themselves instead of asking him.
JHN 17:1-26	The entire chapter is Jesus praying out loud while the disciples stand there. He says the job is done and asks for the glory he had before there was a world. Then the same ask, over and over: keep them, keep them together, keep them away from the evil one, and no, don't pull them out of the world. And one line dropped in flat, with no commentary at all: none of them lost, except the one.
JHN 18:1-21	No agony in the garden here, and no kiss either. Jesus walks out to meet the arrest squad, and when he answers his own question the entire detachment ends up on the ground. He negotiates safe passage for everyone else before letting himself be taken. Peter's sword produces one casualty, and John alone names him: Malchus, right ear. Next time on John: Pilate.
JHN 18:22-40	The procedural answer earns him a slap, which he also questions. Then Peter's second and third denials, the last to a relative of the guy whose ear he cut off, and then the rooster. The delegation won't go inside the Praetorium, because that would leave them unfit to eat the Passover. A governor asks a legal question and gets a philosophical answer.
JHN 19:1-22	In John the flogging happens mid-trial, meant to satisfy the crowd rather than start the execution. Pilate brings the result out with three words people have quoted ever since. The charge changes from politics to Son of God, which makes Pilate more scared, not less. The lever that finally moves him is Caesar's friendship. Next time on John: the cross.
JHN 19:23-42	The soldiers divide the clothes, then decide not to cut up the seamless coat and gamble for it instead, which is also just what soldiers would do. Four women are standing there, three of them called Mary. From the cross he sorts out his mother's living arrangements. Then thirst, vinegar, two words. A spear replaces the leg-breaking, and the writer insists he watched it.
JHN 20:1-31	The resurrection in John starts as a suspected grave robbery. Mary's report is completely reasonable and completely wrong. The run to the tomb is recorded with the winner noted, and John keeps the detail nobody would invent: the head cloth folded up by itself. She thinks he's the gardener; one word, her name, sorts it. Thomas lists conditions, gets every one, uses none.
JHN 21:1-25	An epilogue where seven disciples, having now seen the risen Lord twice, go fishing and catch nothing all night. A man on the beach asks the question fishermen hate most, then tells them which side to cast on. Peter's response to realising who it is: put clothes ON, then jump in the water. Three questions, matching three denials.
JOB 1:1-22	Meet Job. Filthy rich, seven thousand sheep, ten kids, so careful he sacrifices in case his children sinned at a party he was not at. Cut to heaven, where Satan says sure he is loyal, you have got him behind a fence. Fence comes down. Four messengers back to back: oxen, sheep, camels, all ten kids. Job shaves his head and blesses the name.
JOB 2:1-13	Round two. Satan's new pitch: skin for skin, a guy will trade anything to save his own hide. God says fine, just do not kill him. Job ends up covered in sores head to foot, scraping himself with a chunk of broken pot. His wife's entire contribution: curse God and die. Three friends turn up and sit with him a week without a word.
JOB 3:1-26	A week of silence, then Job opens his mouth and curses his own birthday. Wipe the date off the calendar, let darkness grab it, get the professional curse guys in. Why did I not just die at birth? Down there kings and slaves lie together and nobody is boss of anybody. Last line lands hard: the thing I was always scared of showed up.
JOB 4:1-21	Eliphaz goes first and opens with mind if I say something, never a good sign. He compliments Job on how great he used to be at comforting other people. Then the theory: innocent people do not get wrecked, so do the math. Evidence includes lions that starve anyway and a full ghost story, a spirit gliding past his face at night, hair standing up.
JOB 5:1-27	Eliphaz keeps talking. Man is born to trouble like sparks fly up. Then the sales pitch: getting disciplined is actually a gift, because God wounds you and then bandages the exact wound he made. Six troubles and then seven, a truce with wild animals, a peaceful old-age funeral like grain at harvest. Terrible timing, said to a man whose kids are freshly buried.
JOB 6:1-30	Job wants his misery weighed on a real scale against the sand of the sea. He asks if a donkey ever brays over good grass. His actual request is for God to finish him off. Then he nails his friends: a desert creek, packed with ice in winter, bone dry in summer, so the caravans that planned around it show up and just stand there.
JOB 8:1-22	Bildad has no time for bedside manner. How long are you going to blow hot air? God does not bend justice, so your kids died because they earned it. Cool. Anyway, pray about it. His authorities are the ancestors, since we personally know nothing. His image: the godless guy's confidence is a spiderweb he plans to lean on.
JOB 7:1-21	Job's take on human life: a shift you cannot clock out of, a day labourer squinting for the shadow that means quitting time. His nights are the punishment, his days blow past faster than a shuttle. He asks if he is the sea, or some monster that needs a guard posted on it. God's cure for insomnia is nightmares.
JOB 9:1-35	Job's position: obviously I cannot win. God moves mountains before they notice and tells the sun not to bother rising. Which is the problem. He is the strong one and the judge, so who serves him a summons? Then the bomb: he wrecks good people and bad people the same. What I need is a ref who can grab us both. There isn't one.
JOB 10:1-22	Job decides complaining is now his full-time gig. Question for God: do you have human eyeballs? Is your lifespan short? Because you are rushing this investigation like a man on a deadline. Then the good part: you poured me out like milk and curdled me like cheese, wired in the bones, and now you are taking the whole thing apart.
JOB 11:1-20	Zophar goes next and opens by complaining Job talks too much. He would love God to answer Job personally. Buddy, careful what you wish for. Then a great riff on God being higher than heaven and deeper than Sheol, immediately followed by Zophar explaining what is in it. Weirdest burn in the book: a dumb guy wises up when a donkey has a human baby.
JOB 12:1-25	Job is done being polite. Oh for sure, wisdom will die with you guys. The man who prayed and got answered is the punchline and robbers are thriving. Ask the animals. Ask the birds. Ask the fish. Then he steals their God-is-mighty hymn and points it at the important people: judges turned into idiots, priests hauled off, chiefs staggering around drunk. Sing that verse.
JOB 13:1-28	Job: I have heard all of it, and I would rather take this straight to the Almighty. Performance review for his friends: liars, useless doctors, and honestly your best move is total silence. Have you considered that lying for God is still lying, and that he will cross-examine you too? Your greatest hits are proverbs of ashes. Also, could someone list the charges?
JOB 14:1-22	Human life, per Job: short, miserable, blooms like a flower and gets mowed. Compare a tree. Chop a tree down and it smells water and comes right back. A person goes down and stays down till the heavens run out. His pitch to God is weirdly sweet: stash me in Sheol, wait out your temper, set an alarm, call me and I will answer.
JOB 15:1-35	Eliphaz round two, and the nice-guy act is gone. You are full of east wind. Were you born before the hills? Did you sit in on God's private meetings? His theology got meaner too: God does not trust his holy ones. Then the set piece about the wicked guy: terrors, begging bread, fat thighs, condemned houses, tents burning. Totally not about you, Job.
JOB 16:1-22	Job's rating: you are the worst comforters alive. He could crank out their exact speeches, head-shaking included, if they had swapped seats, except he would have said something helpful. Then it gets brutal: God as a beast baring its teeth, as a wrestler grabbing his neck, as a shooting range with Job as the target. Earth, do not cover my blood.
JOB 17:1-16	Spirit gone, days done, grave made up. Job asks for a co-signer to shake hands and post bail, while looking directly at three men who will not. He is sick of being told the light is coming while standing in pitch dark. If rot is his dad and the worm is his mom, where is this hope, and who is going to see it?
JOB 18:1-21	Bildad is mad about being called an animal, and responds with the most heavily trapped speech in the book. Lamp out. Then: net on the feet, snare on the heel, noose in the dirt. The firstborn of death eats his limbs. He gets marched out of his tent to the king of terrors. Zero kids, zero grandkids. Delivered to a man who just buried ten.
JOB 19:1-29	Job has been keeping score: that is ten insults, and none of you looks embarrassed. In his version God is an invading army, road blocked, crown taken, hope yanked out by the roots, siege ramp against the tent. Then everybody is gone. Brothers, cousins, staff, a wife who cannot handle his breath. He wants it chiseled into rock, because his Redeemer is alive.
JOB 20:1-29	Zophar is back and admits up front he is answering fast, which explains a lot. Same thesis: bad guys do not get to enjoy it long. New material is the digestive tract. Sin tastes great, he stashes it under his tongue like candy, then it turns to cobra venom and the money comes right back up. A bronze arrow exits through his liver.
JOB 21:1-34	Job asks for five minutes and says they can mock him after. Then he kicks out the load-bearing wall. Look around: bad people are doing great. Kids set up for life, bulls that never miss, tambourines and harps, then a quick clean exit to Sheol, all while telling God to get lost. So how often does that lamp actually go out?
JOB 22:1-30	Eliphaz starts with a shrug: God gets nothing out of your righteousness anyway. Then he runs out of hints and just makes up charges. You seized collateral. No water for the thirsty, widows sent home empty, orphans' arms broken. None of that happened, but it makes the theory work. Then the pitch: dump your gold in the creek bed and let God be the treasure.
JOB 23:1-17	Job's issue is not theology anymore, it is finding the building. He would love a hearing. Case prepped, mouth loaded with arguments, and he is convinced God would listen instead of bulldozing him. Problem: east, nothing. West, nothing. North, south, cannot spot him. What scares him is that God answers to nobody.
JOB 24:1-25	Job goes full documentary crew. Guys moving property markers, stealing the orphan's donkey, holding the widow's ox as collateral. Poor people gleaning from the vineyards of the crooks who robbed them, sleeping naked in the cold, hugging a rock because there is no roof. Then the night shift: murderer up at dawn, adulterer waiting for dusk, burglars tunneling through walls.
JOB 25:1-6	Bildad's third and final speech is six verses and then he is out of the book forever. God has dominion, fear, armies you cannot count. So how is a human supposed to be clean? Even the moon is not bright and the stars are not pure. And you? You are a worm. He says worm twice, just to be clear.
JOB 26:1-14	Job goes full sarcasm: wow, thanks for all that help to the powerless, and the truckload of wisdom for a man with none. Then he takes Bildad's hymn and does it better. God stretches out the north over empty space and hangs the whole earth on nothing. Water packed into clouds that do not burst. Rahab run through. And that is just the outskirts.
JOB 27:1-23	Job swears an oath by the God who took away his justice, which honestly sums up the whole book. Not agreeing with them, not now, not ever. Then he preaches their sermon for them. Kids multiplied just to feed the sword. Silver piled up like dirt for somebody better to split. House built to moth standards. East wind takes him.
JOB 28:1-28	Time out for a poem, and it is a banger. Humans are incredible at digging things up: shafts sunk in the middle of nowhere, guys dangling on ropes in the dark, mountains flipped over by the roots, where no falcon has ever looked. Then the turn. Where do you mine wisdom? The deep says not here. Only God knows the address.
JOB 29:1-25	Job spends a whole chapter on the good old days. Lamp over his head, kids all around, oil pouring out of rock, a fancy way of saying business was excellent. He walks up to the city gate and young guys hide. And he says why he earned it: made widows sing, eyes for the blind, feet for the lame, predators' jaws broken open.
JOB 30:1-31	Cut to now. He is the entertainment for punks whose dads he would not have let near his sheep dogs, outcasts living in rock holes eating broom roots. He is their song. They spit in his face. The mob pours through him like a hole in a wall. He yells and God just stares back. Brother to jackals, roommate to ostriches.
JOB 31:1-40	Closing argument, done as a stack of self-curses, which takes nerve. I made a deal with my eyes about young women. If I lurked at a neighbour's door, let my wife grind for another man. Best bit is the staff: same God made me and them in the womb. Then he signs his name and demands the charge sheet, to wear as a crown.
JOB 32:1-22	The three friends give up, and the narrator says why: Job thinks he is righteous. Enter Elihu son of Barachel the Buzite, who has apparently been standing there this whole time getting madder at everyone. Mad at Job for defending himself instead of God. Mad at the friends for condemning Job without ever answering him. His own image for himself: a wineskin about to pop.
JOB 33:1-33	Elihu starts with reassurance nobody requested: relax, I am clay too, I will not be scary. Then he plays Job's argument back and answers it: God is bigger and does not owe you a report. But he does talk. In dreams, steering you off the road that ends in the pit. The bone pain is part of the lesson plan. Now zip it, Job.
JOB 34:1-37	Elihu addresses "you wise men," meaning himself plus anyone who nods. He quotes Job for real, then stretches it until Job sounds like he hangs out with criminals. Main argument, and it is procedural: nobody put God in charge of earth, so there is no boss he could be abusing. His sharpest jab: have you ever once tried saying I was wrong, teach me?
JOB 35:1-16	Elihu goes after the what-do-I-get-out-of-being-good question, and his answer is weirdly modern. Look up. The sky is taller than you. Your sin does not dent God and your good behaviour does not pay him. Both land on the people around you. Then a gorgeous line about the God who hands out songs in the night. Conclusion, same as always: Job talks too much.
JOB 36:1-33	Elihu wants more time because a man perfect in knowledge is right here. Meaning him. Same theology as the friends, better manners: chains are a lesson plan, ears opened, prosperity if you listen. One genuinely great line: God rescues the afflicted by means of the affliction. Then he slides into weather. Lightning held in God's hands and told where to hit.
JOB 37:1-24	Elihu's last speech is basically a live weather report and his own heart is pounding. Thunder under the whole sky, lightning to the ends of the earth, and nobody is expected to understand it. God tells the snow to fall and every human hand stops working. Ice arrives on God's breath. Then, mid-storm, somebody else starts talking.
JOB 38:1-41	The whirlwind shows up and God opens with: who is this darkening counsel with words he does not understand? Belt up, I am asking now. Where were you when I poured the foundations and the morning stars were singing? The gates of death, the home address of light, the warehouses where snow is stockpiled for war. Does rain have a dad?
JOB 39:1-30	The interrogation goes full nature documentary and honestly it rules. Do you run the birth schedule for mountain goats? Who cut the wild donkey loose in the salt flats? The ostrich dumps her eggs in the dust with zero fear, because God never issued her any wisdom, and still smokes a horse in a footrace. The horse hears the trumpet and goes Aha.
JOB 40:1-24	God stops long enough for a reply and Job gives the shortest speech in the book: I am small, hand over mouth, I am done. Not an exit, apparently. Then the question at the bottom of the whole thing, out loud: are you going to convict me so you can be the innocent one? Then, instead of an argument, behemoth.
JOB 41:1-34	A full chapter on Leviathan and God is having a blast. Catch him with a hook? Keep him as a pet for your daughters? Touch him once and you will never sign up again. Plates sealed so tight no air gets between them, eyes like the eyelids of dawn, torches out of the mouth. Iron is straw. He laughs at javelins.
JOB 42:1-17	Job's reply is six verses and gives up everything except the charge itself: I knew you by rumour, now I have seen you. Then the twist nobody saw coming. God is furious at Eliphaz and the other two, because they got him wrong and Job got him right. Their penance: go ask the guy you lectured for three rounds to pray for you.
LUK 1:1-35	Luke starts like a proper reporter: dear Theophilus, I checked everything. Then an old priest, Zacharias, and his wife Elizabeth, no kids, both past it. He draws the incense duty, and Gabriel is standing by the altar. You're having a son, call him John. Zacharias asks for proof and gets muted for nine months. Next time on Luke: Mary's turn.
LUK 1:36-80	Gabriel's proof is Elizabeth, six months along. Mary just says yes. Elizabeth's baby kicks the second Mary speaks. Then Mary sings, and it is not a sweet song: proud people scattered, rulers yanked off thrones, rich people sent home with nothing. Later the naming turns into an argument until Zacharias writes it on a tablet, and his voice comes back mid-prophecy.
LUK 2:1-25	Rome orders a census, and Luke even names the governor. Joseph hauls the pregnant Mary to Bethlehem. The birth gets one sentence, and the baby ends up in a feeding trough. The announcement goes to guys working the night shift, and the sign is deliberately unglamorous: a baby in a trough. Next time on Luke: Simeon talks.
LUK 2:26-52	Simeon was promised he wouldn't die before seeing the Christ, and now he's holding him, and basically says: okay, I'm done. His song is about a light for every nation. Then he turns to Mary with the part nobody wants: a sword is going through you. Twelve years go by in a verse, then they lose him for three days.
LUK 3:1-38	Luke dates it properly: Tiberius, Pilate. Then John in the wilderness, quoting Isaiah and opening with "you brood of vipers." Here's the bit Luke keeps: the crowd actually asks what to do. Got two coats? Give one away. Tax collectors, stop padding the bill. Soldiers, live on your pay. Then the family tree, running backwards, and it doesn't stop at Abraham.
LUK 4:1-23	Forty days, nothing to eat, three offers. Turn this rock into bread. Bow to me and every kingdom is yours. Jump off the temple, here's a verse about angels. Scripture, scripture, scripture, and the devil leaves, though Luke says only for now. At Nazareth he reads Isaiah, sits down, and says: today, this just happened. Next time on Luke: someone asks whose son he is.
LUK 4:24-44	What flips the room isn't a claim about himself. It's two foreigners: Elijah went to a widow in Sidon, Elisha cleaned up Naaman. The congregation drags him to a cliff, and he walks straight through them. Over at Capernaum everyone keeps using the same word: authority. Simon's mother-in-law gets her fever told off, and he won't let the demons advertise for him.
LUK 5:1-39	The crowd shoves him into a boat, so he preaches from it. Simon points out they fished all night, does it anyway, and hauls in so much the nets rip. He drops to his knees and asks Jesus to leave. He gets hired instead. Later a paralyzed guy comes through the roof tiles: forgiveness first, argument second, healing to settle it.
LUK 6:1-26	Grain field, Sabbath, snacking disciples, complaint filed. He counters with David and the holy bread. Next Sabbath, a guy with a withered right hand, and Jesus makes it theatrical: stand up here in the middle. They're furious. Then Luke's own detail: before picking the twelve, he prays all night. Blessed: poor, hungry, crying, hated. Woe: rich, full, laughing, popular. Next time on Luke: enemies.
LUK 6:27-49	It gets harder, not easier. Love your enemies. Bless people who curse you. Somebody robs you, don't chase it. Lend without expecting it back, because loving people who love you is a bar sinners already clear. Don't judge. The plank in your own eye versus the speck in someone else's, which he calls hypocrisy.
LUK 7:1-24	The centurion never shows up. First he sends the local elders, who vouch for him. Then he sends friends to say don't bother coming in, just say the word, I know how orders work. Then Nain, which only Luke has: a funeral, a widow burying her only son, and nobody asks for anything. Next time on Luke: he talks about John.
LUK 7:25-50	What did you go out to see, a guy in nice clothes? You went for a prophet, the greatest man ever, who still gets outranked by the smallest person in the kingdom. This generation won't play any game: John didn't eat, so he's possessed; Jesus eats, so he's a drunk. Then a woman with a bad reputation cries on his feet.
LUK 8:1-27	Luke names who's paying: Mary Magdalene, Joanna, Susanna, bankrolling the whole thing themselves. Then the seed story: path, rock, thorns, good dirt. His explanation names what does the choking: worries, money, and having a nice time. His family shows up, can't get through the crowd, and he redefines family instead of going out. Next time on Luke: a naked man in the tombs.
LUK 8:28-56	Jesus asks the man's name and gets "Legion." The demons bargain: not the abyss, put us in the pigs. Locals find the man dressed, and are so freaked out they ask Jesus to leave. Back across the water, two stories tangle: Jairus on his knees about his dying twelve-year-old, and a woman who's bled twelve years. Jesus stops everything to ask who touched him.
LUK 9:1-31	The twelve get sent out with nothing. No staff, no bag, not even a spare shirt. Herod hears and says the awkward line himself: I beheaded John, so who is this guy? The quiet retreat to Bethsaida lasts until the crowd spots them. Five loaves, two fish, seat them in fifties, twelve baskets left. Next time on Luke: the cloud.
LUK 9:32-62	The three almost sleep through it, wake into the glory, and Peter suggests putting up tents, which Luke flatly says he didn't understand. Cloud, voice, then just Jesus. While everyone's marvelling, he says he's going to be handed over, and Luke says they were too scared to ask. So naturally they start arguing about who's greatest, and get handed a kid.
LUK 10:1-21	Seventy more get sent out, and the rules are tighter: no wallet, no sandals, and don't stop to chat with anyone on the road. Stay in the first house that takes you, eat what's put in front of you, announce the kingdom. They come back buzzing that even demons obeyed, and Jesus says that's the least of it. Next time on Luke: a lawyer.
LUK 10:22-42	A lawyer turns up to test him and ends up answering his own question. Then he pushes it: define neighbour. Luke says he was trying to let himself off. A priest sees the beaten man and crosses the street. A Levite does the same. Then a Samaritan does the whole job. Asked which was the neighbour, the lawyer can't say the word Samaritan.
LUK 11:1-28	They ask for a prayer, so he gives them a short one. Then he explains prayer with, who gets it not because they're friends but because he won't quit. He casts out a mute demon and the crowd splits: some say Beelzebul, some demand a sign. He points out Satan firing Satan is a terrible business plan. Next time on Luke: Jonah.
LUK 11:29-54	They want a sign, they get Jonah, plus two embarrassing comparisons: a foreign queen who crossed the world for Solomon, and a whole city that repented for one grumpy prophet. Then a Pharisee invites him to dinner, which goes badly. He's criticised for skipping the handwashing and comes back with cups scrubbed on the outside and full of extortion inside.
LUK 12:1-30	The crowd's so big the first thing he says is: watch out for hypocrisy. Whatever you mutter in a back room ends up broadcast from the roof. Then a guy tries to get him to settle an inheritance fight. Cue the rich farmer, whose whole speech is I, I, I. Next time on Luke: keep the lamps lit.
LUK 12:31-59	Chase the kingdom, sell your stuff, give it away. Then the waiting: belt on, lamps lit, like staff waiting up for a boss coming back from a wedding, who then flips it and serves them dinner. Peter asks, is this for us or everyone? Answer, sideways: the manager who figured the boss was running late and started throwing his weight around.
LUK 13:1-35	People bring him two disasters and both times he refuses to say the dead deserved it, then turns it on the living anyway. A fig tree on death row gets one more year. On a Sabbath he straightens a woman, and asks which of them lets their ox go thirsty on a Saturday. Told Herod's out to kill him, he calls Herod a fox.
LUK 14:1-35	Sabbath dinner at a Pharisee's, and Luke says flat out they were watching him. So he asks first: is healing legal today? He heals the guy anyway. Then he critiques the seating chart: take the worst seat and let the host move you up. To the host: quit inviting people who can pay you back.
LUK 15:1-32	The whole chapter starts as a complaint: he eats with the wrong people. So he tells three stories. Shepherd ditches ninety-nine sheep, throws a party. Woman tears the house apart for one coin, throws a party. Then the younger son blows his inheritance, ends up jealous of pig food, and writes a speech he never finishes, because his dad is already sprinting.
LUK 16:1-31	Weirdest parable in Luke. A manager goes around slashing his boss's invoices so the debtors will owe him favours, and gets praised for being sharp. The point isn't the fraud, it's the forward planning. Then the flip side: a man in purple linen, and Lazarus at his gate covered in sores the dogs lick. The rich man, still issuing orders, asks for water.
LUK 17:1-37	Forgiveness quota: seven times a day. The apostles ask for a faith upgrade, and get told mustard-seed faith would rip up a sycamore. Then ten lepers, and exactly one comes back to say thanks. Luke saves the punchline for the end of the sentence: he was a Samaritan. Don't go back for your stuff. Remember Lot's wife.
LUK 18:1-43	The persistence lesson runs on a judge who admits he doesn't fear God and just wants the widow to stop showing up. The humility lesson runs on a Pharisee whose prayer lists everyone else's flaws, and a tax collector who manages one line. A rich ruler hears "sell everything" and walks off sad. At Jericho a blind man out-shouts the people hushing him.
LUK 19:1-23	Zacchaeus, senior tax collector, rich and short, ends up in a tree. Jesus stops right underneath and invites himself to dinner. The crowd grumbles about the company he keeps. Zacchaeus answers with numbers: half to the poor, quadruple back to anyone he shook down. Then a parable: a man goes abroad to be made king. Next time on Luke: the reckoning.
LUK 19:24-48	The coin goes to the guy who already has ten, and the king's enemies are executed in front of him. Then the entry: an unridden colt, cloaks on the road, disciples yelling about everything they've seen. Told to shut them up, he says the stones will take over. Mid-parade he stops and cries over a coming siege. He clears the temple, then just teaches there daily, untouchable while the crowd's listening.
LUK 20:1-47	They demand his credentials, and he counters with a question about John instead. Then the vineyard: the owner's son murdered, and the crowd's horrified gasp only makes it land harder. Spies open with compliments and ask about Caesar's tax, and get handed a coin. Then the Sadducees with their seven-brothers scenario, answered with Moses at the burning bush. A scribe compliments him, and the questions stop. He asks about David, then calls out the scribes: long robes, best seats, widows' houses eaten.
LUK 21:1-38	Opens with a widow dropping in two tiny coins, which outweighs every large donation in the room. Then the temple stonework, and he says it's all coming down. Notice the order: arrests come first. Synagogues, prisons, kings, and don't rehearse a speech. Your own family will turn you in. Some of you will die. Armies around Jerusalem means run, and it'll be brutal for pregnant and nursing women.
LUK 22:1-36	Luke says outright that Satan got into Judas, and off he goes to name a price. The meal setup is weirdly covert: follow the man with a water jar, take the upstairs room. At the table: a cup, bread as his body, then the line that his betrayer's hand is on the table right now. They argue over who's the greatest, and get told the greatest one serves. Simon hears he'll be sifted like wheat, told he has until the rooster. Next time on Luke: the Mount.
LUK 22:37-71	They produce two swords and he says "that's enough," not encouragement. On the Mount of Olives he asks for the cup, then defers, and Luke alone reports the angel and sweat like drops of blood. The disciples are asleep from grief. Judas names the kiss before it happens. Somebody takes off a servant's ear, and only in Luke does it go back on. Then the courtyard, three denials, the rooster, and Jesus turns and looks at Peter.
LUK 23:1-28	The charges are political, because blasphemy won't fly with Rome: tax refusal, calling himself a king. Pilate finds nothing, hears the word Galilee, and ships the problem to Herod. Herod's thrilled, gets silence, mocks him, sends him back. The two of them become friends that day. Pilate finds no capital offence and offers a flogging instead. The crowd asks for Barabbas, jailed for insurrection and murder, and shouts until it wins. Next time on Luke: the Skull.
LUK 23:29-56	What he tells the mourning women is about their future, not his. At the Skull he's crucified between two criminals, and the first thing he says is a request that they be forgiven. The mockery comes in layers: rulers, soldiers, one of the men beside him. It's the other criminal who says the only accurate thing anyone says all day: this man did nothing wrong. He's promised Paradise. Then darkness, the torn curtain. Luke's centurion says "righteous man."
LUK 24:1-26	The women turn up at dawn with burial spices for a body that isn't there, and get a question instead of an announcement: why are you looking for the living among the dead? Luke names them, then reports the apostles wrote it off as nonsense. Peter runs to the tomb anyway, finds the linen lying there alone, and goes home confused. Then the Emmaus road, where two disciples explain the crucifixion to the crucified guy himself. Next time on Luke: the bread.
LUK 24:27-53	He walks them through Moses and the prophets, then acts like he's moving on, and they talk him into staying. He breaks the bread, and that's the moment they recognise him, right before he vanishes. Back in Jerusalem the eleven are already saying he's appeared to Simon. Jesus turns up in the room, and they assume ghost, so he eats grilled fish to settle it. Then the brief: forgiveness to every nation, you're the witnesses, wait for power.
MAT 1:1-25	Forty-two rounds of who-fathered-who, and the family tree quietly includes Tamar, Rahab, Ruth and the woman Matthew will only call Uriah's wife. Then the tidiness breaks. Mary's pregnant, Joseph knows it isn't his, and he plans to end it quietly. Angel, dream: marry her, name him Jesus. He does.
MAT 2:1-23	Star-watchers stroll into Jerusalem and ask the current king where the new king is. Herod goes pale, gets Bethlehem out of the scholars, and asks them to send word so he can worship too. Nobody should have believed that. Gold, frankincense, myrrh, then a dream and the long way home. Joseph runs for Egypt. Herod kills every boy in Bethlehem under two. The family comes back only once he's dead.
MAT 3:1-17	John's in the desert in camel hair eating bugs and honey, and the whole Jordan region shows up to get dunked. Pharisees and Sadducees arrive and he opens with "you offspring of vipers." Being Abraham's kids counts for nothing. Then the guy whose sandals he isn't fit to carry gets in line. John says this is backwards. Jesus insists. Sky opens, dove drops, voice says: my Son.
MAT 4:1-25	Forty days, no food, and here comes the devil: rocks into bread, jump off the temple and let the angels catch you, bow once and own every kingdom on earth. He even quotes scripture for the jump. Jesus answers all three with scripture. Devil leaves, angels bring food. John gets arrested, so Jesus moves to Capernaum and preaches John's exact line. Two sets of brothers drop their nets and their dad.
MAT 5:1-24	Up the hill, sits down, and congratulates everyone who looks like they're losing: the broke in spirit, the grieving, the gentle, the ones starving for justice, the merciful, the clear-hearted, the peacemakers, anyone getting trashed on his account. You're salt, don't go bland. You're light, get on the stand. He's not scrapping the law. The bar goes up instead: call your brother a fool and it files with murder. Next time on Matthew: it gets harder.
MAT 5:25-48	The bar keeps climbing. Settle with the guy suing you before a judge gets involved. Staring with intent already counts. An eye or a hand is a cheaper loss than all of you. Oaths: quit, you can't even change your hair color. Hit? Other cheek. Sued for your coat? Hand over the cloak. Forced one mile, walk two. Love your enemies, because God's sun comes up on everybody.
MAT 6:1-34	Three ways to be religious for an audience get shredded: giving with a trumpet, praying loudly on the corner, fasting with a face you wrecked on purpose. All three already got paid, in attention. Prayer isn't a word count, plus a note about forgiving people first. Your heart follows your money. You can't work for God and cash both. Then: quit panicking. Birds don't farm. Wildflowers dress better than Solomon.
MAT 7:1-29	Judge people and the same ruler comes back for you. Plank in your own eye first, then the speck. Don't throw pearls at pigs. Ask, seek, knock, because no dad hands his kid a rock when the kid asked for bread. The good gate is narrow. Fake prophets come dressed as sheep, so check the fruit. "Lord, look at all my miracles" gets "I never knew you." Crowd's stunned: he doesn't talk like the scribes.
MAT 8:1-34	A leper asks and Jesus actually touches him. A Roman officer asks for a remote healing using chain-of-command logic and gets called the best faith in Israel. Peter's mother-in-law is healed and starts serving food. Two would-be followers get cold water: no pillow, let the dead bury the dead. He naps through a storm, then tells the weather to stop. Demons move into pigs, the pigs run off a cliff, and the town asks him to go.
MAT 9:1-38	A paralyzed guy shows up on a bed and gets his sins forgiven, which the scribes decide is blasphemy without saying it out loud. So Jesus heals him too, to prove the point. Then he hires a tax collector and tells the Pharisees to go look up "mercy, not sacrifice." Nobody fasts at a wedding. A dead girl's dad interrupts, a woman sick twelve years grabs his coat, and the paid mourners laugh right before he raises her.
MAT 10:1-42	Twelve guys get named and handed power over demons and disease, and Matthew tags Judas as the traitor right there in the roster. Packing list: nothing. Town won't listen? Shake the dust off and go. Then the honest part: courts, floggings, governors, kings, family turning you in. Don't rehearse a speech, the words will show up. Sparrows cost pennies and God still counts them. He brought a sword, not peace.
MAT 11:1-30	John's in prison and sends a message asking, basically, did I bet on the right guy. Jesus doesn't say yes. He sends back a list: blind see, lame walk, lepers clean, deaf hear, dead up, poor getting good news. Then he talks up the guy who just doubted him: more than a prophet, Elijah if you'll take it. John didn't eat, so he's possessed. Jesus eats, so he's a drunk.
MAT 12:1-28	Disciples grab a snack in a grain field on the Sabbath and the Pharisees write it up. Jesus counters with David eating the holy bread and priests who technically work every Sabbath. Next scene, a withered hand and a loaded question. Who wouldn't pull their sheep out of a pit on a Saturday? He fixes the hand, and the Pharisees walk out and start planning a murder. Next time on Matthew: he demolishes their Satan theory.
MAT 12:29-50	You can't loot a strong guy's house without tying him up first. Every sin is forgivable except talking against the Holy Spirit. The tree shows in the fruit, and the throwaway comments count. They demand a sign and get Jonah: three days in the fish, three days in the ground. Then the creepy bit: an evicted demon comes back with seven worse. His mom and brothers are outside; he points at his disciples instead.
MAT 13:1-30	Crowd's so big he has to teach from a boat. Story one is about soil: seed hits the path, the rocks, the thorns, and finally ground that pays back a hundredfold. The crowd looks without seeing, he says, then explains it anyway: snatched, shallow, choked, or actually growing. Story two: somebody plants weeds in the wheat overnight, and the owner won't let anyone pull them. Next time on Matthew: the sorting, plus one very expensive pearl.
MAT 13:31-58	More comparisons, all small stuff: a mustard seed that turns into a tree birds nest in, yeast worked into three measures of flour. Inside, the disciples want the weeds explained and get a straight decoder: sower, field, good seed, harvest, angels, furnace. Then two guys who sell literally everything, one for buried treasure, one for a pearl. He goes home, gets the classic hometown treatment, and barely does anything there.
MAT 14:1-36	Herod hears about Jesus and nervously decides John's back from the dead. Backstory: a birthday dance, a rash oath in front of guests, and John's head on a platter. Jesus leaves by boat and the crowd beats him there on foot. Disciples say send them home; he says you feed them, and five loaves cover five thousand. Then he walks across the lake at night and Peter sinks the second the wind spooks him.
MAT 15:1-39	The Jerusalem crowd complains that his disciples don't wash their hands. He fires back about their loophole: call your money a gift to God and you never have to support your parents. Your mouth is an exit, not an entrance. A Canaanite woman asks help for her daughter, gets ignored, gets a line about dogs, and comes back with the crumbs and wins. Then four thousand fed off seven loaves, with the disciples asking where bread would come from.
MAT 16:1-28	They want a sign from heaven. He points out they can predict tomorrow's weather off a red sky but can't read the moment they're standing in. He warns about the yeast of the Pharisees and they panic about forgetting bread. Then: who do you say I am. Peter nails it, gets renamed the rock and handed the keys, then tells Jesus he isn't going to die and gets called Satan.
MAT 17:1-27	Up a mountain, his face lights up like the sun and his clothes go blinding white. Moses and Elijah show up for a chat. Peter, in the wildest moment of his life, suggests putting up tents. A cloud rolls in, a voice says listen to him, and all three hit the dirt. On the way down, Elijah already came and got mistreated, meaning John. Then a boy the disciples couldn't help, and a temple tax that turns up inside a fish.
MAT 18:1-35	They ask which of them is the greatest, which kind of answers itself, and Jesus parks a kid in the middle of the group. Trip up one of these little ones and you'd be better off in the lake wearing a millstone. A shepherd ditches ninety-nine sheep to find one stray. Got a problem? Talk alone, then witnesses, then the assembly. Peter tries to cap forgiveness at seven, gets seventy times seven, plus a guy forgiven a fortune who throttles a coworker over pocket change.
MAT 19:1-30	The trap question: can a man divorce his wife for any reason he likes. He goes back to Genesis instead of the rulebook, and says Moses only allowed divorce because people are hard-hearted. The disciples' takeaway is that marriage sounds like a bad deal, which was not the point. Kids get shooed away and Jesus overrules it. Then a guy with a spotless record gets told to sell everything and walks off sad, because he's loaded.
MAT 20:1-34	A vineyard owner hires workers all day, then pays everyone the same denarius, latecomers first, in full view. The dawn crew is furious. He points out they got exactly what they signed for. On the road to Jerusalem, Jesus lays out the ending: handed over, mocked, whipped, crucified, raised. James and John's mom asks for the two best seats. The other ten fume, and he explains here the boss is the servant. Two blind guys in Jericho shout over a crowd, and he stops for them.
MAT 21:1-23	Two disciples get sent to grab a donkey and a colt, which Matthew calls the prophecy about a humble king. Cloaks hit the road, everyone's yelling Hosanna. Then the temple, where he throws out the merchants, saying this was supposed to be a house of prayer. He heals people right there. The chief priests complain about kids chanting. He finds a fig tree with no figs and kills it, then tells the disciples faith moves mountains. Next time on Matthew: he asks a question first.
MAT 21:24-46	He makes them a deal: tell me if John's baptism came from heaven or from people, and I'll tell you where my authority comes from. They go with "we don't know," and he says great, then I'm not telling you either. Then a story: two sons, one says no and goes anyway, one says sure and doesn't. Second story: tenants kill the servants sent for the rent, then murder the owner's son figuring the land becomes theirs. They'd arrest him, but the crowd loves him.
MAT 22:1-46	King throws a wedding, the invited guests can't be bothered, and some kill the messengers, so he wipes them out and fills the hall with whoever's on the road. Three traps in a row follow. The tax question arrives smothered in compliments, and he asks whose face is on the coin. The Sadducees bring a made-up scenario about one woman and seven brothers, and get told they've misread it. A lawyer asks for the biggest commandment and gets two. Then he asks about David calling his son Lord.
MAT 23:1-39	Do what they teach, don't copy what they do. They write heavy rules and carry none, grab the best seats and love being called Rabbi. You, he says, collect no titles. Then the woes: they slam the kingdom door, tithe their herb garden while skipping justice and mercy, filter out gnats and swallow camels, scrub the outside of the cup, look like gorgeous tombs stuffed with bones, and build monuments to prophets their own ancestors killed. Then the anger drops out: a hen, chicks that wouldn't come.
MAT 24:1-27	The disciples do the tourist thing about the temple, and he says none of it will be left standing. Up on the Mount of Olives they ask when. First answer: watch out for fakes. Wars, famines, plagues, earthquakes? Just the early contractions. What's coming is arrest, execution, being hated everywhere, people turning each other in, and love going cold, with the message reaching every nation first. When Daniel's abomination shows up, get out of Judea. Next time on Matthew: what the real arrival looks like.
MAT 24:28-51	No tip-off needed. Vultures find the body, lightning crosses the sky. Sun and moon go dark, stars fall, the Son of Man shows up, angels gathering the chosen. Fig tree leafs out, summer's coming. Nobody gets the date, not even the angels. Think Noah: everybody eating and marrying right up to the flood, one taken, one left. Stay awake, burglars don't book appointments. And the manager in charge better be feeding people, not beating them.
MAT 25:1-25	Ten bridesmaids wait on a bridegroom running late, and the only thing separating the smart five from the rest is a spare bottle of oil. Everyone falls asleep. Midnight, the shout goes up, door shuts while the oil-less five are out buying more. Watch, he says. Then the money story: three servants get five, two and one talents. First two double it, same "well done" for both. Third buries his, blames a hard master. Next time on Matthew: how that goes over.
MAT 25:26-46	It goes badly. If the boss is really that harsh, he says, you'd have banked it for interest. The talent goes to the servant already holding ten, and the third guy is out. Then the big scene: Son of Man on the throne, sorting nations like a shepherd splitting sheep from goats. The test isn't religious at all: food, water, a bed for a stranger, clothes, a hospital visit, a prison visit. Both sides ask when they saw him, and get the same answer: the least of these was me.
MAT 26:1-39	Two days out, Jesus says flatly what's coming, while the priests agree to grab him quietly. At Bethany a woman pours costly ointment on his head; the disciples do the math about the poor, but Jesus calls it burial prep. Judas names his price: thirty silver coins. At the meal he names his betrayer, hands out bread and wine as his body and blood, and tells Peter he's got until the rooster. In Gethsemane he asks for the cup to pass. Next time on Matthew: the arrest.
MAT 26:40-75	Three times he prays, three times his three closest friends are asleep. Judas shows up with an armed crowd and marks him with a kiss. Somebody swings a sword, takes off a servant's ear, and Jesus shuts it down: no angels needed. Every disciple runs. The trial can't find a witness until two cite a line about tearing down the temple. Under oath his answer makes the high priest rip his own clothes. They spit on him and hit him. Peter denies him three times and leaves crying.
MAT 27:1-32	Morning, and the priests hand him to Pilate. Judas tries to return the silver, gets told that's his problem, throws it into the sanctuary and hangs himself. The priests buy a burial field for strangers with it. Before Pilate, Jesus answers one question, then goes silent. Pilate offers Barabbas or Jesus; his wife warns of a dream. The crowd picks Barabbas and shouts crucifixion, and Pilate washes his hands, which fixes nothing. Soldiers mock him and pull Simon of Cyrene to carry the cross. Next time on Matthew: Golgotha.
MAT 27:33-66	He turns down the wine mixed with gall and is crucified between two robbers, soldiers rolling dice for his clothes. Everybody says: save yourself, come down. Three hours of darkness end with him crying out that God has abandoned him, mistaken for a call to Elijah, then he's gone. The temple curtain rips top to bottom, the ground shakes, tombs open. The centurion says it: this really was the Son of God. The women watch from a distance. Joseph of Arimathaea gives up his own tomb; the priests seal it.
MAT 28:1-20	Two women reach the tomb at dawn and walk into an earthquake, an angel shoving the stone aside and sitting on it, and guards frozen stiff. The message is four lines: not here, risen, come look, go tell them Galilee. They run off scared and thrilled, and Jesus meets them himself. The guards tell the priests, who pay for a story admitting they slept on the job. The eleven worship him in Galilee, some still doubting. Last word: all authority, every nation, baptize, teach, I'm with you to the end.
PRO 1:1-33	The book opens with a syllabus: wisdom, justice, street smarts for beginners, and how to crack a riddle. Prerequisite: fear of Yahweh. Then dad's first real lesson is about peer pressure and it is shockingly violent. If the guys say come lurk with us and we will split the loot, the trap they are setting closes on them.
PRO 2:1-22	One giant if-then sentence. If you take these words, stash them, and dig for wisdom the way people dig for silver, then fear of Yahweh becomes something you actually get instead of just repeating. After that discretion is basically your bodyguard, keeping you clear of guys who enjoy being evil and a house with a downhill exit and no return traffic.
PRO 3:1-35	The chapter everybody knows half of. Trust Yahweh with your whole heart, stop leaning on your own take, and he straightens the road, which sounds easy right up until you try it. Discipline gets rebranded as affection, a line that hits differently depending on the dad. Then, out of nowhere: pay your neighbour today, do not tell him to come back tomorrow.
PRO 4:1-27	Dad cites his sources, which is classy: this is what my dad told me. The pitch is buy wisdom at any price, everything you own if that is the number. On bad company he does not hedge: do not walk that road carefully, go around, because those guys cannot fall asleep until they have tripped somebody. The good road brightens like sunrise.
PRO 5:1-23	The adultery chapter, and it runs on aftertaste. Her lips drip honey, her mouth is smoother than oil, and the customer review is wormwood and a double-edged sword. It even hands you the deathbed regret in advance: man, I hated instruction, I blew off every teacher. The counteroffer is drink from your own well and keep your springs on your own property.
PRO 6:1-35	Assorted emergencies chapter. Co-signing a stranger's loan is treated like a hostage crisis: do not sleep, wiggle out like a bird escaping a net. Then the lazy guy gets a field trip to watch an ant, who has zero managers and has already finished the summer stockpile, while your ruin shows up like an armed robber after a little nap.
PRO 7:1-27	Dad quits the theory and tells a story, from his window, through the lattice, a detail he absolutely did not have to include. Kid with no sense takes the route past a certain corner at dusk. The woman who meets him gets the best speech in the chapter: my husband is out of town with a bag of money till the full moon.
PRO 8:1-36	Wisdom grabs the mic and makes her own case, posted up where the roads meet. Nothing crooked in her mouth, better return than silver and rubies, and a straight-up flex that kings rule by her. Then the part theologians never stopped fighting about: she was around before the deeps, before the mountains got set down, before there was even dust.
PRO 9:1-18	Two dinner invites, printed in the same font on purpose. Wisdom builds a house, carves seven pillars, sets the table and sends staff out to yell for the clueless. Folly sits in her own doorway and uses literally the same words, and her only menu item is that stolen water tastes sweet. The difference is not the pitch, it is the guest list.
PRO 10:1-32	The one-liners kick off, and the format is hard binary: righteous on one side, wicked on the other, no middle. Main obsessions are work and mouths. Sleeping through harvest is framed as a personality defect, not a scheduling issue. Pile up enough words and you will never run short of sin. Best line: a lazy employee is vinegar on the teeth.
PRO 11:1-31	Opens with rigged scales, because nothing exposes your actual theology faster than a thumb on the weights. Gossip gets an actual definition, betraying a confidence, which beats vaguely calling it bad. The generosity math should not work but does: scatter and you get more, hoard the grain and the whole neighbourhood curses you by name. Then: a gold ring in a pig's nose.
PRO 12:1-28	The collection hits its stride and starts name-calling, because hating correction is not unfortunate here, it is stupid. There is a quietly lovely line about a righteous man taking care of his animal. The lazy hunter gets caught not for failing to hunt but for not bothering to cook what he shot. The fool shows his annoyance the same day.
PRO 13:1-25	Mouths, money, and the rod. Guard your mouth, guard your life. Fling your lips open, you are done. Sharp pair about faking it: one guy performing rich on nothing, another performing broke on a fortune. The famous one shows up here: hope deferred makes the heart sick. Also, hang out with wise people and you get wise, which is either encouraging or brutal.
PRO 14:1-35	This chapter keeps admitting the system is not tidy. Your heart knows its own bitterness and will not hand it to a stranger. Laughter can be covering something. There is a road that looks totally correct and ends in death, which quietly undercuts every confident proverb around it. No oxen means a clean stall, and also no harvest.
PRO 15:1-33	A whole chapter about tone of voice, which sounds impossible to stretch to thirty-three verses and somehow works. The gentle answer defusing rage kicks it off, and a well-timed word is a pleasure, not a chore. Then the comparison that carries this book's economics: better a plate of herbs where there is love than steak served with hate.
PRO 16:1-33	This one is about the gap between planning and happening. You pick the route, God moves your feet. Everybody's ways look spotless to the guy walking them, which is exactly why somebody else weighs the motives. Then the greatest hits, back to back: pride before destruction, nice words as a honeycomb. Best is the quietest: controlling your own temper beats conquering a city.
PRO 17:1-28	Opens with the single most convincing argument in the book: a dry crust in peace beats a banquet with a fight going on. Great concrete conflict rule too, starting a quarrel is breaching a dam, so shut it before the water is moving. Also it is safer to run into a bear whose cubs got stolen than a fool mid-meltdown. And the last verse is weirdly generous to fools: keep your mouth shut and people will just assume you are smart.
PRO 18:1-24	Mostly a chapter about talking, and it is shockingly sharp about how arguments actually go. A fool gets zero pleasure from understanding and all his pleasure from sharing his opinion, a sentence that has aged extremely well. And there is real procedural insight: whoever talks first sounds totally right, until somebody cross-examines him. The rich guy's wall is unscalable in his own imagination, six words doing serious damage.
PRO 19:1-29	Poverty is the running theme and the treatment is bleak. Money collects friends. Broke guys lose even their relatives, and chase them with pleas that reach nobody. The counterweight is a single line: pity the poor and you have made a loan to God, who settles up. The bit about the hot-tempered guy is uncomfortably practical: bail him out and congratulations, you have signed up to do it again. And then the sluggard, hand in the dish, unable to see the point of the trip back.
PRO 20:1-30	Wine is a mocker and beer is a brawler, which is the entire hangover in six words. The genuinely interesting thread is how little you know yourself: who can claim a clean heart, and the human spirit described as God's flashlight going through your drawers. One bit of straight comedy: the buyer going nah, it is junk, it is junk, and then bragging about the deal the second he is around the corner.
PRO 21:1-31	Opens by describing a king's heart as plumbing, a water channel God redirects wherever he wants, which is comforting for everyone except the king. Doing justice gets rated above sacrifice, a bolder move than it looks in a book this pious. Lot of housing advice too: the corner of the roof beats sharing a house with a contentious woman, and failing that, literal desert. And the last verse is the whole book compressed: prep the horse anyway, but the result is not yours.
PRO 22:1-29	A good reputation beats money, and rich and poor came off the same assembly line. There is the train-up-a-child verse, and the cold economic take that the borrower is the lender's servant, which needs no theology at all. The sluggard returns with the greatest excuse in scripture: there is a lion. Outside. In the street. Then the format shifts and the collection announces itself as thirty sayings, the best of which warns that God will take the poor man's court case personally.
PRO 23:1-35	Dinner etiquette for the ambitious, and it is grim. If your host is cheap, the food he is pushing is food you will be throwing up later, along with all your compliments. Do not wear yourself out getting rich, because money grows eagle wings and leaves. And finally the wine passage, the best writing about drinking anywhere: who has got bruises he cannot explain and bloodshot eyes? It ends on the guy who cannot feel the beating he took and is already lining up the next round.
PRO 24:1-34	House built by wisdom, rooms furnished by knowledge, wars won by having enough advisors, all fair. Then the chapter does something this book mostly dodges and demands you actually do something: go rescue the people being marched off to die, and do not try the we-did-not-know line, because the guy who weighs hearts already thought about that. A righteous man falls seven times and gets back up, which is permission, not a performance target. Then the closing walk past the sluggard's vineyard: thorns, nettles, wall caved in.
PRO 25:1-28	Second collection, credited to Hezekiah's copy department, and the imagery gets a serious upgrade. Starts at court with genuinely useful advice: do not seat yourself high, because getting invited up beats getting moved down while the prince watches. Then the metaphors come in a burst. A well-timed word is gold apples in silver settings. Singing cheerful songs at someone grieving is like stealing their coat in the cold. A guy with no self-control is a city with the walls knocked down.
PRO 26:1-28	Twenty-eight verses on fools and the editor has stopped being nice. Honour on a fool sits like snow in July. Two verses back to back give completely opposite instructions about answering him, which is either a scribe's joke or an honest admission that both options stink. Best image: a fool going back to his folly is a dog returning to its vomit, which nobody has ever improved on. Butting into a fight that is not yours is grabbing a strange dog by the ears.
PRO 27:1-27	A chapter of unusually great sentences. Don't brag about tomorrow, and let somebody else do the praising. Open rebuke beats hidden love, and a friend's wounds get called faithful, the nicest thing this book says about criticism. A nearby neighbour beats a faraway brother on the day it all goes wrong. Iron sharpens iron. Sheol, Abaddon and human eyeballs are the three things never satisfied. Grind a fool in a mortar and you still just have fool paste.
PRO 28:1-28	Opens with the wicked sprinting away from absolutely nobody, the sharpest image in the whole collection. After that it's mostly money and how it warps whoever's holding it. Rich guy thinks he's brilliant; the broke guy with sense sees right through him. There's a rare tender line: hiding your sins doesn't work, owning them and dropping them gets mercy. And a political shot that hasn't aged a day: a bad ruler over defenceless people is a roaring lion and a charging bear.
PRO 29:1-27	Basically a chapter about government. The people cheer or groan depending entirely on who's in charge. A king who judges the poor fairly keeps his throne, one who takes bribes dismantles the country. Elsewhere it gets personal: flattery is a net laid for your feet, a fool dumps out all his anger while a wise guy holds it, and taking a fool to court gets you rage and sneering but no verdict. The no-vision-people-run-wild line is famous. The one about hasty talk being worse than folly deserves to be.
PRO 30:1-33	Agur son of Jakeh shows up, the most interesting voice in the book, calling himself the dumbest guy in the room and asking who's gathered up the wind in his fists. His prayer is one of the few genuinely modest asks in scripture: no lies, no poverty, no riches, because full people deny God and hungry people steal. Then he goes hard on lists: things that never say enough, things too amazing to figure out, small creatures wise anyway, including a lizard that got into a palace.
PRO 31:1-31	King Lemuel's mom gets the last word and she doesn't hedge. Not women, not wine, since a drinking king forgets the law. Speak up for people who have no voice. The closing poem about the worthy woman reads as domestic and is honestly mostly commercial: she scouts a field and buys it, plants a vineyard, trades linen with the merchants, while her husband's contribution is being well regarded at the gate. Charm is fake, beauty's vapour, and the ask at the end is: pay her and credit her in public.
SIR 1:1-30	The material isn't his and he says so up front: all wisdom comes from the Lord. Nobody's counting the sand, the raindrops, or the days of eternity. Wisdom predates everything, got created and poured out over God's works, which is a weirdly industrial image. Then the phrase this book never lets go of: the fear of the Lord. Beginning, fullness, crown, root, all inside twenty verses. Unfair anger is never justifiable, and showing off gets your secrets read out in public.
SIR 2:1-18	Just signed up to serve God? Item one is that it's going to be rough. Gold gets tested in fire and acceptable men in the furnace of humiliation, which is honest and zero comfort. He tells the God-fearing to wait, trust and hope, then dares anyone to name one person from history who trusted and got humiliated for it. The woes target hesitation, not wickedness: scared hearts, limp hands, and anybody out of patience. Better to land in God's hands than in people's.
SIR 3:1-31	A chapter on parents, honest about what honour actually costs. The famous part is a father's decline: help him, don't grieve him, and if his mind starts going, be patient instead of humiliating him while you're still strong. The reward gets described beautifully, sins melting like ice in good weather. Then a pivot to humility: the bigger you are, the more you humble yourself. Don't chase things too hard for you, modest advice from a wisdom teacher.
SIR 4:1-31	First half is about poverty and it does not hedge. Don't make hungry eyes wait. Don't give anybody a reason to curse you, because whoever made him is listening. Be a dad to the fatherless and you get treated like a son of the Most High. Then wisdom shows up as an instructor with alarming methods, grinding her student through discipline until she decides he's trustworthy, and only then coming back by the straight road with the secrets.
SIR 5:1-15	A list of sentences not to say out loud, which makes this one of the more fun chapters here. Don't say your stuff is enough. Don't ask who's in charge of you. And definitely don't say the thing everyone's thought, that you sinned and nothing happened. The right reading of that silence is patience, not permission. Mercy and wrath share an office. Don't winnow with every wind, don't walk down every path. Don't understand something? Hand over the mouth.
SIR 6:1-37	The friendship chapter, and the most clear-eyed thing anyone wrote on the subject in the ancient world. Keep lots of people on good terms with you, and one actual advisor in a thousand. The taxonomy is brutal: the friend who's in it for his own convenience, the friend who converts to enemy and takes the fight public, and the dinner buddy who bosses your staff around and then can't be located the second things go bad.
SIR 7:1-36	A very long list of don'ts, from a guy who has watched people wreck themselves in every one of these ways. Don't angle for a promotion. Don't volunteer to be a judge in case you turn out to be scared of a powerful face. The household section is less comfortable, since daughters get watched and sons get their necks bowed. The closing line is the book's whole method: remember your last end.
SIR 8:1-19	A chapter entirely about picking your fights, and its conclusion is to pick basically none. Not the powerful guy, not the rich guy, not the guy who won't stop talking. Don't sue a judge, because the verdict will follow his honour rather than the evidence. Don't lend upward, and if you have to, treat the money as gone. The kindest lines are about aging and dying: don't mock the old or gloat over the dead. We're all on the list.
SIR 9:1-18	The chapter every modern reader trips over, because the advice about women is framed relentlessly as hazard management: don't stare, don't linger, don't drink with a married woman. At least the reasoning puts the problem in the looker. Then it pivots, and the second half is genuinely warm. Never swap an old friend for a new one, because a new friend is new wine and only worth drinking once it's aged. Stay clear of anybody with the power to kill you.
SIR 10:1-31	Political theory, and the core claim is that a people takes its character from whoever's running it. As the ruler is, so is the entire city. An uninstructed king wrecks his people. Then a long and enjoyable demolition of pride, tracking empires changing hands over injustice, violence and greed. Best pair of lines in the chapter: he's a king today, tomorrow he's dead, and what he inherits is creeping things and worms.
SIR 11:1-34	A chapter about not judging the surface, and it argues the case with a bee, tiny among flying things and still the best thing on the table. Kings have wound up sitting on the dirt and total nobodies have worn crowns. Then the saver, who scrimps for years, announces he's finally going to enjoy his stuff, and has no idea how little time is left. Best image: the proud man as a decoy partridge in a cage.
SIR 12:1-18	Generosity and enemies, and the first is uncomfortable. Help the godly and the lowly, and specifically withhold bread from the ungodly, because he'll use it against you. Then the enemy section, a small masterpiece of paranoia. His wickedness is rust on brass, and even if he acts humble, you'll find when you wipe the mirror that the rust never came off. He talks sweetly while scouting you for a pit, and when trouble shows up he's already there, foot behind your heel.
SIR 13:1-26	The class chapter, and it's merciless. Touch pitch, you're marked. Hang around the rich and powerful and you're the clay pot next to the iron kettle, and only one of you survives that collision. The portrait of the patron reads like reporting: he trades on you while you're useful, humiliates you with his own hospitality until he's stripped you two or three times, then laughs. When a rich guy falls there's a line of helpers. When a poor guy falls he gets a lecture.
SIR 14:1-27	The miser's portrait, a study in self-harm more than greed. His eye is never satisfied with his own share, he grudges the bread on his own table, and everything he saves by starving himself is money somebody else will enjoy. Nobody is worse off, the author says, than the guy who envies himself. The counter-advice is unexpectedly generous for this book: be good to yourself, be generous to your friend while you're still breathing, and don't waste a good day. Nobody goes hunting for luxury in the grave.
SIR 15:1-20	Wisdom gets described in domestic terms, meeting the law-keeping man like a mother and opening his mouth in the assembly, which for a scribe is the actual prize. Then the argument the chapter exists for. Don't say God made you fall, because he has no use for a sinful man. God made humans and left them in the hand of their own counsel, about as strong a free-will statement as the tradition has. Fire and water are on the table. Pick one.
SIR 16:1-30	Opens bleak about family: better to die childless than raise ungodly kids. Then the historical evidence, delivered like a court docket: the giants who revolted in their strength, the neighbours Lot had to live next to, and the six hundred thousand footmen who managed it purely through hardness of heart. Somebody's going to say there's no way God notices one guy in a creation this size. Heaven, the deep and the earth all move when he shows up.
SIR 17:1-32	A compressed anthropology. Man is made of dirt and goes back to dirt, with days issued by the number, and a kit list: counsel, tongue, eyes, ears, heart. He got shown good and evil and is under continuous observation, since all his works are like the sun in front of God. Best image is administrative and honestly lovely: your charitable giving gets kept like a signet ring. The case for repenting now is practical, not pious. Dead people don't say thanks.
SIR 18:1-33	Opens with a great statement of the limits of the whole wisdom project: when a man finishes, he's only at the beginning.
