A hundred years max, one drop from the sea, one pebble off the beach, and God's patience comes straight out of noticing how short we are. Then the practical section, with one of the best lines in the book: a word is better than a gift. Learn before you talk, pay the vow now. And don't throw banquets on credit.
SIR 19:1-30	Opens with a hard economic fact, a drunk worker does not get rich, plus a note that despising small stuff ruins you gradually instead of all at once. Then the discretion chapter proper, and it's very good. Never repeat what you were told, friend or enemy, because he heard you and watched you and will get around to hating you at a convenient moment. A secret can safely die with you. It won't make you explode. The fool carries one like an arrow in the thigh.
SIR 20:1-32	A chapter built almost entirely on silence, and it sorts out the kinds. One guy is quiet because he's got nothing. Another is quiet because he knows his moment hasn't come. The braggart talks straight through his. Then a run of paradoxes: profit that becomes a loss, a gift that pays back nothing and a gift that pays back double, a bargain that costs sevenfold. Best line: slipping on the pavement beats slipping with your tongue. And better to hide your folly than hide your wisdom.
SIR 21:1-28	Sin gets the snake treatment, with lion teeth attached, and the point is proximity: it only bites if you get close. Building your house with other people's money is compared to stockpiling rocks for winter. But most of the chapter is a portrait gallery of the fool. His insides are a cracked pot that won't hold knowledge. Instruction sits on him like handcuffs and on a wise guy like gold jewelry. Best closer: the heart of fools is in their mouth.
SIR 22:1-27	Opens by comparing a lazy man first to a filthy stone and then to something off a dunghill, which suggests the author had a specific guy in mind. Teaching a fool gets described as gluing a broken pot back together, or waking a guy out of deep sleep who then goes, huh, what? Then the line everybody quotes: mourn the dead seven days and a fool his entire life, because at least the dead are resting. Sand, salt and a block of iron are easier to carry.
SIR 23:1-28	A prayer opens it, and what he asks not to get is very specific: a proud look, greed, a shameless mind. Then the discipline of the mouth, with casual swearing as the target, because a guy who's constantly naming God is like a servant who's constantly getting beaten, always carrying a bruise. The rest is about adultery, and the best bit is the internal monologue: walls are up, it's dark, who's watching. The answer: God's eyes are ten thousand times brighter than the sun.
SIR 24:1-34	The centrepiece of the book, and Wisdom delivers it herself. She came out of the mouth of the Most High, covered the earth like mist, walked the circuit of heaven and the floor of the abyss, and then went looking for an address. The Creator assigned her one: Jacob, Israel, Zion. What follows is all trees and spices, cedar, palm, Jericho roses, cinnamon, myrrh, and it's the loveliest writing in Sirach. And all of it turns out to be the law of Moses.
SIR 25:1-26	Starts warm, with three beautiful things: relatives who get along, neighbours who are actually friends, and a couple who genuinely agree. Then the second half shows up, and it's the most misogynistic stretch in the book: he'd rather share a house with a lion than a wicked wife, sin and death both started with a woman, and she gets no freedom of speech. There's no joke in that, and no reason to hunt for one.
SIR 26:1-29	Opens generously, with a good wife doubling her husband's lifespan, and produces two of the loveliest images in the book: her beauty running a house like sunrise, and a face in ripe age like the lamp on the holy candlestick. In between sits the author's list of terrors, and it's entirely social: getting slandered around town, facing a hostile crowd, a false accusation. It ends on three things that make him angry: a soldier living in poverty, and men of understanding treated like garbage.
SIR 27:1-30	Commercial observation first, and it's memorable: sin wedges itself in between buying and selling the way a nail goes between two stones. Then the assay imagery Sirach can't get enough of, sieve and furnace, applied to conversation, so you don't praise anybody until you've heard them argue. The heart of the chapter is betrayed confidences and the tone is unusually hurt. Break a secret and your friend is already a bird out of your hand. Wounds get bandaged, insults get patched up, this doesn't.
SIR 28:1-26	An argument about grudges, framed as a question you can't answer: you're feeding a grudge against a guy made of the exact same flesh, and you were planning to ask for mercy yourself? Remember your last end and drop it. A spark can be blown up or spat out, and both options come out of the same mouth. Then the tongue: it's scattered nations, knocked down fortified cities, and where a whip marks skin, a tongue breaks bones. So install a door and a bar.
SIR 29:1-28	On lending, and the portrait of the borrower is drawn from life: he's kissing your hands until the money's in them, then the due date hits and suddenly the times are hard, and if you ever see half of it back you should count yourself lucky. Which is why, the author notes, people have quit lending entirely. Best part is the dignity section: your own shelter of logs beats great food in a house where you don't dare open your mouth.
SIR 30:1-25	The parenting half has not aged well and there's no point pretending otherwise, because the recommended tools are stripes, a bowed neck and zero freedom, on the theory that an unbroken horse turns stubborn. The stated goal is at least affectionate: a well-taught son continues his father, so the father dies and somehow hasn't died. Then the chapter switches subject entirely. Health beats all the gold there is. Death beats a bitter life. Love your own soul and put the sorrow down, because worry ages you early.
SIR 31:1-31	Money first, and the observation is medical: worrying about your riches eats your flesh and kills your sleep. His test for a rich man is hard to pass, because it isn't whether he has money, it's whether he had the chance to cheat and passed. Then the etiquette section, the most fun thing in Sirach. Don't reach wherever your eyes go. Stop eating first, for manners. On wine he's balanced: as good as life in measure, and what kind of life is it without any.
SIR 32:1-24	A guide to behaving at a party, and it's charming. Got put in charge of the feast? Don't get grand about it, take care of the guests, sit down last. Nobody talks over the music, which gets two of the book's better similes, a carbuncle in gold and an emerald in gold. The advice to the young guy is the best part: talk only if you're needed, hesitate even when asked twice, and be the person who knows things and keeps quiet.
SIR 33:1-31	A chapter about distinction. Why is one day better than another when all the daylight comes off the same sun? Because God separated them, hallowing some and leaving others regular, and the author runs the same logic on people, who are clay in the potter's hand. The fool gets a great image, a heart like a cartwheel and thoughts like a rolling axle. Then hard-nosed retirement advice: don't sign anything over early, because it's better that your kids ask you than that you ask them.
SIR 34:1-26	The dream chapter, and the debunking is quick: fixating on a dream is grabbing at a shadow and chasing wind. What he offers instead is experience, and he cites his own, including several occasions of nearly getting killed while travelling. The final third turns severe and it's the best part. A sacrifice bought with stolen money is a joke. Stiffing a labourer on his wages is shedding blood. And fasting for a sin you plan to repeat is washing after touching a corpse and then touching it again.
SIR 35:1-20	The argument is that regular behaviour counts as liturgy: keeping the law multiplies offerings, returning a favour is fine flour, almsgiving is a thank offering, and just stopping the wrongdoing works as atonement. He still wants the actual offering, given cheerfully. Then the part that matters. Don't imagine the gift buys anything, because the judge takes no bribes. And the prayer of the humble punches through the clouds and refuses to leave until the sceptres of the unrighteous get snapped.
SIR 36:1-26	The chapter opens in a completely different register, as a national prayer: send your fear on the nations, show new signs, crush the heads of the rulers who go around saying there's nobody but us, gather the tribes, have compassion on Jerusalem. It's the closest Sirach ever gets to a psalm. Then, with no transition whatsoever, we're back to household advice. Marriage gets described as a pillar of rest and a fence around a plot, and the guy without one is a robber skipping town to town.
SIR 37:1-31	On advice, and it's the most cynical and useful thing in the book. Everyone claims to be your friend. One enjoys your good times and vanishes in the bad, another works alongside you strictly for the meals. Then the advisors, and the warning is precise: every advisor recommends getting advice, so figure out his angle first. The list of people never to consult is the best bit: a coward about war, a merchant about exchange rates, a lazy guy about work. Trust your own gut instead.
SIR 38:1-34	It opens by defending doctors: God made the physician and made medicines out of the ground, so pray, make the offering, and then get out of the doctor's way. The mourning advice is startlingly practical: real crying, a proper burial, capped at a day or two so the neighbours can't talk. The last third is a magnificent portrait of skilled labour, the smith wrestling iron in furnace heat, the potter spinning the wheel with his feet, and a snobby conclusion that these guys never make it into council.
SIR 39:1-35	The scribe's job description, and it's unmistakably a self-portrait: study the ancients, work through the hard sayings, serve among great men, travel abroad, get up early to pray, and get rewarded with a name that outlasts the world. Then the author admits he's filled up like a full moon, easily the least modest line in here. Nobody gets to ask what this is or why that is, because everything has a use and a season, the nasty inventory included: winds, hail, famine, all standing by for orders.
SIR 40:1-30	The opening is the bleakest passage in Sirach: a heavy yoke on everybody from the womb to burial in the earth, which he calls the mother of all things, and fear of death reaching the guy on the throne and the guy in the hemp shirt equally. Even sleep gives you nothing, because you wake up like a man who just got out of the front line. Then the mood lifts into a comparison game, every good thing topped by a better one. Ends cold, on begging.
SIR 41:1-24	The opening is the best thing here because it refuses to pick a side: death is bitter to a comfortable guy who still has an appetite, and welcome to a guy who's old, failing and out of patience. Both are true and he says both. A good reputation outlasts a thousand piles of gold. The rest is an inventory of shame, sorted by who's watching: lying to a prince, stealing from your hosts, and putting your elbow on the table when somebody says hi.
SIR 42:1-25	The matching list of things NOT to be ashamed of, and it's unexpectedly commercial: the law, exact weights, settling accounts, and getting everything in writing by number and weight, easily the most practical verse in the book. Then a father's insomnia about a daughter, worried at every stage of her life, plus another run of hostile remarks about women. The second half switches register entirely into a hymn about God's knowledge, and the closing idea is genuinely elegant: everything's made double, each thing against its opposite, nothing left half-finished.
SIR 43:1-33	A hymn to the weather, and this is Sirach at his absolute peak. The sun bakes the countryside at noon and burns three times hotter than a guy working a furnace. The moon names the month; the stars never fall asleep on watch. Then a whole meteorology: the rainbow drawn as a circle of glory, snow sprinkled down like birds, hail broken small, ice strapping a breastplate onto the water. Sailors report the sea and its monsters. The honest conclusion: the sum of it is, he is all.
SIR 44:1-23	The famous roll of honour kicks off, and its most striking verse is about the people who didn't make the list, who have no memorial and are as if never born. He includes not just rulers and prophets but composers and writers. Then the parade starts. Enoch pleased the Lord and left. Noah was found righteous and, in a great phrase, taken in exchange for the world. Abraham got tested, came out faithful, and got the oath about dust and stars and land from sea to sea.
SIR 45:1-26	Moses first, beloved of God and men, who shut down the wonders and took his commandments face to face. Then Aaron, who gets three times the space, and almost all of it is clothing: linen breeches, ephod, gold pomegranates and bells, the Urim and Thummim, a gold plate on the mitre that just says HOLINESS. The revolt of Dathan, Abiram and Korah gets handled in two verses of fire. Aaron gets the firstfruits and zero land, because God is the portion. Phinehas takes third place, David a mention.
SIR 46:1-20	Joshua gets the highlights reel: the sun going backward so one day became two, hailstones landing on the enemy at exactly the right moment. Out of six hundred thousand guys on foot, only two made it into the land, the ones who stood up against the complaining. Caleb keeps his strength into old age. The judges get a group blessing conditioned on their hearts. Then Samuel, whose detail isn't a battle but an audit: before he died he stated publicly he'd never taken so much as a shoe off anybody.
SIR 47:1-25	David gets compared to the fat off the peace offering, a compliment here, plus lions handled like farm animals, the sling stone, and Goliath's trash talk shut down. He gets credit for organising the temple singers. Solomon gets praised at length: river of understanding, gold gathered like tin, whole countries amazed. Then the sentence turns: you bowed your loins to women, you blemished your honour. The kingdom splits, with Rehoboam and Jeroboam named as the guys who finished it.
SIR 48:1-25	Elijah shows up as fire, pure momentum: famine, sky shut, fire three times, a dead man raised, kings brought down. Best line is his actual job description: turn the heart of the father to the son. Elisha inherits the spirit and his corpse prophesies after death. And then the flat verdict: it didn't work, nobody repented, they got carried off. Hezekiah closes it out cutting solid rock for water, while Isaiah delivers the city from Sennacherib.
SIR 49:1-16	Josiah gets the loveliest compliment in the book: a memory like blended incense. The accounting on the monarchy is brutal and short: except for David, Hezekiah and Josiah, all of them failed. Jeremiah gets a mention, sanctified in the womb for a job description of four verbs, two of them demolition. Ezekiel gets the cherubim in one line, the twelve prophets get handled as a batch, and the rebuilders get named down to Adam.
SIR 50:1-29	The book's climax isn't a battle, it's a church service. Simon son of Onias walks out of the sanctuary and the author stacks images for a page: morning star, full moon, rainbow, roses, frankincense, a cypress up in the clouds. Then the ritual itself: the robe, relatives standing around him like palm trees around a cedar, wine poured at the altar, trumpets, everybody face down. After that the author signs his name, and adds a sour aside about three neighbouring peoples.
SIR 51:1-30	A closing thanksgiving, and the emergency he's thanking God for is reputational, not military: a slanderous tongue, forged lies, slander carried to the king. That's the danger a court scribe faces. Then his own story of getting wisdom, warmer than anything else in the book: he asked for her at the temple as a kid, and the reward he names is a tongue. The pitch that follows: come lodge in the house of instruction, get her for free, for he worked little and got a lot of rest.
SNG 1:1-17	The woman talks first, worth noting for a book this old, and she does not warm up: kiss me, your love beats wine. Her answer to the daughters staring at her tan is practical, not apologetic: the sun did that while she worked the vineyards her brothers stuck her with. His opening compliment compares her to a horse in Pharaoh's chariots.
SNG 2:1-17	They trade images and neither one holds back. She's a lily; he's the one apple tree in a forest of regular timber, and she sits in its shade on purpose. Faint with love and needing raisins is either deeply romantic or a snack request. Then his invitation, easily the best writing here: winter's done, the flowers are up, the figs are coming in.
SNG 3:1-11	A night scene that reads like a dream and probably is one. She's looking for him from her bed, then the streets, then the squares. The watchmen show up and she just asks them, which is either bold or dream logic. She finds him right after and grabs hold with zero plans to let go. Then a hard cut to a parade.
SNG 4:1-16	The long compliment, running a completely different aesthetic. Hair like a flock of goats coming down a mountain is about the movement. Teeth like a freshly washed flock, none missing, is about completeness. A neck like David's armoury tower is about bearing, not size. Then the poem turns into a garden: locked, sealed, saffron, cinnamon, myrrh. Her reply settles it: blow the fragrance out.
SNG 5:1-16	Garden visit accomplished, and then the mood flips. She's asleep with her heart awake when he knocks, hair damp from the dew. Her hesitation is completely domestic: she's undressed, her feet are washed. By the time she reaches the door, he's gone. The watchmen, merely annoying in chapter three, beat her and take her cloak. The poem never explains it.
SNG 6:1-13	The daughters volunteer to help search and she deflects: he's off in his garden picking lilies. Then the thesis again, I'm his, he's mine. His praise escalates in an odd direction, because she's also awesome as an army with banners, and he asks her to look away because her eyes are winning. Then the math: sixty queens, eighty concubines, one dove.
SNG 7:1-13	Third inventory, the most enthusiastic, and this time it starts at the sandals and works up. Waist like a heap of wheat ringed with lilies. Eyes like the pools of Heshbon. And a nose like the tower of Lebanon facing Damascus, a compliment nobody alive would risk. He announces he's climbing the palm tree. Then she takes over: fields, villages, vines.
SNG 8:1-14	She opens with a workaround for public affection: if he were her brother, kissing him in the street wouldn't raise eyebrows. Then the verse the whole book has been driving at, and it lands less like love poetry than a verdict: love is strong as death, jealousy cruel as the grave. Floods can't put it out. She informs her brothers she's a wall.
WIS 1:1-16	The book opens by addressing you judges of the earth. Wisdom straight up will not move into a soul that's cooking something up. The surveillance imagery is heavy: an ear of jealousy catching every mutter. So watch the trash talk. Then the line that makes the whole book worth it: God did not make death. People befriended it themselves.
WIS 2:1-24	The author hands the mic to the ungodly and lets them make their case, the boldest move in the book, because they make it well. Life's short, nobody comes back, reason is a spark that goes out. Therefore: wine, perfume, rosebud crowns. Almost persuasive right up to squeeze the poor, spare no widow, and torture the righteous guy to see if God shows up.
WIS 3:1-19	The rebuttal, and its main move is changing where you're standing. The souls of the righteous are in God's hand, so what looked like ruin was actually peace. The suffering gets reclassified as assay work, gold in the furnace, and the payoff is surprisingly energetic: sparks running through dry stubble, judging nations. Then a childless woman and a eunuch get promised better than kids.
WIS 4:1-20	An argument against measuring a life by its length or its headcount of descendants. A crowd of ungodly offspring gets compared to shoots with no roots. Against that, the famous line: understanding is your grey hair, a clean life is ripe old age. Then the delicate part, why a good person dies young. Pulled out early, before wickedness could work on his head.
WIS 5:1-23	Courtroom scene, and the author clearly loves writing the losing side's statement. Their regret speech is genuinely great: this is the guy we made a punchline, we called his life crazy, the sun never even rose for us. Then the images of their own disappearing: a ship leaving no track, a bird leaving nothing, an arrow whose path closes up behind it.
WIS 6:1-25	The lecture to management keeps going, and its main point is one no ruler wants to hear: your authority was issued to you, the issuer keeps receipts, and the audit scales with the job title. Big guys get searched out mightily. Then the author switches to a courtship voice: wisdom does the hunting herself, so the early riser finds her already parked at his gate.
WIS 7:1-30	The speaker, generally taken to be Solomon, opens by insisting he's completely ordinary. Ten months in a womb, first sound out of his mouth the same wail as everybody, because no king ever got a different launch. Then he prayed and got wisdom, rating gold as sand next to her. The course catalogue runs from star positions to medicinal roots.
WIS 8:1-21	A chapter of open courtship, and the author is not shy: sought her since he was young, wanted her as his bride. Then the argument becomes a sales deck with headings. Want money? She works all things. Want understanding? She's the architect of everything. The last move is the honest one: none of this is available unless God hands her over. So he prayed.
WIS 9:1-18	The prayer, and it's the most humble thing in the book. He asks for wisdom, the one sitting right beside God's throne, and bases the request on his limits instead of his job: a servant, son of a handmaid, weak, short-lived. Then the sentence the whole tradition kept: a decaying body weighs the soul down. Nobody's mapped the heavens.
WIS 10:1-21	A speedrun through Genesis and Exodus where Wisdom does absolutely everything and nobody gets named. A guy who ditched her in anger died in the same rage that killed his brother. She steered a righteous man through the flood on a poor piece of wood, a shockingly dismissive way to describe the ark. Then she took the whole nation across the Red Sea.
WIS 11:1-26	The author picks one idea and hammers it: the exact same thing that punished Egypt supplied Israel. Egyptians got clotted blood where the river should be, and the Israelites got water out of solid flint. Then a fantastic tangent about restraint: God could have sent bears, lions, or brand new custom beasts breathing fire and shooting sparks out of their eyes, and passed.
WIS 12:1-27	A long argument about why judgment takes so long. The land's old inhabitants get described in the worst possible terms, and then the author insists God still spared them, sending hornets ahead of the army so they'd have somewhere to repent. He also insists their wickedness was inborn, a problem he never resolves. God is sovereign over his own strength, so he judges gently.
WIS 13:1-19	The anti-idol argument opens with real generosity. People who mistook fire, wind, stars and water were at least looking at something real, and their blame is small, because they were searching. Then the patience runs out at manufactured gods, and the detail is savage: the woodcutter cooks dinner over the offcuts, carves the last knotty scrap, paints it red, bolts it to the wall.
WIS 14:1-31	The wood argument continues. A guy boarding a ship prays to a plank more rotten than the boat he's standing on, when a raft once carried the entire future of the human race. Then a genealogy of idolatry: a grieving dad honours a statue of his dead kid, princes make statues mandatory, and ambitious sculptors improve the likeness until people are worshipping a man.
WIS 15:1-19	A breather addressed to God before the author goes back to the workshop. This time it's the potter: out of one batch of clay he makes vessels for clean uses and vessels for the other thing, then makes a god out of the same clay. The guy doing this is dirt. What the author can't stand isn't the theology, it's the professional pride.
WIS 16:1-29	The parallel gets worked way past subtle. Egypt gets vermin, Israel gets quail. When Israel gets bitten by serpents the cure is explicitly not medical: not herbs, not ointment, a word. Then the physics goes weird, with fire burning harder inside water. The manna is the best detail: bread that tasted like whatever you wanted, survived fire, and melted under one weak sunbeam.
WIS 17:1-21	The plague of darkness, retold as a study of fear. The Egyptians who thought they had a holy nation locked up become the prisoners, cut off from each other by a curtain of forgetfulness. The only glow is a self-kindled flicker that scares them worse than the dark did. The magicians who sold terror-removal services are sick with fear themselves.
WIS 18:1-25	Light on one side, dark on the other. Egypt hears Israelite voices without seeing anybody and decides those people are lucky. The payback is stated plainly: they planned to kill infants, and now their own children are gone. Then the image this chapter is famous for: peaceful silence over everything, night at its midpoint, and God's word leaping off the throne as an armed warrior.
WIS 19:1-22	The last chapter ties it off. God knew Egypt would change its mind, and it does, chasing the people it had begged to leave, while still standing at the graves. Dry land coming up out of the Red Sea, Israel crossing while skipping like lambs. Sodom turned strangers away, Egypt enslaved its own benefactors, and both crowds went blind at the door.
1KI 19:1-21	One message from Jezebel and the man who just beat four hundred and fifty prophets folds. He runs to Beersheba, walks into the desert and asks to die. God's response isn't a lecture, it's food and sleep, twice: the journey is too much. Then forty days to Horeb. Wind, an earthquake, a fire, and God is in none of them. Then a still small voice. Elijah gives the same answer before and after: I'm the only one left. He's given work and one correction: seven thousand others.
1KI 20:1-23	Ben Hadad besieges Samaria and demands Ahab's silver, gold and family. Ahab says yes, which tells you everything. Only when it becomes a house-by-house search do the elders talk him into refusing. Ben Hadad swears there won't be dust enough for a handful. Ahab's best line ever: don't let the guy putting armour on brag like the guy taking it off. A prophet promises victory with two hundred and thirty-two provincial young men. It works: enemy command is blind drunk. Next time on 1 Kings: the valley.
ACT 9:1-21	Saul turns up still breathing threats, headed for Damascus with warrants in hand. Then the light, the fall, and a question instead of an accusation: why are you persecuting me? His escort hears the sound and sees nothing. Best bit of the chapter is Ananias, who gets a crystal-clear divine instruction and argues with it, citing the guy's record and his arrest powers. Told to go anyway, he goes, and opens with "brother." Next time on Acts: the man with the warrants leaves town in a basket.
ACT 9:22-43	First consequence of the conversion: Saul has to be smuggled out of a city in a basket through a hole in the wall. In Jerusalem nobody believes he's actually a disciple, which is fair, and Barnabas has to vouch for him. Then he argues with the Hellenists until he's shipped off to Tarsus for his own safety. Then two quick Peter stories: a man bedridden eight years, and the widows of Joppa holding up the clothes Tabitha sewed for them.
ACT 10:1-25	Two visions, engineered to collide. Cornelius is a Roman officer everybody likes, and he gets an angel with a name and a street address. Peter's on a roof, starving, and gets a sheet lowered full of every animal he's spent his whole life not eating, plus an instruction to dig in. He says no. Luke makes it happen three times. Then somebody knocks at the gate while he's still standing there working out what that was. Next time on Acts: the sermon.
ACT 10:26-48	Cornelius greets him by dropping at his feet, and gets told to stand up, I'm just a man. Peter's opening line admits out loud that he isn't supposed to be in this house. Then the entire story in about six sentences, including the odd detail that the risen Jesus was shown to chosen witnesses and not to the general public. He never gets to finish. The Spirit shows up mid-sermon, and Peter's question about withholding water from people who already have the Spirit is not really a question.
JER 51:1-31	Babylon's oracle is huge, and the best line is pure medical sarcasm: get some balm for her pain, maybe she'll pull through, immediately followed by, we tried to heal Babylon and she isn't healing. She was a golden cup that got the entire earth drunk. Then out of nowhere the hymn from chapter ten shows up word for word, about the God who keeps wind in a storehouse. Babylon gets called God's battle axe, swung at farmers and yokes included, right before she gets handed the bill.
JER 51:32-64	Zion finally gets to speak: he swallowed me like a monster, stuffed his mouth with my best stuff, threw the empty dish out. God's answer is basically a lawsuit, I'll plead your case, then dry up her water. The picture of Babylon's end is a party: host the feast, get them drunk, let them sleep permanently. Then the closing stunt, genuinely great theater: Jeremiah writes it out, hands it to a quartermaster heading to Babylon, and says read this aloud, chuck it in the Euphrates.
JER 52:1-17	An appendix, written totally flat, which is exactly right. The dates carry it: tenth month, siege forts built, dragging into year eleven, and by the fourth month, no bread anywhere. Then the night escape through the garden gate, the capture on the Jericho plain, and the sentence carried out in order so the sons die while their father can still see it. Temple and palace burned, walls flattened. The last people mentioned are the poorest, left to look after the vineyards. Next time on Jeremiah: somebody takes inventory.
JER 52:18-34	A book of thundering poetry ends with a packing list, and that's the point. Pots, shovels, snuffers, basins, spoons, gold and silver tallied separately, Solomon's pillars measured to the finger, bronze too heavy to weigh. Then the executions at Riblah, listed by job title. Then the deportation math, three numbers adding to four thousand six hundred, shockingly small for a kingdom's end. And the weirdest ending in the prophets: a king let out after thirty seven years, given clean clothes and dinner every day for life.
1KI 20:24-43	Syria's insight: fire the thirty-two kings, hire real captains, fight in the valley where Israel's god allegedly doesn't work. Israel lines up looking like two little flocks of goats. A man of God says: you called him a hills god, watch this. A hundred thousand Syrian infantry in a day, then the wall of Aphek falls on twenty-seven thousand more. Ben Hadad hides and sends his guys out in sackcloth, and Ahab calls him brother. So a prophet gets punched, bandages up, and cons Ahab into sentencing himself.
1KI 21:1-29	Ahab wants Naboth's vineyard for a herb garden and gets a no: family land. So the king of Israel goes to bed and refuses dinner. Jezebel asks if he's the king or not, then handles it: letters in his name, fake fast, Naboth in the seat of honour, two paid liars, a stoning, all legal on paper. Ahab strolls down to collect his garden and Elijah is standing in it. The sentence rhymes: dogs licked Naboth's blood, dogs get yours. Ahab repents and gets it pushed onto his son.
1KI 22:1-26	Three peaceful years, then Ahab wants Ramoth Gilead back. Jehoshaphat signs up before asking whether God is on board. Four hundred prophets shout go, one with iron horns as a prop. Ahab admits there's one real prophet, Micaiah, whom he hates for never saying anything nice. The messenger coaches him to agree, so Micaiah walks in and does a dead-on impression of the four hundred until Ahab demands the truth. Then: Israel scattered like sheep with no shepherd. Next time on 1 Kings: one arrow, aimed at nobody.
1KI 22:27-53	Bread and water till I get back, says Ahab. If you get back, says Micaiah, God never spoke through me. Ahab fights in disguise and asks Jehoshaphat to wear the royal robes, which he somehow accepts. Syria's orders are to kill only Israel's king, so they charge the robes, then realise their mistake. Some soldier fires blind and hits Ahab in the seam of his armour. They prop him up so the troops hold, and he bleeds out by sunset. The dogs show up, right on cue.
2KI 1:1-18	Moab bails the second Ahab dies. Ahaziah falls through his own window lattice and, instead of asking the God of Israel anything, sends to Baal Zebub of Ekron for a prognosis. Elijah ambushes the messengers: is there no God in Israel, and also, you are not getting out of that bed. The king hears the description, hairy guy, leather belt, and goes, yep, Elijah. Fifty soldiers go to fetch him. Fire from the sky, gone. Another fifty, gone. The third kneels, and that works.
2KI 2:1-25	Elijah tries three times to ditch Elisha, and three times Elisha says absolutely not. At every stop the local prophets helpfully inform him his boss is getting taken today. Thanks, he knows. Elijah rolls up his coat, whacks the river, and they walk across dry. Last request? Double portion of your spirit, which is the firstborn's share. Fine, if you are watching when I go. Fire chariot, whirlwind, gone. Elisha picks up the coat, hits the water, and it splits again, so the job is his.
2KI 3:1-27	Jehoram gets graded on a curve: evil, but he did take down his father's Baal pillar, so, progress. Moab's king is a sheep man who used to pay tribute in wool, not any more. Three kings march seven days through the desert and run out of water, and Jehoram assumes God set this up to kill them. Elisha says flat out he would ignore Jehoram if Judah were not standing there. Moab sees red water at sunrise, thinks it is blood, charges in to loot, and gets wrecked.
2KI 4:1-24	A widow is about to lose her two sons to a creditor. All she has is one pot of oil. Borrow every empty jar in the neighbourhood, shut the door, start pouring. It pours until there is nothing left to pour into, then stops. Sell it, pay the debt, live on the change. Then a woman in Shunem keeps feeding him and builds a guest room on her roof. Gehazi points out: no son, old husband. Next time on 2 Kings: it is well.
2KI 4:25-44	She sees Gehazi coming, says everything is fine, and does not get into it with the assistant. Then she grabs Elisha's feet and asks the real question: did I ask you for a son? Plan A is Gehazi running ahead with the staff to lay it on the boy's face, and it does nothing at all, and the text just lets that sit there. Plan B is a shut door, prayer, and Elisha lying full length on the boy twice.
2KI 5:1-14	Naaman runs Syria's army, wins everything, and has leprosy. The person who fixes this is a kidnapped Israelite girl serving his wife, who mentions there is a prophet back home who could sort it out. That tip goes up the chain and turns into an international incident, with the king of Israel ripping his clothes because he is sure it is a setup. Then Elisha does not even come to the door. Next time on 2 Kings: the bill.
2KI 5:15-27	Naaman comes back declaring there is no God anywhere but Israel and tries to pay. Elisha swears he will not take a thing. So Naaman asks for two mule-loads of Israelite dirt instead, plus a pre-excuse for bowing in Rimmon's temple. Gehazi watches all that money leave and cannot stand it. He chases Naaman down, invents two young prophets who just turned up hungry, and asks for silver. Naaman sends double. Gehazi stashes it and says he went nowhere. Elisha: my heart went with you.
2KI 6:1-18	The prophets' dorm is too small, so they go cut timber by the Jordan and a man loses a borrowed axe head in the river. Elisha throws in a stick and floats the iron. Then the plot changes gear. Syria keeps setting ambushes and Israel keeps not walking into them, so the king starts hunting for a mole. The leak is Elisha, who apparently hears what you say in your own bedroom. Next time on 2 Kings: a tour of Samaria.
2KI 6:19-33	Elisha tells the blinded army they have the wrong city, walks them into Samaria, and opens their eyes. The king asks twice, eagerly, whether he can kill them. No, says Elisha, feed them. They go home and the raids stop. Then Ben Hadad besieges Samaria and the humour stops. The famine gets priced: a donkey's head for eighty pieces of silver. A woman calls to the king from the wall: two mothers agreed to eat their sons, hers was eaten, and the other has hidden her child.
2KI 7:1-20	Elisha announces tomorrow's grain prices: flour and barley cheap in Samaria's gate, which after eighty-shekel donkey heads sounds insane. The king's officer says not even windows in heaven could manage it, and gets told he will watch it happen and eat none of it. Meanwhile four men with leprosy sit outside the gate doing the math: stay and die, or surrender to Syria and maybe not. The Syrian camp is empty, the army having heard a huge force coming and bolted. The officer gets trampled in the gate.
2KI 8:1-29	Elisha warns the Shunammite about a seven year famine, so she moves to Philistine country and comes back to find her land gone. She walks in to petition the king at the precise moment Gehazi is telling him the story about her son, which is timing you could not script. She gets it all back with interest. Then it turns dark. Sick Ben Hadad sends Hazael to ask if he will live. Elisha: tell him yes, he is going to die.
2KI 9:1-18	Elisha runs the anointing like a hit job: one trainee prophet, one flask of oil, take Jehu into a back room, pour, deliver, get out fast. The kid announces Jehu is king, that Ahab's whole house is coming down and dogs will eat Jezebel, then bolts. The officers outside go, what did the crazy guy want. Jehu plays it down until they push, and instantly there are cloaks on the stairs and a coup. Next time on 2 Kings: rider two.
2KI 9:19-37	Rider two also disappears into the column, and then the watchman calls it: that driving is Jehu, the man drives like a maniac. Joram, still wounded, drives out to meet him personally, and Jehu's answer to is it peace is a line about his mother. He wheels around yelling treason and takes an arrow through the heart. Jehu dumps the body on Naboth's field. Then Jezebel does her eyes, leans out the window, and her own eunuchs throw her down.
2KI 10:1-17	Jehu writes to Samaria: you have seventy of Ahab's sons and a walled city, so pick a king and fight me. They do the math on two dead kings and write back that they will do whatever he says. Letter two takes them up on it, and seventy heads turn up in baskets. Jehu stacks them at the gate and gives a speech that boils down to: I killed the king, sure, but who killed all these? Next time on 2 Kings: the sacrifice.
2KI 10:18-36	Jehu announces that Ahab served Baal a bit but he is going to serve him a lot, and calls every Baal priest in Israel to a huge sacrifice. Attendance mandatory, absence fatal. Packed house. He hands out robes, which conveniently makes everyone identifiable, and sweeps the place for Yahweh worshippers. The offering ends and so does the congregation. They demolish the temple and turn it into a public toilet. Baal is gone. The golden calves at Bethel and Dan stay.
2KI 11:1-21	Athaliah hears her son is dead and wipes out the whole royal family to keep the throne. She misses one. Jehosheba grabs the baby Joash and his nurse and hides them in the temple for six years while Athaliah runs the country. Year seven, Jehoiada assembles the guard, hands out King David's own spears, and rings the boy with armed men. They crown him. Athaliah walks in, sees a seven year old wearing the crown, and screams treason, which is accurate and six years late.
2KI 12:1-21	Joash does the right thing for exactly as long as Jehoiada is standing next to him telling him what it is. High places, still there. The good bit is the budget scandal. He tells the priests to fix the temple out of the offerings, twenty-three years later it is still broken, so he pulls the money off them. Jehoiada drills a hole in a chest lid, and the scribe and the high priest count it. Then Hazael marches on Jerusalem and the treasury goes out as a bribe.
2KI 13:1-25	Two kings of Israel, one chapter, same verdict on both. Syria grinds Jehoahaz down to fifty horsemen, ten chariots and ten thousand infantry. He prays, God sends help, nothing actually changes. Then Elisha's deathbed. He has King Jehoash shoot an arrow east, calls it the arrow of victory, then says hit the ground with the rest. He taps three times and stops. Elisha is furious, because the number was the test: five or six and Syria is finished, three and you get three.
2KI 14:1-29	Amaziah kills the men who murdered his father but spares their kids, quoting the law that each man dies for his own sin, which is genuinely decent. Then he wipes out ten thousand Edomites, gets cocky, and sends Israel a message: let us look each other in the face. Jehoash replies with a fable about a thistle asking a cedar for its daughter and getting stomped flat, plus advice: you beat Edom, great, enjoy it at home. Amaziah goes anyway and gets flattened at Beth Shemesh.
2KI 15:1-19	Judah gets one king here and Israel burns through four. Azariah reigns fifty-two years, catches leprosy and spends the rest of it in a separate house while his son runs things. Then Israel at full speed. Zechariah, six months, killed in front of a crowd by Shallum, and that is Jehu's four generations used up exactly. Shallum lasts one month before Menahem kills him. Next time on 2 Kings: who is funding all this.
2KI 15:20-38	Answer: every rich man in Israel, fifty shekels each, and Assyria goes home for now. After that it is a straight run of assassinations with the same verdict stamped on each. Pekahiah, two years, murdered in the palace fortress by his own captain Pekah. Pekah, twenty years, during which Tiglath Pileser walks in, takes Galilee, Gilead and all Naphtali, and deports everyone. Then Hoshea kills Pekah, and he is the last one there will be. Judah has Jotham. Fine, builds a gate, leaves the high places standing.
2KI 16:1-20	Ahaz does not get graded, he gets described, and the detail is that he put his own son in the fire. Syria and Israel besiege Jerusalem and cannot crack it. So Ahaz empties the temple and the palace of gold and mails it to Tiglath Pileser with a note signed your servant and your son. It works: Assyria smashes Damascus. Then he sees an altar there he likes and couriers the blueprints home for Urijah to build.
ACT 11:1-30	Peter gets back to Jerusalem and instead of a welcome he gets audited about who he had dinner with. His entire defence is telling the story over again from the top, sheet and animals and all. His closer, who am I to argue with God, ends it flat. Cut to Antioch, where guys from Cyprus and Cyrene are already preaching to Greeks. Barnabas comes to check it out and digs Saul out of Tarsus. Somebody here first says the word Christian.
ACT 12:1-25	James is killed in half a verse. Herod notices the reviews are good and grabs Peter next, sixteen soldiers for one fisherman who sleeps through the night before his execution. The jailbreak is weirdly domestic: get dressed, sandals, grab your coat. Peter thinks he's dreaming until the angel leaves him alone in a street. The prayer meeting praying him out of jail refuses to believe he's at the door and decides it must be his ghost. Herod lets a crowd call him a god and dies.
ACT 13:1-25	The staff list at Antioch casually includes Herod's foster brother. The Spirit pulls Barnabas and Saul out mid-fast and they sail for Cyprus, where a magician going by Elymas tries to keep a Roman governor off the faith. Saul, who becomes Paul in the exact same sentence, blinds him, and the governor signs up. John Mark bails at Perga, a detail that wrecks a friendship later. Then Paul's first big sermon: Egypt, wilderness, judges, Saul, David. Next time on Acts: he finally gets to it.
ACT 13:26-52	Second half is basically a close reading with a knife in it. God promised his Holy One wouldn't rot. David very much rotted. So the psalm was about somebody else. The whole argument is built out of a text the room already accepts, which is exactly why half of them love it and half want him gone. The Gentiles ask for an encore, the entire city shows up the next Sabbath, and that's when the jealousy kicks in. Then they shake the dust off and walk to Iconium.
ACT 14:1-28	Lystra is the weirdest scene in the book. A man lame since birth stands up, and the crowd, yelling in Lycaonian so the apostles can't follow, concludes the gods have landed and starts walking oxen to the gate. Barnabas gets Jupiter. Paul, who does the talking, gets Mercury. Their counter-sermon quotes no scripture at all, just rain and harvests, because this crowd has never heard of Moses. Then visitors from Antioch talk that same crowd around and they stone Paul. He gets up and walks back into town.
ACT 15:1-41	First church council, called because some guys from Judea rolled into Antioch announcing that salvation has a surgical prerequisite. Luke calls the fallout no small discord, his polite way of saying screaming match. Peter's argument: God already handed these people the Spirit without checking anybody's paperwork. James finds a prophecy and proposes four rules, none of them the one being demanded. Then the chapter faceplants: having settled the biggest question in the book, Paul and Barnabas break up for good over whether John Mark deserves a second chance.
ACT 16:1-18	Straight out of a council that ruled circumcision unnecessary, Paul circumcises Timothy. Luke reports it without blinking. Then closed doors: banned from Asia, blocked from Bithynia, funnelled to the coast until a Macedonian shows up in a dream. Watch verse ten, where the story switches to we. Europe opens with a riverside Sabbath chat and a businesswoman in the purple trade. Then a fortune-telling slave girl trails them for days shouting accurate advertising, and Paul exorcises her out of irritation. Next time on Acts: her owners check the books.
ACT 16:19-40	The charge skips the fortune telling, recast as Jews pushing un-Roman customs, a money grievance dressed up for court. Beaten and jailed with feet in stocks, Paul and Silas are singing by midnight. An earthquake throws every door open, and the jailer nearly kills himself, assuming they've fled. His question, what must I do to be saved, comes from a man still holding the blade. He spends the night washing their wounds. Offered quiet release next morning, Paul mentions his Roman citizenship and makes the magistrates come in person.
ACT 17:1-34	Three cities, three receptions, getting more polite each time. Thessalonica gives him a riot and the sharpest accusation in the book: these people say there's another king. Beroea earns Luke's only compliment, for checking Paul against scripture daily. Athens writes him off as a babbler, then invites him to explain himself anyway. The Areopagus speech quotes zero scripture, starting from an altar addressed to a nameless god and borrowing two lines of Greek poetry. Works great, until he says resurrection, and the room dissolves.
ACT 18:1-28	Corinth is where everything slows down: a year and a half, a day job stitching tents, and when the synagogue turns hostile Paul relocates one door away. The vision he gets isn't a commission, just reassurance. Gallio rules this an internal squabble about words and names and refuses to hear it. A crowd beats Sosthenes in front of the bench, and Gallio can't be bothered to look up. The vow and shaved head go unexplained. Apollos turns up brilliant but out of date, and gets quietly updated by two tentmakers.
ACT 19:1-21	Paul rolls into Ephesus, asks disciples about the Holy Spirit, and gets the flattest answer possible: never heard of it. Twelve get rebaptized, and the operation moves into a rented hall belonging to Tyrannus, running daily for two years. Cloth pulled off Paul is healing people, so freelance exorcists try borrowing the name of Jesus. The demon's answer, Jesus I know, Paul I know, but who are you, is the best line in the book. Next time on Acts: the silversmiths get involved.
ACT 19:22-41	A riot with a purely financial motive, in religious language. Demetrius calls a trade meeting, leads with profits, mentions the goddess second, and one verse later the city is chanting in the theatre. Paul wants to address the mob and gets physically blocked by his friends. Alexander gets shoved forward, the crowd clocks him as a Jew, and the chanting restarts for two hours. What finally ends it is a city clerk pointing out that courts exist and these men stole nothing. Meeting adjourned.
ACT 20:1-38	Lots of ports, lots of names, and one incident that happens purely because Paul will not wrap it up. A kid named Eutychus dozes off in a window during a sermon past midnight, drops out, and is picked up dead. Paul revives him, then keeps talking until sunrise. He walks to the next port alone. The Miletus speech is his only farewell on record, and he says goodbye twice: you won't see my face again, and wolves are coming, some from this room. Everyone cries at the dock.
ACT 21:1-22	The trip south is one long attempt to talk Paul out of going. Disciples at Tyre say through the Spirit, don't go, and the congregation walks them to the beach to kneel in the sand. At Caesarea, Philip's four prophesying daughters get one sentence and vanish. Agabus ties himself with Paul's belt to show whose hands it ends up in. Paul's reply, why are you breaking my heart, is the closest he comes to admitting the cost. Jerusalem raises a rumour going round. Next time on Acts: the PR fix.
ACT 21:23-40	The plan is basically a photo op: sponsor four men's vows, get seen footing the bill, rumour handled. It works until it doesn't. Visitors from Asia spot him and start yelling that he smuggled a Greek into the temple, a guess they made from seeing Trophimus with him in town. Temple doors slam behind him, the beating starts, and the Roman garrison saves him by sprinting down as the city riots. Carried up the steps over the crowd, Paul asks the commander for a word.
ACT 22:1-30	The defence is his conversion story told again, and the changes are the point, aimed at a crowd holding rocks. He opens in Hebrew, buying instant quiet, and pointedly describes Ananias as a devout law-keeping man every Jew in Damascus vouches for. The crowd rides along fine through the light, the voice and the blindness. Then he says Gentiles, and the dust is airborne. Paul waits until he's tied to the post before mentioning he's a Roman citizen, born to it, unlike the officer who paid for his.
ACT 23:1-35	Paul gets one sentence out before somebody punches him, calls the high priest a whitewashed wall, then walks it back on learning who he is. He plays the room: spots the council split between Sadducees and Pharisees, announces he's on trial over the resurrection, and the sides go for each other while soldiers haul him out. Forty men swear off food and water until Paul is dead, a vow they'll regret. The plot is blown up by a nephew nobody knew existed. The commander sends hundreds for one prisoner.
ACT 24:1-27	Tertullus burns two verses buttering up a governor history remembers as a thug, then gets to charges that are enormous and unsupported. Paul's reply is straight lawyering: twelve days, easily checked, nobody saw him arguing, and his accusers couldn't be bothered to show. Felix decides nothing. When the talk hits judgment he gets scared and calls it a night, the most honest reaction to a sermon in the book. He keeps calling Paul back for two years hoping for a bribe, then leaves him chained as a parting gift.
ACT 25:1-27	New governor, same request, same ambush waiting on the road. Festus keeps the trial in Caesarea, then asks the question that corners Paul: fancy being tried in Jerusalem after all? The appeal to Caesar is two words and cannot be taken back. Festus grants it, and every remaining chapter follows from that sentence. Then Festus briefs Agrippa, the most useful summary in the book because the man is baffled: it's some argument about their religion and a guy called Jesus who died and who Paul insists is not dead.
ACT 26:1-32	Third telling of the conversion and easily the slickest, because the audience is a king and Paul knows what he's doing. He compliments Agrippa's expertise and roots it in the hope of the twelve tribes, an internal family argument. Only this version has the bit about kicking against the goads, a Greek proverb for a Greek-speaking court. Festus interrupts yelling that Paul has studied himself insane. Agrippa's reply is notoriously hard to translate and reads like a polite no. He'd have walked, had he not appealed.
ACT 27:1-21	Luke's shipwreck is the most detailed sea story from the ancient world. Harbour's no good for wintering; the prisoner objects; the centurion goes with the captain instead, and the vote carries. A gentle south wind convinces everyone they were right. Then the storm gets a name, Euroclydon: hauling the dinghy aboard, wrapping cables under the hull, chucking gear overboard by hand. Fourteen days with no sun or stars means no navigation at all. Next time on Acts: I told you so, then something actually useful.
ACT 27:22-44	With the I-told-you-so out of his system, Paul becomes the calmest man on the boat. He catches sailors faking an anchor drop to steal the lifeboat and has soldiers cut it loose, stranding everyone together on purpose. Then he gets the starving crowd to eat breakfast: took bread, gave thanks, broke it. The wreck is technical and unsentimental. The last danger is the rulebook: soldiers who lose prisoners die in their place, so killing them looks sensible. The centurion overrules it to save one guy, and everyone makes it ashore.
ACT 28:1-31	The locals light a fire for soaked strangers, then watch a snake latch onto Paul's hand and revise their opinion of him twice in one paragraph, murderer to god. Malta gives him three months, some healings and supplies for the last leg. The run into Rome is quiet: believers hiking out to meet him. And the ending refuses to be one: no trial, no Caesar, no execution. A rented house, an open door, two years arguing with people who half agree, and the final word of the book: unhindered.
EXO 1:1-22	Seventy relatives moved to Egypt and now there's a whole nation of them. New king, never heard of Joseph, panics: what if they side with our enemies? So he works them to death building Pithom and Raamses, and they just keep multiplying. Plan B: order the midwives Shiphrah and Puah to kill the boys. They flat out don't, and tell Pharaoh the Hebrew women are too fast for them. God gives them families for it. Plan C is Pharaoh telling everybody to throw Hebrew baby boys in the Nile.
EXO 2:1-25	Levite mom hides her baby for three months, then builds him a tarred basket and puts him in the Nile, technically following orders. Pharaoh's daughter fishes him out, and his sister smoothly arranges for his actual mother to get paid to nurse him. He gets the name Moses. Grown up, he sees an Egyptian beating a Hebrew, checks nobody's looking, kills him, buries him. Word gets out anyway. He bolts to Midian, breaks up a bullying at a well, marries Zipporah, has a son called Gershom. Pharaoh dies. God hears.
EXO 3:1-22	Moses is tending Jethro's sheep at Horeb when a bush catches fire and refuses to burn up. He goes to look, which is apparently the interview. Shoes off, holy ground. God names himself the God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob and says Moses is the one handling Egypt. Moses asks who am I. God answers a different question. Pressed for a name, he gets I AM WHO I AM. Pharaoh will say no until things get ugly, and Israel will leave loaded with Egyptian silver and gold.
EXO 4:1-31	Moses keeps finding reasons not to go. Nobody will believe me gets him three party tricks: staff into snake, hand into leprosy, water into blood. I'm bad at talking gets him a question about who made mouths. Finally he says send someone else, and an angry God assigns Aaron as his mouthpiece. Moses heads back to Egypt. Then a weird night at a lodging place: God moves to kill him, and Zipporah grabs a flint and circumcises their son. Aaron shows up, the elders believe.
EXO 5:1-23	Moses and Aaron walk in and ask Pharaoh to let Israel go hold a feast in the wilderness. Pharaoh says he's never heard of Yahweh. Then he decides the real problem is these people have too much free time: same brick quota, no more straw, go find your own. The foremen get beaten when production drops, complain to Pharaoh, get called lazy again, and come out to find Moses standing right there. They tear into him. Moses turns around and tears into God. It's worse.
EXO 6:1-30	God's answer is a mic drop: Pharaoh won't just let them go, he'll throw them out. Then the name Yahweh, and a stack of I will promises about rescue and land, which the ancestors never got by that name. The people are too wrecked by their workload to listen. Moses falls back on his usual line about bad lips. Then everything stops for a family tree, quick through Reuben and Simeon, slow through Levi, because the point is showing exactly who Moses and Aaron are. Ends with Moses still objecting.
EXO 7:1-25	Moses is eighty, Aaron is eighty-three, and they're the team. Aaron chucks his staff down, it turns into a serpent, and Pharaoh's magicians go yeah we can do that, and do. Then Aaron's staff eats theirs. Pharaoh: not impressed. So the Nile goes to blood, fish die, whole river reeks, no drinkable water in Egypt. The magicians copy that too, which helps precisely nobody. Pharaoh walks back into his house like nothing happened. Everyone else digs holes by the river hoping for clean water.
EXO 8:1-32	Frogs. In the beds, in the ovens, in the dough. Magicians' contribution: more frogs, thanks guys. Pharaoh caves and Moses lets him pick when the frogs leave. He picks tomorrow. Why would you pick tomorrow. They die everywhere in stinking piles, Pharaoh feels better, and immediately goes back on the deal. Round three: dust becomes lice, and the magicians tap out and tell Pharaoh this is God's finger. Round four: flies everywhere except Goshen. Pharaoh starts haggling. Flies leave. Deal's off again.
EXO 9:1-18	Egypt's livestock drop dead and Israel's don't. Pharaoh actually sends someone to check, confirms not one Israelite animal died, and does nothing. Moses then flings furnace ash up and it turns to boils on everyone, including the magicians, who can't stand up in front of him anymore. God hardens Pharaoh's heart. Then a bigger speech: I could have wiped you out already, I kept you around so everybody hears about this. Also, tomorrow, hail like Egypt has never seen. Next time on Exodus: the sky falls in.
EXO 9:19-35	The warning comes with instructions: get your people and animals under a roof. Some of Pharaoh's officials listen. Some leave their workers standing in a field. Then hail, thunder, and lightning smash everything outdoors across Egypt, except Goshen. Pharaoh finally says it: I've sinned, Yahweh is right, we're wrong, make it stop. Moses stops it, then tells him flat out that he still doesn't actually fear God. Correct, obviously. Flax and barley are wrecked, wheat and spelt survive because they hadn't sprouted yet. Storm ends, Pharaoh doubles down.
EXO 10:1-29	Pharaoh's own staff stage an intervention: how long is this guy going to wreck us, Egypt is already destroyed. Pharaoh counteroffers men only, kids stay, then has Moses thrown out. East wind, locusts, everything green in Egypt gone. Pharaoh apologizes again, Moses prays, a west wind dumps the swarm in the Red Sea. Then three days of darkness so thick you can feel it, while Israel has lights on. Last offer: go, leave the animals. Moses says not one hoof. Pharaoh: see my face again and you die.
EXO 11:1-10	One plague left, and after it Pharaoh won't just let them go, he'll shove them out. Meanwhile: go ask your Egyptian neighbours for their silver and gold. They do, because Egypt likes them now and thinks Moses is a very big deal. Then the announcement, and it's not funny: around midnight every firstborn in Egypt dies, Pharaoh's heir, the servant girl's kid, the cattle, and there'll be a scream across the country like nothing before or since. Over in Israel, not even a dog barks. Moses walks out furious.
EXO 12:1-22	New calendar: this month is now month one. Grab a year-old male lamb, no defects, one per household, split with the neighbours if it's too much food. Kill it on the fourteenth at evening. Blood on both doorposts and the top beam. Roast it whole, eat it with flat bread and bitter herbs, burn the leftovers. And eat it with your belt on, shoes on, staff in hand, because you are leaving. Moses tells the elders: hyssop for the blood, nobody outside till morning. Next time on Exodus: midnight comes.
EXO 12:23-51	The blood on the door keeps the destroyer out, and Israel is told to explain the ritual to their kids forever. Then midnight, and every firstborn in Egypt dies, from Pharaoh's son to the prisoner in the dungeon. Not one house without a death, and the country screams. Pharaoh calls Moses in the dark: get out, and bless me too. The Egyptians shove them out so fast the bread never rises. Six hundred thousand men plus families walk out after four hundred and thirty years, to the day.
EXO 13:1-22	Every firstborn, human or animal, now belongs to God. Moses tells them: remember this day, seven days of flat bread, keep it like a mark on your hand and between your eyes. Firstborn donkey? Swap in a lamb, or break its neck. Firstborn son? Redeemed. Kid asks what this is about? You tell them the story. God skips the short coastal road because one glimpse of an army and these people would turn straight around. Moses packs Joseph's bones. Cloud by day, fire by night.
EXO 14:1-31	God has them camp with their backs to the sea so Pharaoh thinks they're cornered, and Pharaoh takes the bait: six hundred chariots. Israel sees the dust and immediately goes were there no graves in Egypt. Moses says stand still and watch. God says why are you yelling at me, move. The cloud parks between the two camps all night. An east wind splits the sea and Israel walks through on dry ground. Egypt drives in after them, the wheels fall off, the water comes back.
EXO 15:1-27	Victory song time. Horse and rider chucked in the sea, the water piled up by God basically snorting. The song quotes the enemy mid-swagger, I'll chase them, I'll catch them, I'll split the loot, right before the wind hits. Then it lists everyone about to be terrified: Philistia, Edom, Moab, Canaan. Miriam grabs a tambourine and the women dance it out. Three days later: no water. Then Marah, where it's bitter. Moses throws in a tree, it goes sweet. They camp at Elim, twelve springs, seventy palms.
EXO 16:1-16	Six weeks out and the camp is already nostalgic for Egypt, where apparently they sat around meat pots eating all the bread they wanted. Bold retelling of slavery. God says fine, bread from the sky, double on day six, and it's explicitly a test. Moses: you're not yelling at me, you're yelling at God. Evening: quail all over the camp. Morning: dew lifts and there's small flaky stuff on the ground. Everyone stares at it going what is that. An omer each. Next time on Exodus: somebody keeps leftovers.
EXO 16:17-36	Everyone gathers different amounts and somehow it measures out exactly right. Moses says don't keep any overnight. Some people keep it overnight. It fills with worms and reeks. Day six they gather double and Moses explains the Sabbath, and that batch keeps fine. Some go out looking on day seven anyway, find nothing, and God asks how long they plan to keep ignoring instructions. They name it manna, white like coriander seed, tastes like honey wafers. A jarful gets stored as a museum piece. Forty years of it.
EXO 17:1-16	Rephidim, no water, and the mob turns on Moses again. Moses to God: these people are about to stone me. God: take the elders, hit a rock. Water comes out. Moses names the spot Massah and Meribah, after the argument, because they'd just asked is God even with us. While drinking rock water. Then Amalek attacks. Joshua fights, Moses stands on a hill with the staff up, and Israel wins whenever his arms are up. His arms give out, so Aaron and Hur hold them up till sunset.
EXO 18:1-27	Jethro shows up with Zipporah and the two boys, who Moses had apparently sent home at some point. Moses tells him the whole Egypt story and Jethro is thrilled, blesses God, throws a sacrifice. Next morning he watches Moses settle every single argument in the nation from sunrise to sunset and goes: no. You'll burn out. Pick honest men as chiefs of thousands, hundreds, fifties and tens. Small stuff theirs, big stuff yours. Moses actually listens.
EXO 19:1-25	Three months out, Israel camps at Sinai. God's pitch: you saw what I did to Egypt, keep the covenant and you're a kingdom of priests. The people say yes before hearing any terms. Prep: wash up, wait three days, do not touch the mountain, and if someone does you shoot them from a distance rather than go get them. Day three: thunder, thick cloud, a loud trumpet, the mountain on fire. Moses climbs up, God sends him straight back down.
EXO 20:1-26	The big ten. No other gods, no idols, don't misuse the name, keep the Sabbath, respect your parents, no murder, no adultery, no stealing, no lying about your neighbour, and don't even want his house, his wife or his donkey. The mountain thunders and smokes the whole time, and the people back way off: you talk to us, we'll die if he does. Moses says that's kind of the idea. Then altar specs: dirt or uncut stone, no chisel marks, no stairs.
EXO 21:1-36	From cosmic thunder straight to the legal code. Hebrew servant: six years, then free. But if the master gave you a wife, she and the kids stay and you leave alone, which is exactly the trap that makes a guy say I'm staying, and then his ear gets punched through with an awl at the door. Death penalty for murder, for hitting or cursing your parents, for kidnapping. Eye for eye. Then goring bulls: repeat offender with a warned owner, and the owner dies too.
EXO 22:1-31	Property law hour. Steal an ox, pay back five. Steal a sheep, pay back four. Nobody explains why sheep are cheaper. Kill a burglar at night, fine. Kill the same burglar after sunrise, that's on you. Then a mountain of who-pays rules: your animal ate my crops, your fire spread, my stuff vanished at your house. Then a hard swerve into capital offences, and then the part that lands: don't mistreat foreigners, because you were foreigners. And give a man his coat back before dark.
EXO 23:1-33	Courtroom rules, and one is genuinely surprising: don't favour the poor man in his lawsuit either. Justice bent nicely is still bent. No rumours, no mob verdicts. If your enemy's ox wanders off, take it back to him, and if his donkey collapses, go help. Land sabbath every seventh year, so the poor and the wildlife eat off it. Also, out of nowhere, don't boil a baby goat in its mother's milk. The conquest goes slowly on purpose, because an empty country fills up with wild animals.
EXO 24:1-18	Moses, Aaron, Nadab, Abihu and seventy elders get summoned up, but only Moses gets close. Moses reads everything out and the people say we'll do all of it, which they say three separate times here. He builds an altar with twelve pillars, throws half the sacrificial blood on it, and flings the rest on the crowd. Signed and sealed, messily. Then seventy-four guys see the God of Israel, and all the text describes is the floor: sapphire pavement, clear as sky. Nobody dies. They eat.
EXO 25:1-40	Building fund, donations only: gold, silver, bronze, dyed yarn, linen, goat hair, ram skins, acacia, oil, spices, gemstones. Reason given: I want to live among you. The ark: acacia, gold inside and out, and the detail worth catching is that the carrying poles never come out of the rings. Ever. This is a God who stays packed. Gold lid, two hammered cherubim, and that's where God talks to Moses. Then the bread table and a lampstand beaten from one solid talent of gold.
EXO 26:1-18	Now the tent. Ten linen curtains, blue and purple and scarlet, cherubim woven in, joined in two sets of five with fifty gold clasps so it counts as one piece. Over that, eleven goat hair curtains, one more than the layer underneath, and the spare gets folded double over the front like an awning. Then two weatherproof layers: red ram skins and sea cow hides. Then the frame: acacia boards, twenty down the south side. Next time on Exodus: what all of that stands in.
EXO 26:19-37	Silver, and a lot of it: forty sockets under the twenty south boards, forty more on the north, sixteen at the back, two per board every time. Six boards close the west end plus two weird doubled-up corner boards, which only somebody who's actually built a corner writes down. Five gold-plated bars per side, and a middle bar running the entire length hidden inside the boards where nobody will ever see it. Then the veil on four gold pillars, separating holy from most holy.
EXO 27:1-21	The altar is bronze, not gold, five cubits square, hollow, with horns growing out of the corners as one piece. Full equipment list: pots, shovels, basins, meat forks, fire pans, all bronze, plus a grating halfway up and poles for carrying. Then the courtyard fence: a hundred cubits by fifty, linen on sixty pillars, silver hooks, bronze sockets, screen at the east gate. Even the tent pegs are bronze. And then the tiniest instruction here: bring beaten olive oil, because a lamp burns every night, permanently.
EXO 28:1-24	Aaron and his four sons, Nadab, Abihu, Eleazar and Ithamar, are the priests, and now they need outfits. The stated reason is literally glory and beauty, which nothing else in this legal code bothers to say. Breastplate, ephod, robe, tunic, turban, sash, all in gold, blue, purple, scarlet and linen. Two onyx stones on the shoulder straps, six tribe names each in birth order, so the high priest carries the whole nation on his shoulders. Next time on Exodus: how it stays put, and the bells.
EXO 28:25-43	Braided gold chains, rings and a blue cord, all so the breastplate doesn't flap around. Inside it go the Urim and Thummim, which the text names and then never explains, not once, ever. The robe's hem alternates gold bells and woven pomegranates the whole way around. Why? So you can hear him going in and coming out, and so he doesn't die. Gold plate on the forehead reading HOLY TO YAHWEH. And the final item is linen underwear, also on pain of death.
EXO 29:1-23	Ordination day, and it's messy. A young bull, two flawless rams, a basket of unleavened bread. Wash Aaron and his sons, dress them, dump anointing oil on Aaron's head. Then the bull: killed, blood finger-painted onto the altar horns, the rest hauled outside camp and burned. Ram one gets burned whole. Ram two is where it gets weird: blood on the tip of each man's right ear, his right thumb, his right big toe. Next time on Exodus: they eat.
EXO 29:24-46	The fat and the thigh and the bread go in the priests' hands, get waved in front of God, then burned. Moses gets the breast. From here on, the waved breast and thigh are the priesthood's permanent cut of everyone's peace offerings, a salary paid in meat. The ram gets boiled and eaten at the tent door by priests only, leftovers burned, not saved. Seven days of this, a bull a day. Then two lambs a day forever. And the point: I will live among them.
EXO 30:1-18	Second altar, small and gold, for incense only. Horns, poles, sits right in front of the veil. Aaron burns incense on it morning and evening whenever he does the lamps, and nothing else goes on it at all. Then, out of nowhere, census rules: counting people is apparently risky, so every man twenty and up pays half a shekel so there's no plague. Rich pay the same as poor. Then a bronze basin between tent and altar. Next time on Exodus: what happens if you skip it.
EXO 30:19-38	Turns out the basin is life or death: wash your hands and feet or die, stated twice. Then two recipes with exact weights. Anointing oil: myrrh, cinnamon, fragrant cane, cassia and olive oil, blended like a perfumer would. It anoints the tent, the furniture, both altars, the basin and the priests. Incense: gum resin, onycha, galbanum and pure frankincense, equal parts, salted. Copy either formula and you're cut off from your people, even if you just wanted to smell nice.
EXO 31:1-18	Six chapters of blueprints and finally someone has to build the thing. God names him personally: Bezalel of Judah, filled with the Spirit of God for gold, silver, bronze, gem cutting and woodwork. Not prophecy. Craftsmanship. Oholiab of Dan is his second, and every skilled worker gets topped up too. Then God reasserts the Sabbath, death penalty attached, right after handing out a massive building project, which is not subtle. Then the two stone tablets, written with God's own finger.
EXO 32:1-16	Moses has been up the mountain too long and the crowd loses patience: Aaron, make us gods. Aaron's big idea is collect everyone's gold earrings. He casts a calf, and instantly it's these are your gods, Israel. Aaron builds an altar in front of it and announces a feast to Yahweh, having it both ways. Next morning: sacrifices, food, drinking, partying. God offers to wipe them out and restart with Moses. Moses talks him down: they're your people, you swore to Abraham. Next time on Exodus: he sees.
EXO 32:17-35	Joshua hears the racket and calls it war. Moses says no, that's singing. Then he comes over the ridge, sees the calf, and hurls the tablets into the rocks. He burns the calf, grinds it to powder, dumps it in the water and makes them drink it. Then he turns on Aaron, who delivers an all-time excuse: I threw the gold in the fire and out came this calf. The Levites step up, and about three thousand die. Moses offers to be erased from God's book instead.
EXO 33:1-23	God's offer: you get the land, you get the angel out front, but I'm not coming, because you're stubborn and I'd end up destroying you on the way. The people go into mourning, taking off all their jewellery. Meanwhile Moses pitches a tent outside camp, and whenever he goes in, God talks to him face to face like a friend, while everyone watches from their doorways. Moses refuses the deal outright: if you don't come, don't send us. He wins, then asks to see the glory.
EXO 34:1-18	Take two. Moses cuts the stone this time, which stings a bit. Up at dawn, nobody else allowed, not even grazing animals. God comes down and announces his own name: merciful, gracious, slow to anger, full of loving kindness, forgives everything, and does not let the guilty off. Moses asks him to come along anyway, admitting these people are a nightmare. God renews the covenant: no treaties, no intermarriage, smash the altars, because his name is Jealous. Next time on Exodus: a glow nobody can look at.
EXO 34:19-35	The renewed terms, mostly repeats: firstborns belong to God, sons get redeemed, nobody shows up empty-handed. Rest on day seven even during ploughing and harvest. Three festivals a year, plus a promise nobody will eye up your land while the country's away. No yeast with sacrificial blood, no Passover leftovers, bring your firstfruits, and once again, do not boil a baby goat in its mother's milk. Forty days up there, no food, no water. He comes down glowing and doesn't notice, so he starts wearing a veil.
EXO 35:1-35	Before anyone touches a tool, Moses restates the Sabbath and adds a new rule: no lighting fires on the seventh day. Right before a project involving melting gold, that's not a coincidence. Then the fundraising pitch: gold, silver, bronze, dyed yarn, linen, goat hair, skins, acacia, oil, spices, gemstones. People show up in droves, handing over brooches, earrings, signet rings, armlets. The skilled women spin the yarn and goat hair. And Bezalel and Oholiab get one new credit: the ability to teach.
EXO 36:1-38	Best problem in the whole book: too many donations. Bezalel and Oholiab start work, and people keep showing up every morning with more stuff, until the craftsmen stop and tell Moses there's way more than enough. Moses sends a proclamation telling everyone to stop giving. It works. Then the build, and it's chapter twenty-six word for word in past tense: linen curtains with cherubim and gold clasps, goat hair curtains and bronze ones, acacia boards in silver sockets, gold bars, the veil, the door screen.
EXO 37:1-29	Bezalel personally builds the good stuff. Ark: acacia, gold inside and out, gold rings, gold-plated poles. Mercy seat: solid gold, two cherubim hammered from the same piece, wings up, faces turned toward each other. Table: gold-plated, rim, rings, poles, gold dishes and pitchers. Lampstand: one talent of pure gold beaten into a single piece, base, shaft, six branches, blossom cups, buds, flowers, no joins, seven lamps and snuffers. Then the gold incense altar. And finally the oil and the incense, mixed by the same guy.
EXO 38:1-31	Bronze work. The altar: five cubits square, three high, hollow, horns on the corners, mesh grating, poles, pots, shovels, basins, forks and fire pans. Then the detail that stops you: the washbasin is cast from the bronze mirrors donated by the women who served at the tent door. Then the courtyard fence, linen on sixty pillars, bronze sockets, silver hooks. Then a full audit: twenty-nine talents of gold, a hundred talents plus 1,775 shekels of silver, seventy talents of bronze, from 603,550 men paying half a shekel each.
EXO 39:1-21	Time to make the outfits. One detail the instructions skipped: they hammer the gold into sheets, cut it into wire, then weave it in with the blue, purple, scarlet and linen. That's why the ephod glitters. Shoulder straps joined at the ends, two onyx stones in gold with the tribe names. Then the breastplate, folded double, twelve gemstones in four rows, braided gold chains and rings, tied to the ephod with a blue cord so it can't come loose. Next time on Exodus: bells, delivery day.
EXO 39:22-43	The blue robe, woven whole, hem alternating pomegranates and gold bells. Linen tunics, turban, sash, the gold plate reading HOLY TO YAHWEH. Done. Then they carry the whole thing to Moses: tent, ark, table, lampstand, altars, basin, every piece down to the pegs and ropes. Third time this book has listed these objects, first time they actually exist. Moses checks it all over, confirms it's exactly right, and blesses them. After the golden calf, that's the point.
EXO 40:1-38	Setup day: first day, first month, second year. God gives the order and Moses does every step himself. Frame up, ark in, veil hung, table set, lamps lit, incense altar burning, bronze altar out front, basin filled, courtyard raised. Anoint everything. Wash, dress and anoint Aaron and his sons.
